Nexans · NEX-FR · the new Memo conveyor's card of 25 October 2026

What the Investment Analyst wrote behind each of the eleven ratings: Nexans

The Investment Analyst's answers to the 101 questions and its 13 group judgements, 58,800 words, set out under the eleven ratings: each rating, then each of its lettered sub-items, then the answers beneath.

Word for word. Every answer is the Investment Analyst's text exactly as written; a script checked that every word is on this page, in order. What is changed is layout only: answers are grouped by rating rather than by layer; each answer is titled with the question as it was set (the Investment Analyst's own heading appears beneath only where it adds words); an answer that belongs under more than one sub-item is shown in full once, at its first place, and as a one-line link everywhere else.

Which answer sits where comes from the same table the ratings map uses. Each answer carries its layer and group, because "positive" means something different in each layer. Each layer's own material (what it was asked, what its scores mean, the Investment Analyst's notes and appendices) is at the end, so nothing is left out.

The ratings alone: the ratings map.

How to read the scores and letters

The Investment Analyst ended each answer with a score from 1 to 100: 100 is the most positive for an investment case, 1 the most negative, and 50 neither. What "positive" refers to differs by layer, so each layer states its own meaning (listed just below).

Where the Rater has scored this stock (every memo from 3 November 2026), the number in each pill is the Rater's, set after the memo was written from the Investment Analyst's judgements against a key per group of questions; the Investment Analyst's own score is kept in the pill's tooltip. The letter beside each score is read from the one canonical source for ratings, the same file the eleven ratings page and the memo scorecards read; nothing is graded on this page. The letter follows the item's rule: for most items this stock's rank among the stocks scored on the same scale on that same question or group judgement, re-cut each night (Richard's ruling of 4-Nov-26: every letter by rank; in the file's words: A above 90%; B+ above 82.5% to 90%; B above 75% to 82.5%; C+ above 66.67% to 75%; C above 58.33% to 66.67%; C- above 50% to 58.33%; D above 25% to 50%; F up to 25%); on the External change forces and Internal change forces items the number carries a sign (50 = neither positive nor negative) and the letter is the rank, so read the two together. “37th of 56” is the stock's place among them; a dashed outline marks a provisional grade (fewer than 30 stocks scored).

What the scores mean in each layer

Layer 1

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

Layer 2

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the likely effect on the company's financial forecasts is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

Layer 3 and Layer 4

Finish each answer and each judgement on a group or set with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For the third set's questions, the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for the predictability of the company's financial forecasts over the next 18 months, and so for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

For the fourth set's questions, the score says how favourable for an investment case in the company the gap is between our assessment and what current guidance, sell-side estimates and the stock market narrative already reflect: 100 the most favourable, 1 the least, 50 neither.

Inputs

1

External headwinds/tailwinds

B+725th of 36

Memo topic 1, Tailwinds in the operating environment: the Rater's score

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

External change forces

Layer 1 · External change forces

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group. The rating above stands for it, so it carries no number of its own.

The external forces are strong, structural and well evidenced at the sector level, and they are now reaching Nexans most powerfully through Grid, the engine the market and the company's own guideline both under-model. The high-voltage tailwind remains, but for Nexans it has moved from a volume story to a conversion and order-replacement story at a time when competition for post-2029 slots is widening and transmission operators have started to trade speed for cost; this is the main external source of fragility in the case, and it lands on the one asset (the mass-impregnated line at Halden) where Nexans is most concentrated. European construction and non-metal input inflation are real but bounded headwinds to Connect. The macro regime is a headwind to the multiple far more than to earnings. For the model: high single-digit Grid growth with capacity steps in 2027 and 2028; Transmission revenue flat to slightly down in FY2026 and recovering in 2027 to 2028 only on signed orders, with margin rising on vintage and Electra; Connect Europe flat, North America growing; and a non-metal input-cost headwind in 2027.

1a

External headwinds/threats

6 answers
C-0238C-0544C-0736C-0948C-3538C-3958
C-02

To what extent, and in which direction, is competitive intensity changing?

F3826th of 35

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement. Competitive intensity is rising, modestly in the near term and materially for contracts that deliver after 2029. The high-voltage oligopoly still behaves with discipline, but the qualified supplier set is widening for the first time in a decade, and Nexans is the incumbent most exposed to that widening because it already lost share and its backlog is the thinnest of the three European leaders. In low voltage, intensity is high and unchanged in Europe and Nexans has chosen to enter the most consolidated low-voltage market in the world, the United States, as a 4 percent player.

  • High-voltage subsea and land (Transmission). In the last twelve months the first entrant won European 525 kV subsea awards (LS Cable & System on TenneT's BalWin4 and LanWin1), Hellenic Cables completed 525 kV high-voltage direct-current qualification (July 2026) and won Greek awards worth €1.5bn (Corinth-Kos) and €1.15bn (the island framework), Sumitomo Electric opened its Nigg plant in Scotland (September 2026) and LS Cable is building a €220m cable-laying vessel for H1 2028. Morgan Stanley (11 October 2026) reports Chinese suppliers targeting European subsea tenders. A Prysmian transmission strategy director estimates Nexans lost 3 to 4 percentage points of European high-voltage subsea share to NKT, Asian suppliers and others. Incumbent capacity is also rising: NKT's Karlskrona expansion enters commercial operation in Q2 2027, Prysmian is adding towers and three vessels by 2027.
  • The behaviour, so far, has not changed. Prysmian refuses to build without customer down-payments ("the party is over"), NKT says it would "not be disappointed" to lose share on the wrong projects, and all three European leaders printed rising Transmission margins in H1 2026. Intensity is rising in the number of qualified bidders, not yet in price.
  • Low voltage. European construction-linked low voltage is at a cyclical trough with competitors "seeing pricing pressure" (Jefferies). In the United States, Prysmian (Encore Wire, then Atkore at about €3.3bn enterprise value) and Southwire (more than $2bn of modernisation) hold about 60 to 65 percent of building wire; Prysmian and Southwire each hold about 30 to 35 percent of US data-centre cable. Nexans enters through Republic Wire.
  • Medium voltage and accessories (Grid). Every major producer is adding medium-voltage capacity under larger, longer frameworks; this protects Nexans's position rather than creating a monopoly.

Direction and skew. Over the next six months intensity is broadly stable (backlogs are full); over 18 to 36 months the skew is towards more intensity in high-voltage awards for post-2029 delivery and in US low voltage. For the model, this argues against extrapolating new-award pricing for Transmission beyond the 2024 to 2026 vintage and for treating Republic Wire's margin as at risk rather than as a base to grow from.

C-05

How are input costs, input availability and supplier concentration changing, and is the net effect a headwind or a tailwind?

C4413th of 35

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement. Metals are a revenue and working-capital effect, not a profit effect, and their scarcity structurally favours Nexans's integrated model. The near-term net effect is nevertheless a mild headwind, because the inputs that are not indexed (polymers, oil-derived compounds, energy and labour) are the ones that have inflated in 2026, and they bite hardest in Connect and Grid, where pricing power is weakest.

  • Copper and aluminium (about 70 percent of cable production cost). Copper reached a record in September 2026 (about $13,000 per tonne, up about 30 percent year on year). Metal is passed through on 83 to 85 percent of the business with no lag on major contracts, and about 80 percent of direct costs are passed through or hedged, so the profit effect is small. The effects that do reach the Consumer's model are: revenue at current metal prices inflates (a "false friend" for anyone reading the top line); working capital rises (copper-related supplier payables under negotiated arrangements rose to €449m at end-2025 from €341m); and the Core exposure revaluation (€75.2m in H1 2026) inflates reported but not adjusted profit and will reverse if copper falls.
  • Availability favours the integrated maker. Nexans warns of a 10 to 20 percent copper supply gap by 2030; four rod mills cover about half its copper needs; KGHM supplies up to 28,000 tonnes a year to 2027; the Lens plant adds up to 80,000 tonnes of recycled-copper processing from 2027; a five-year Hydro agreement (July 2026) secures about 85,000 tonnes of low-carbon aluminium rod. NKT and Cenergy own no rod mills.
  • Polymers and compounds (not indexed). Brent rose to about $111 after the Strait of Hormuz disruption; polymer and feedstock shortages and bunker fuel costs (up about 100 percent year to date, Bernstein) are not covered by escalation clauses. The precedent is 2010, when delayed polymer pass-through cost Nexans about €70m of EBITDA. Nexans depends on a single source, Borealis, for cross-linked polyethylene compounds.
  • Energy. September 2026 European electricity prices were up about 97 percent and gas up 133 percent year on year (Rothschild & Co Redburn); Nexans secures energy at least 18 months ahead, so the cost arrives with a lag, mostly in 2027.
  • Labour and installation. Expert procurement evidence shows supplier margins 2 to 5 points below expectations from installation costs and wage premia; Nexans's appointment of a Chief Operating Officer (Stéphane Artaud, from 1 November 2026) to fix industrial operations and the supply chain suggests management sees these as live.

Net. A headwind of perhaps €20m to €50m of EBITDA over late 2026 and 2027 if oil and energy stay high, concentrated in Connect and Grid and partly recovered through price with a lag; a structural tailwind beyond 2027 from integration and recycling. The Consumer should hold Connect margins flat rather than recovering in 2027 and carry a polymer-and-energy sensitivity.

C-07

To what extent is a macro force acting on the company, on its sector and on its industry, and at which level does it bite hardest?

D3625th of 35

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement. The macro force that matters most today is the rates-and-energy regime (US ten-year yield near 5.1 percent with a Federal Reserve hike priced for December, Brent at about $111, European Central Bank tightening expected, French sovereign stress spreading to Italy). It bites hardest at the sector level, through the multiple, and at the company level through Connect's European construction exposure; the industry's regulated grid demand is largely insulated.

  • Sector level (the multiple). The house regime reading is risk-off, with defensives leading cyclicals on all five windows, breadth in the bottom fifth of three years and the Momentum tilt moved to Underweight. Cable makers fell 4.8 percent over the three months to 13 October and have fallen further since; the sector digest notes that the market marks a cable maker down when capacity is announced, years before prices fall. Nexans's price is down 24 percent over six months with relative strength at the 18th percentile. Higher discount rates compress multiples on long-duration grid stories regardless of earnings.
  • Company level (Connect). Higher mortgage rates and weak consumer confidence keep European residential construction contracting; Nexans has historically named its building-related activity the part of Electrification most exposed to recession. European industrial production is also contracting (euro-area -0.5 percent month on month in August).
  • Industry level (grid demand). Regulated utilities recover capital through formula rates and allowed returns, and higher rates have so far been passed into utility-scale power-purchase prices rather than destroying demand (Jefferies). The second-order risk is transmission operators' funding: higher rates and grid-cost politics are what sit behind TenneT's and Elia's re-phasing.
  • Currency. The krone is 26.6 percent of standard sales; translation is unhedged by policy; a weaker krone helps the income statement and hurts equity (see reading note 1). Canadian dollar, Turkish lira and US dollar weakness took €166m off 2025 standard sales and €23m off EBITDA, and €30m off Q1 2026 sales.
  • Energy inflation reaches margins with an 18-month hedging lag (see C-05).
C-09

What other material external dynamic, positive or negative, is at work?

–48

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement. Four further dynamics matter, two of them working on perception rather than earnings.

  • Copper as a false friend (negative for reading the numbers). Record copper inflates reported revenue, reported operating income (through Core exposure) and working capital without changing adjusted profit; when copper falls, the reported lines will look worse at the moment the adjusted lines improve. The Risks report's winning analysis-of-competing-hypotheses profile is precisely this masking effect.
  • The cable group's valuation regime (negative now). Peers' multiples sat at high percentiles of their own histories (Prysmian 83rd, Cenergy 92nd on 12 October; NKT 21st, Nexans 49th then), and the group explains a large share of each member's daily moves (about 45 percent for NKT). A sector de-rating drags Nexans whatever its own news; Nexans is now at the 5th percentile of its five-year range, so the drag is partly spent.
  • The Great Sea Interconnector's geopolitics (positive, improving). Meridiam's entry and resumed seabed surveys make the €1.2bn project an upside option that guidance excludes; Nexans's 4 October statement that it coordinates only with the promoter and the Greek and Cypriot authorities keeps the Turkish dimension live.
  • Labour scarcity and capacity ramps across the chain (negative, slow). Skilled-labour shortages are the stated primary constraint for Siemens Energy and Quanta, and wage premia are reported by installation contractors; for Nexans this coincides with several simultaneous ramps (Grid capacity, accessories, Charleroi, Morocco, Republic Wire's 30 percent expansion).
C-35

To what extent are new entrants (from adjacent markets; standard entrants; orthogonal or innovative entrants) entering or threatening this market, and how much of a threat is each type?

F3830th of 35

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. Entry is real for the first time in a decade, and the threat is medium and rising, concentrated on awards for delivery after 2029 and on the Mediterranean, the region that matters most for Nexans's mass-impregnated line.

  • Adjacent entrants (Asian majors moving into Western subsea): medium and rising. LS Cable (TenneT 525 kV awards, UK plant 2027, US plant 2028, vessel H1 2028; also its first strike in 37 years in September 2026), Sumitomo (Nigg operating; Südkabel; the SSEN Shetland framework), Taihan (a 640 kV plant and the 10,000-tonne vessel Skandi Connector). Expert: developers are "actively qualifying alternative Asian manufacturers".
  • Standard entrants (regional specialists stepping up): medium, and highest for Nexans. Hellenic Cables qualified at 525 kV in July 2026; the Greek operator IPTO, Nexans's Great Sea Interconnector customer, awarded Hellenic the €1.5bn Corinth-Kos link and the €1.15bn island framework, showing a preference for local supply. Without vessels and with a balance sheet that limits guarantees, Hellenic is a second-tier threat in the north and a first-tier threat in the eastern Mediterranean.
  • Chinese suppliers: low now, a tail risk. Excluded by security rules and the Net-Zero Industry Act; Morgan Stanley (October 2026) reports them targeting European subsea tenders; relief valves in the Act (a price premium above 10 percent) could open the door if cost pressure grows.
  • Orthogonal or innovative entrants: none material. Superconducting cables are Nexans's own initiative.
C-39

Where relevant or material, how are suppliers behaving: a support to the company, or a source of risk?

C+5810th of 35

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. Suppliers are mainly a support, because Nexans has internalised or contracted the critical ones; the risk sits in a few concentrated, non-indexed inputs.

  • Support: copper secured through four rod mills (about half of needs), KGHM, multi-source procurement and a seven-year Tier 1 miner agreement (Bernstein); aluminium through the Hydro agreement; recycled metal through Lens, RecyCâbles and the RTE partnership; installation through owned vessels (Aurora, Electra and a third); supplier finance extends payables (copper-related payables on 30 to 120 days; €449m under negotiated arrangements at end-2025).
  • Risk: a single source (Borealis) for cross-linked polyethylene compounds; polymers and compounds lacking escalation clauses; energy secured 18 months ahead, so the 2026 price spike reaches 2027 costs; supplier finance is a working-capital support that can reverse; no supplier-level concentration data is disclosed.
1b

External tailwinds

7 answers
C-0170C-0342C-0468C-0872C-3264C-3766C-4366
C-01

To what extent is end-market demand changing, structurally or cyclically, and with how much visibility? (A cyclical swing counts when it has two-plus years of visibility.)

B707th of 35

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement. End-market demand is changing structurally upward at the sector level, but it reaches Nexans through three engines that are now moving in different directions, and the visibility of each is very different. Grid (distribution utilities) is the engine that has changed most in the last six months and is the most under-appreciated: structural, contracted and accelerating. Transmission (high-voltage subsea and land systems) remains structurally underpinned for the decade, but its order flow has entered a slower, more price-sensitive phase at exactly the moment Nexans's own backlog is thin relative to peers. Connect (low-voltage building, industrial and data-centre cables) is cyclically weak in Europe and structurally strong only in North American data centres.

Grid: structural, with two-plus years of visibility, and accelerating.

  • Organic growth went from +4.9 percent in H1 2026 (Q2 +4.2 percent, capped by capacity) to +14.8 percent in Q3 2026 against consensus of 6.1 to 7.0 percent, with breadth across European utilities, North American utility and data-centre frameworks, solar projects in South America, Turkey and Morocco, and accessories.
  • About two-thirds of Grid revenue sits under multi-year frameworks; Enedis signed a seven-year €600m framework in February 2026 that doubles annual volumes; utilities now sign six-to-seven-year frames against two-year frames historically.
  • The cyclical qualifier is seasonality (Q2 and Q3 are strong quarters in Europe), so 14.8 percent is not a run-rate; the structural evidence is that management is adding about 40 percent European Grid capacity by 2028 and 39 percent accessories capacity (€62m) from 2026 to 2028 because demand outruns supply.

Transmission: structural demand, but a softer order phase.

  • The industry evidence is strong: European grid investment of €530bn to €540bn from 2025 to 2031 under the EU Grids Package, TenneT's €65bn to 2029, high-voltage direct-current subsea capacity booked to the end of the decade, and contract prices per kilometre roughly doubled since 2019 to 2020.
  • But three demand signals turned less favourable in the last six months, and they matter more for Nexans than for peers: transmission system operators have begun to push back on cost (TenneT re-phasing 7.7 GW of German offshore wind connections by up to a year, Elia postponing on "unprecedented price increases", Energinet pausing connections); Germany has moved unawarded direct-current corridors back towards overhead lines, removing an estimated 4,000-plus kilometres of underground high-voltage direct-current cable from the 2027 to 2032 pipeline (contracted backlogs are protected); and Prysmian's own commentary refers to an "air pocket" in high-voltage direct-current awards.
  • Nexans's Transmission organic growth went from +33 to +40 percent in Q3 and Q4 2025 to -0.1 percent in H1 2026 and -4.0 percent in Q3 2026; the Q4 2026 comparison is +40 percent. The backlog of €7.8bn equals about 4.5 years of FY2025 Transmission sales (€1,691m) and carries visibility through 2028; Nexans needs new awards sooner than NKT (€13.0bn backlog, about 8 years of its Transmission sales) or Prysmian (about €18bn to €20bn).

Connect: cyclical weakness in Europe, structural pull in North America.

  • European construction contracted for a 53rd consecutive month in September 2026 (Jefferies); Rothschild & Co Redburn does not expect a European residential recovery before 2028; Nordic residential demand is "deeply subdued with no near-term signs of recovery".
  • Against that, Connect organic growth was +12 percent in Q2 and +10.5 percent in Q3 2026, led by North American hyperscale data centres, South American solar and Middle East and African demand. US construction spending is down 1.7 percent year on year, with data-centre-driven office spending up about 30 percent: the US strength is narrow.

What it means for the model. Model Grid at high single-digit organic growth through 2028 with a capacity ceiling (not mid-single digit, as the company's medium-term guideline says); Transmission revenue flat to slightly down in FY2026 and returning to growth in 2027 only if the replacement orders and new 525 kV projects load the plants; Connect Europe flat in volume to 2027 with North America carrying growth. The direction of the next six months is positive for Grid and Connect North America and negative for Transmission revenue (Q4 comparison), with the order intake for post-2028 subsea deliveries the key unknown.

C-03

To what extent is a disruption force strengthening or being disproved, and in which direction does it act on the company?

F4228th of 35

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement. No disruption force moves Nexans's numbers inside three years, but two forces strengthened in the last six months and both act against the company more than against its peers: the policy substitution of overhead lines for underground cable in Germany, and the long-run displacement of mass-impregnated cable technology, which is the technology of Nexans's distinctive Halden line. A third force, the shift of data centres to 800-volt direct current and superconducting distribution, is a genuine two-way force in which Nexans holds an early position.

  • Overhead lines replacing underground direct-current cable (strengthened, negative). Germany's mid-2026 legislative pivot removes the statutory priority for underground cabling on new direct-current corridors; contracted backlogs are protected, but over 4,000 kilometres of potential underground high-voltage direct-current orders for 2027 to 2032 are lost to the industry. Nexans holds an Amprion framework and TenneT frameworks; the exposure is to future orders, not to the backlog.
  • Extruded cable replacing mass-impregnated cable (slowly strengthening, negative for Nexans specifically). A Prysmian project director expects extruded cross-linked polyethylene cable to replace mass-impregnated technology over five to ten years. Nexans's 2026 guidance depends on loading its mass-impregnated line with a replacement order that is still unsigned; Mallorca-Menorca, the award announced on 22 October, uses 132 kV alternating-current extruded cable, not mass-impregnated cable. Nexans does have extruded 525 kV capability (LanWin2 entered industrial production in October 2026; Charleroi adds 525 kV land capacity from mid-2027), so the risk is to the utilisation and pricing of one line, not to the franchise.
  • Reconductoring and grid-enhancing technologies (stable, mildly negative). High-temperature low-sag conductors and grid-enhancing technologies raise capacity without new lines; Nexans sells conductors and monitoring, so this is partly offsetting.
  • Data-centre architecture (strengthening, two-way). 800-volt direct-current retrofits drove 10 to 20 percent procurement growth in Q3 2026 (expert) and reduce conventional copper per megawatt; at the same time superconducting cable is priced at five to ten times conventional cable and Nexans's European data-centre quoting pipeline rose from 50 megawatts to about 1 gigawatt in six months (Bernstein, Deutsche Bank). Nexans lacks optical fibre, so Prysmian's combined energy-and-data offer is the stronger bundle.
  • Being disproved: Chinese entry into Western subsea has so far been held back by security rules and the Net-Zero Industry Act's non-price criteria; solid-state transformers are judged not yet commercially viable (Siemens Energy specialist).

Net. The direction of travel is mildly negative for Nexans's Transmission order book after 2028 and mildly positive for its data-centre optionality. Neither belongs in the base-case numbers before 2028; both belong in the terminal-margin and multiple debate.

C-04

To what extent is political or regulatory change under way, and how does it bear on the company?

B+685th of 35

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement. Political and regulatory change is extensive and, on balance, positive: it funds demand, raises barriers against low-cost entrants and rewards local production. But the next leg of policy is about affordability, and the first instances of transmission operators and governments trading speed for cost appeared in the last six months. For Nexans the balance is positive in Grid and US low voltage, positive but weakening in Transmission, and negative in a few specific places (US tariffs on Canadian rod, Germany's overhead-line pivot, the antitrust legacy).

Supportive changes:

  • Funding: the EU Grids Package (€530bn to €540bn, 2025 to 2031), the EU Electrification Action Plan (46 percent electricity share by 2040), Germany's offshore wind law moving to two-sided contracts for difference with 70 GW targeted by 2045, the UK's 8.4 GW contracts-for-difference round, the UK RIIO-T3 framework (9 to 10 percent nominal return on equity), US utility capital expenditure of $1.45tn for 2026 to 2030 (Jefferies), and the Cyprus regulator's 3.7 percent weighted average cost of capital premium for the Great Sea Interconnector for 17 years.
  • Barriers: the Net-Zero Industry Act weights non-price resilience and sustainability criteria at 15 to 30 percent in clean-energy tenders and filters bids when a single third country supplies more than 65 percent of EU demand; UK and Dutch operators increasingly require local content; US Section 232 places 50 percent tariffs on imported copper cable and connectors, and Executive Order 14420 (August 2026) targets foreign bulk-power equipment at 69 kV and above. These help Nexans's European plants, Charleston and Republic Wire's Ohio and Cincinnati footprint.
  • Procurement: Enedis selected Nexans on security of supply, carbon and circularity as well as price; low-carbon credentials (Hydro aluminium below 4.0 kilogrammes of carbon dioxide per kilogramme) win tenders.

Adverse or two-sided changes:

  • US tariffs on copper in semi-finished products have significantly reduced production at Nexans's Montreal rod mill and stop Republic Wire using Canadian rod.
  • Germany's overhead-line pivot (see C-03); permitting and grid-connection queues (about 375 GW in Europe; connection waits of up to ten years in Denmark).
  • Affordability politics: the US House passed the Ratepayer Protection Act (September 2026, 417 to 3); regulators scrutinise grid costs; the Net-Zero Industry Act lets authorities waive resilience criteria where they add a price premium above 10 percent.
  • Antitrust: a €75m contingency provision at end-2025 for investigations and follow-on claims, the UK Spottiswoode class action, Italian and French proceedings (€3m French settlement, April 2026).
  • Geopolitics: the Great Sea Interconnector was delayed by Turkish-Cypriot maritime disputes and a regulatory review; Meridiam's 66 percent stake (5 August 2026) and the tripartite seabed survey agreement improve the odds of resumption, but 2026 guidance assumes no execution.
  • France: Nexans is French-listed; French sovereign spreads are at decade highs and the house's country tilt is a Double Underweight. Nexans's French revenue is a minority of sales (core Eurozone, including France, Germany and Italy, was 22.4 percent of standard sales in 2025), so the exposure is through the risk premium, not earnings.
C-08

To what extent is a key driver at the industry or sector level accelerating the company's demand?

C+729th of 35

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement. Yes, materially, and it is visible in the numbers now: the grid-modernisation and data-centre electrification cycle is accelerating Nexans's Grid and North American Connect demand faster than the company's own medium-term framework assumes. It is not accelerating Transmission revenue in the near term, because that engine is set by plant loading rather than by demand.

  • Distribution grids. European utilities' network investment is running about 10 to 12 percent above last year with order volumes about 15 percent higher (expert); US utility capital expenditure is set to rise about 30 percent over 2026 to 2030 with about 49 percent directed to transmission and distribution (Ambit); regulated US electric utility budgets grew 22 percent year on year in Q2 2026 (Bernstein). The direct read-through is Grid's +14.8 percent Q3 organic growth and the decision to add 39 percent accessories capacity.
  • Data centres. Data-centre order intake for power equipment is expected at 40 to 50 GW for 2026 (Morgan Stanley); Nexans links Q3 Grid and Connect growth to North American hyperscale data centres and reports a European quoting pipeline at gigawatt scale. The driver is powerful but concentrated, and the house's environment note flags that the artificial-intelligence capital-expenditure theme is now cracking at its financing (credit spreads of neoclouds and hyperscalers wider), not at its demand.
  • High-voltage transmission. Approved US transmission programmes of about $80bn into the early 2030s and European interconnection targets support the backlog, but the near-term effect on Nexans is on margin and order quality, not on volume.

For the model. The acceleration justifies Grid growth above the company's mid-single-digit guideline through 2028 (high single digit, capacity-limited) and a data-centre-led North American Connect line, with the concentration risk carried as a downside scenario rather than in the base case.

C-32

How is customer demand behaving: to what extent does it support the thesis, and how fragile is that support?

C+6411th of 35

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. Customer demand supports the thesis strongly in Grid and data centres, solidly in Transmission's backlog, and not at all in European construction. The new fragility is behavioural: the largest customers, transmission operators, have begun to optimise for cost over speed, and that loop reaches suppliers' down-payment terms and award timing before it reaches price.

  • Distribution utilities: committing earlier, signing longer frames, paying for certainty; Enedis doubled annual volumes; a utility manager reported Nexans-related activity up more than 15 percent.
  • Transmission operators: still committing (TenneT frames; RTE more than €1bn; National Grid's more than £70bn five-year plan), but phasing on cost (TenneT's five German offshore wind farms, 7.7 GW, delayed up to a year; Elia's three-year postponement; Energinet's connection pause of up to ten years); a five-operator procurement memorandum is coordinating buying.
  • Developers: offshore wind economics stretched by rates and costs; US cancellations; some European auctions drew zero bids, offset by German contract reform and 37 GW of auctions in the next twelve months.
  • Data centres: demand above capacity (Allianz: 206 units of demand against 183 of capacity in 2028); grid connections of four to six years above 100 megawatts; financing of neoclouds is the current stress point.
  • Construction customers (through distributors): weak in Europe, especially the Nordics; mixed in the United States.
C-37

What are regulators and political actors doing, does it help or hurt the company, and how fragile is that position?

B+666th of 35

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. Regulators and politicians are, on balance, helping: they fund the demand, protect local manufacturing and keep low-cost Chinese supply out of critical grids. The position is fragile in three specific ways: affordability politics that slow awards, permitting that slows conversion, and the possibility that cost pressure leads governments to relax local preferences.

  • Helping: the EU Grids Package and Electrification Action Plan; national offshore programmes (Germany 70 GW by 2045; France 45 GW by 2050 underpinning Nexans's RTE frameworks); the Net-Zero Industry Act's resilience criteria and the proposed EU local-content framework; US Buy America, Section 232 and Executive Order 14420; regulated returns (Italy 5.5 percent; Greece 6.87 percent pre-tax; Cyprus's premium for the Great Sea Interconnector).
  • Hurting: US copper tariffs reducing Montreal rod-mill output and blocking Canadian rod for Republic Wire; Germany's overhead-line pivot; affordability measures (the US Ratepayer Protection Act; regulators' scrutiny of grid costs); antitrust (the 2014 €70.6m EU cartel fine, the UK class action, a €75m provision); export controls and sanctions screening across 39 countries.
  • Fragility: the Net-Zero Industry Act's resilience rules lapse if they add more than a 10 percent price premium; a severe grid delay could lead regulators to suspend local preferences; French political risk into the 2027 presidential election affects the risk premium on a French-listed stock.
C-43

How large is the realistic total addressable market relative to the company, and what rate of growth does it allow for over the mid term?

D6618th of 35

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement. The addressable market is large relative to Nexans and growing at mid to high single digits, so the market itself does not cap growth; Nexans's chosen strategy (selectivity) and its capacity do. The market allows 6 to 8 percent annual revenue growth for a capacity-matched participant; Nexans targets 3 to 5 percent organic growth in Electrification and is currently delivering about 5 percent (nine months of 2026), with Grid running well above.

  • Global cable market: about €116bn in 2023 rising to about €157bn by 2028 (about 6 percent a year); power cable about $196bn in 2025 rising to about $297bn by 2034. Nexans holds roughly 4 to 5 percent.
  • High voltage: an addressable market above €10bn a year from 2024 to 2030, with cumulative excess demand of about €13bn through 2028 (NKT research); awards exceeded €15bn in the first nine months of 2024 against €2bn to €3bn a year in the late 2010s.
  • Distribution grids: Europe's medium-voltage grids need about €425bn of modernisation; European utilities' average annual capex up about 60 percent between 2020 and 2030; US utility capex $1.45tn for 2026 to 2030, about half on transmission and distribution.
  • Data centres: data-centre electricity use forecast to rise about 165 percent by 2030; US data-centre power consumption growing about 30 percent a year to 2028.

For the model. Revenue growth is a supply-side and strategy variable for Nexans, not a demand variable: Grid can grow as fast as capacity lands (about 40 percent more European capacity by 2028), Transmission as fast as orders fill existing plants, Connect with construction and US data centres.

Overall external picture

C-06

Where is the company's own revenue and demand cycle, and to what extent is it turning?

D5819th of 35

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement. The group's revenue cycle troughed in H1 2026 and is turning up, but the earnings-per-share cycle troughs in FY2026 as a whole and turns in 2027, and the turn is uneven by engine. Grid is in an accelerating upcycle, Connect is past its organic low but structurally split, and Transmission is in the middle of a planned normalisation whose length depends on replacement orders that are not yet signed. The single most important dating point for the model is the Transmission trough: H2 2026 into H1 2027.

  • Group. Organic growth of standard sales was +5.1 percent in 2024, +8.3 percent in 2025, +1.5 percent in H1 2026, +9.0 percent in Q3 2026 and +3.9 percent for the nine months; Electrification (excluding the shrinking metallurgy line) was +4.5 percent in H1, +7.1 percent in Q3 and +5.3 percent for the nine months, inside the company's 3 to 5 percent 2024 to 2028 framework. The Q3 headline was flattered by Other Activities (+24.7 percent against a weak, tariff-distorted comparison) and seasonality. Q4 2026 will be weaker because Transmission faces a +40 percent comparison.
  • Transmission. 2024 and 2025 were peak installation years; 2026 is normalisation (Q2 -6.2 percent, Q3 -4.0 percent, Q4 likely worse). The re-acceleration in 2027 rests on four things: the replacement Mass Impregnated order (guidance assumes the line is loaded at the end of 2026); 525 kV projects under the TenneT framework (LanWin2 production started in October 2026, BalWin3 to follow); Mallorca-Menorca (six-month engineering phase, delivery from mid-2027); and Charleroi land capacity from mid-2027. Electra is booked to end-2028. The cycle therefore turns in 2027 with high probability on volume already in hand, and the margin turn is already visible (H1 2026 margin 13.7 percent, +195 basis points on flat sales).
  • Grid. An upcycle constrained by capacity, which arrives from late 2026; the turn has happened.
  • Connect. Europe at the bottom of a construction cycle with no recovery before 2027 to 2028; North America growing on data centres and on Republic Wire scope (24.7 percent scope contribution in Q3, about 3 points below the 28 percent consensus expected).
  • Earnings. FY2026 earnings per share fall on perimeter, depreciation, acquisition interest and tax even as EBITDA rises; the FY2027 rebound (consensus +30 percent on the brokers' basis) is the turn the market will pay for.
C-10

Taking each sector and industry thematic driver in turn, and then all of the above together: how strong are the external headwinds and how strong are the tailwinds at value-chain, industry and sector level, and what is the overall external change picture?

B648th of 35

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement, thematic driver by thematic driver.

  1. Grid modernisation and electrification (sector tailwind, strong, accelerating). The strongest and most durable force; law-dated and regulator-funded. For Nexans it acts most directly on Grid and partly on Transmission. Strength: strong.
  2. High-voltage scarcity turning into contracted margin (industry tailwind, strong but the window is narrowing). Prices have plateaued high and executed margins rise as old contracts roll off through 2028 (the sector's strongest theme, rated B, medium, on 23 October). Nexans benefits least of the three leaders because its conversion is the weakest (13.7 percent Transmission margin against 21.2 percent at Prysmian and 20.2 percent at NKT) and its backlog the thinnest, and the window is being shortened by entrants and by transmission operators' cost discipline. Strength: moderate for Nexans.
  3. Data-centre power (sector tailwind, strong, concentrated). Strong in North American Connect and Grid; the financing crack is the risk. Strength: moderate to strong.
  4. Offshore wind (mixed). Europe stable with 37 GW of auctions in the next twelve months and German contract reform; US cancellations; zero-bid auctions in places; developers stretched by rates. Strength: neutral.
  5. Protectionism and local content (tailwind with edges). Raises barriers in Europe and rewards US local production; blocks Canadian rod; can be waived for cost. Strength: mild tailwind.
  6. Construction cycle (value-chain headwind, strong in Europe). The one theme acting directly against about a third of Nexans's profit (European Connect). Strength: moderate headwind, no relief before 2027 to 2028.
  7. Input inflation outside metals (value-chain headwind, mild to moderate). Polymers, energy, labour. Strength: mild headwind through 2027.
  8. Rates and risk-off (sector headwind to the multiple, strong now). Strength: strong headwind to valuation, weak to earnings.

Overall external picture. At the sector level the tailwinds are strong and structural; at the industry level they are net positive but the high-voltage leg is maturing into a phase where conversion and order replacement matter more than demand; at the value-chain points where Nexans is most exposed (European construction, mass-impregnated subsea, US low voltage) headwinds are concentrated. The external picture supports earnings growth through 2028 with good visibility in Grid and fair visibility in Transmission, and argues for caution on any assumption that Nexans's Transmission margin converges fully to peers or that Connect Europe recovers before 2028.

Internal change forces/risks

D6219th of 36

Memo topic 2, Internal change forces: the Rater's score

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

Internal change forces

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group. The rating above stands for it, so it carries no number of its own.

Nexans has finished the transformation that made it a focused electrification company and is now in a harder, less visible phase: converting a strong market into margin through capacity, utilisation and integration, under a leadership team that is new at every level. The positive forces are broad and coherent and the board has bound management to the existing targets, but the most consequential near-term items (the replacement Mass Impregnated order, the Transmission margin path, Republic Wire's commercial ramp and the Connect margin) are all still to be proven, and disclosure has become thinner just as proof is needed. The capital-allocation stance (acquisitions first, buybacks only to offset dilution, even at a 10x forward price-to-earnings ratio) is the main internal choice the market may continue to discount. For the model: Transmission margin rising steadily on vintage roll-off and Electra; Grid volume up with capacity; Republic Wire at its entry EBITDA plus a slow synergy ramp; Connect Europe flat; no buybacks; leverage falling towards 1.0x only in the absence of further acquisitions.

The risk profile is dominated by one dated, binary operational item (the replacement order for Halden's mass-impregnated line), a cluster of correlated execution risks (subsea projects, the US integration, simultaneous capacity ramps under a new team) and a sector-wide sentiment risk tied to the capacity-overhang narrative and the rates regime. C-14

To what extent are standards improving in execution, operational measures, governance or cost discipline? (choices and actions)

D5425th of 35

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement. Standards are improving where the company controls the outcome (project execution, capacity delivery, board governance, selectivity), and slipping in two places that matter to an investor: Connect profitability and disclosure. On balance an improvement, but not yet one that shows in the group margin.

Improving:

  • Transmission execution: margin +195 basis points to 13.7 percent in H1 2026 on flat sales; the Electra vessel delivered on time and within its €380m budget and is booked to end-2028; 525 kV extruded subsea cable moved from qualification into industrial production for TenneT's LanWin2 in October 2026; Charleroi's high-voltage tower inaugurated on schedule.
  • Integration: Republic Wire installed two new production lines, runs seven days a week, internalised compound sourcing and gave Nexans access to more than 250 US sales agents within four months; La Triveneta Cavi met its synergy milestones (bought at 5.6x EBITDA, 4.6x after synergies).
  • Operating model: SHIFT continues as SHIFT AI (useful operational data for managers from 20 to 90 percent, dynamic pricing, stock modelling); the 2018 to 2024 programme delivered about €300m of structural EBITDA.
  • Governance: board independence 80 percent; a fully independent audit committee; one share one vote; a dedicated Chief Operating Officer.
  • Culture: engagement 79 percent (above the 2028 target of 78 percent); 46 percent participation in the 2025 employee share plan; core electrification turnover of about 10 percent.

Slipping:

  • Connect margin fell from 13.6 percent to 11.8 percent in H1 2026 (40 basis points below consensus) on Nordic weakness and dilutive Southern European and Latin American mix.
  • Cash conversion fell to 42.7 percent in H1 2026 from 83.1 percent (prior-year down-payments).
  • The nine-month update omitted adjusted EBITDA, segment margins, net income, free cash flow and working capital: a step back from the transparency for which Nexans was recognised (Labrador's top-20 list in France).
  • No clawback provisions; auditors in place for 20 years (PwC) and 11 years (Forvis Mazars).
C-86

What negative corporate change developments are present/plausible?

D3622nd of 35

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement. The negative developments cluster around execution load, capital intensity and transparency, and several of them reinforce each other: a new team running simultaneous capacity ramps and an acquisition integration, with leverage up, cash conversion down, the key replacement order unsigned, and less disclosure than before.

  • The replacement Mass Impregnated order is still unsigned. Preferred-bidder status was announced in July 2026 with a line "fully loaded until mid-2028"; 2026 guidance assumes the line is loaded at year-end; Deutsche Bank expected the award "within weeks" in early October; the 22 October update did not announce it, and Mallorca-Menorca is an extruded alternating-current project. A slip into 2027 leaves Halden under-absorbed.

C-59

How holistic and congruent is the culture, with alignment across the total system (as at Lifco, Avanza or Ryanair)? Such that the goals, strategy, culture, execution, decisions are congruent, holistic and symbiotic?

C-5515th of 35

Layer 1 · Company - Management

The Investment Analyst's heading How holistic and congruent is the culture, with alignment across the total system?

Judgement. Operationally the system is congruent: goals (margin, cash, returns), strategy (selectivity), culture (SHIFT, E3) and incentives (EBITDA, free cash flow conversion, ROCE, relative shareholder return) point the same way, and employees are aligned through ownership. The incongruence is at the top, in how the last leadership change was handled, which weakens the claim that the system puts shareholders first.

C-81

What fundamental risks are there that are sudden, high-magnitude and low-probability, and how plausible and how large is each?

C4415th of 35

Layer 1 · Risks

Judgement. The fundamental tail risks are concentrated in high-voltage project execution and utilisation, in the US acquisition and in unindexed input costs; none is likely in any one year, but several are correlated (an idle Halden line weakens bargaining power on replacement work; a US price war and tariffs hit Republic Wire together), and the company's history shows its crises arrive in clusters.

  1. Replacement Mass Impregnated order delayed beyond Q1 2027 or lost. Plausibility: about 25 to 30 percent for a material delay (preferred-bidder status since July; unsigned at 22 October). Magnitude: about €25m to €60m of EBITDA a year from under-absorption while it lasts, weaker pricing on stopgap work (repairs), and a share-price reaction back to the Great Sea Interconnector scare band (a 17 percent fall in H1 2026; 11x forward earnings or below).
  2. A subsea project execution failure (cable fault, installation incident, liquidated damages). Plausibility: low, about 5 to 10 percent a year across a large project book. Magnitude: €50m to €150m of provisions; Prysmian's 2018 Western Link precedent (€165m, 140 basis points of margin, shares -38 percent).
  3. Republic Wire impairment or a US low-voltage price war. Plausibility: about 15 to 20 percent over two years (Prysmian rates US low-voltage margin compression at 60 percent for itself; Atkore's margin halved from 30 to 15 percent). Magnitude: a goodwill write-down of €100m to €300m and €20m to €40m of lost EBITDA a year.
  4. An unindexed input shock (polymers, energy, freight) that outruns pricing. Plausibility: medium, 20 to 30 percent while Brent is near $100 and the Strait of Hormuz is disrupted. Magnitude: €30m to €70m of EBITDA in a year (the 2010 polymer precedent was about €70m).
  5. Antitrust outcomes. Plausibility: low to medium. Magnitude: above the €75m contingency provision in a bad case (the UK Spottiswoode class action; Italian and French proceedings; Prysmian carries €200m of specific provisions).
  6. Geopolitical escalation around the Great Sea Interconnector. Plausibility: low. Magnitude: limited to the upside option now that guidance excludes the project, but the €1.2bn sits in the backlog.
  7. A copper collapse. Plausibility: medium. Magnitude: mostly optical (Core exposure reversal in reported profit, lower current-metal revenue, a working-capital release that helps cash).
  8. A Norwegian krone shock. A 10 percent fall in the krone helps the income statement (about +€28m to +€32m of net income) and hurts equity (about -€140m); a stronger krone does the reverse.
D-03

In relation to the differences between the forecasts over the next 18 months to four years, vs. the last four to six years' financials, how diffuse or concentrated are the key drivers that generate the differences?

D5019th of 35

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement. The drivers are concentrated by identity and diffuse by risk, and the distinction is the whole answer. By identity, three items generate about four-fifths of the EBITDA difference between the next two to four years and the last four to six: Transmission margin conversion, the Republic Wire scope step and Grid volume on new capacity; two of them (Transmission and Republic Wire) account for 61 percent of the FY2025-to-FY2028 increase and 72 percent of the FY2025-to-FY2027 increase. That is far more concentrated than 2020 to 2025, when the improvement came from a management programme (SHIFT) acting on thousands of accounts and dozens of plants, a portfolio rotation across three disposals, a doubling of Halden's volume and a scarcity cycle that lifted every segment at once. By risk, however, the concentration is less than it looks, because the three large drivers depend on different things that do not fail together: Transmission conversion on contracts already signed and a vessel already delivered; Republic Wire on US building-wire prices; Grid on European utility frameworks and plant commissioning. The one driver that is both concentrated and binary is small in the bridge (the loading of Halden's mass-impregnated line, perhaps €15m to €25m a year inside the Transmission figure) but large in its effect on perception, because it is the one the market has learned to watch. Below EBITDA the differences from history are concentrated and mechanical: depreciation up about €56m and net financial items up about €32m between FY2025 and FY2027, both dated and both already visible in H1 2026. The concentration therefore raises the stakes on a few dated events (the replacement order; the 24 February and July 2027 prints; US pricing through 2027) without making the aggregate forecast fragile, and it makes the case more trackable than the diffuse self-help story that preceded it.

The differences, quantified.

Period What the forecast differs from The difference Drivers of the difference, in order
FY2027 against FY2025 (the 18-month window) EBITDA €728m to €909m +€181m Republic Wire scope +€72m (40 percent); Transmission vintage, Electra and loading +€58m (32 percent); Grid volume and mix +€37m (20 percent); Other +€5m; Connect organic +€9m
FY2028 against FY2025 €728m to €1,013m +€285m Transmission +€94m (33 percent); Republic Wire scope and synergies about +€80m (28 percent); Grid +€64m (22 percent); Connect organic about +€42m (15 percent); Other +€5m
FY2030 against FY2025 €728m to €1,103m +€375m the same three, plus Grid normalising and Transmission flat: no new driver after FY2028
Below EBITDA, FY2027 against FY2025 depreciation €244m to €300m; net financial items -€60m to -€85m; tax rate 30.9 to 30.0 percent -€81m of pre-tax drag Electra, Halden, Republic Wire plant and the acquisition debt: dated, visible, mechanical
Cash, FY2027 against FY2025 free cash flow €344m (company) to about €303m (company basis) about -€40m no down-payment tailwind (a flat backlog), capital spending still at €320m, interest and reorganisation cash

Against the last four to six years, the difference in character is as the second set described: FY2020 to FY2025 added about 580 basis points of margin from many small actions, and FY2025 to FY2028 adds about 170 basis points from three large ones. The volume side is also narrower: group organic growth of about 4.5 percent a year comes almost entirely from Grid (8 to 9 percent) and North American Connect, with Transmission flat to low single digit and Connect Europe flat.

The sensitivity of each driver to one event.

  • Transmission conversion is sensitive to one event only at the margin: the replacement order's timing moves FY2027 by €5m to €8m per quarter of slip beyond March 2027 and by €15m to €20m per half-year if the line stands idle; an execution incident on a large project (liquidated damages, a cable fault) would be the larger single-event risk (€50m to €150m on the Western Link precedent, probability 5 to 10 percent a year). The rest of the conversion (legacy frameworks finishing by late 2026; Electra replacing charters) has no single point of failure.
  • Republic Wire is sensitive to one variable rather than one event: US building-wire spreads, which are set by Southwire and Prysmian and supported by a tariff schedule that is itself in doubt (Deutsche Bank, 25 October 2026). A normalisation from about 15 percent margins towards 11 percent (Morgan Stanley's earlier scenario) would take €15m to €25m off FY2028; a tariff delay with 600 kt of COMEX stock released would be the trigger.
  • Grid is sensitive to commissioning dates and to one customer behaviour (call-off rates under frameworks); the demand side has no single point of failure, as the breadth of Q3 (European utilities, North American utilities and data centres, South American solar, Turkey, Morocco) shows.
  • Connect organic is diffuse and weak: construction, distributor stocking, polymers, energy, mix; no single event, but no single remedy either.

How concentrated, compared with peers. NKT is the extreme case of concentration (over 70 percent of EBITDA from high-voltage projects, down-payment-funded cash, and a 2027 to 2028 Karlskrona ramp as the single event), Prysmian the diffuse case (Transmission, Grid, US low voltage, telecom and now Atkore). Nexans sits between: three engines, of which the most profitable is also the one with the single specialised asset. The relevant historical lesson from T-D2 is that forecasts resting on one or two high-voltage drivers were met far less often than diversified ones (Nexans 2013 and 2018, NKT 2019, Prysmian 2018), and that the failures came from utilisation gaps and execution faults, not from demand. Nexans today has a 4.5-year backlog with down-payments, which it did not have in 2013 or 2018, so the base rate of failure is lower than its own history suggests, but the mechanism of failure, if it comes, would be the same one.

What the concentration implies for monitoring and for the memo. Three dated checks cover about 80 percent of the FY2027 difference: the replacement order's signature (by April 2027 at the latest on the conversion average); the H2 2026 Transmission margin and the FY2027 guide on 24 February 2027; and Republic Wire's first half-year of disclosed contribution (also 24 February) alongside US price announcements. Grid needs only the quarterly organic growth print. That is a tractable case to track, which is the positive side of concentration; the negative side is that the share price will move on each of these three events whatever the aggregate says, as it did on 22 October (down about 7 percent in two days on a sales beat, because the order was absent).

Internal corporate change forces

C-11

To what extent have the chief executive, or other key leaders (e.g. finance director, operations director, board, division heads, etc.) or other key actors (e.g. activists, major shareholders) changed, and what does the change signal?

D5218th of 35

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement. The whole top of the company has changed inside thirteen months, and the controlling shareholder has left. The change signals continuity of strategy with a deliberate shift of emphasis from portfolio surgery to industrial execution, and the sequence of appointments, ending with a dedicated Chief Operating Officer in October 2026, is an admission that execution (plant loading, capacity ramps, supply chain, Transmission conversion) is now the binding constraint. It also means the delivery record the market relies on belongs to people who have left.

  • Chief executive. Christopher Guérin, architect of the 2018 to 2025 turnaround, was replaced on 13 October 2025 by Julien Hueber, a 23-year insider who ran PWR Grid and Connect Europe (about €2.6bn of revenue, 23 plants) and took its adjusted EBITDA margin from 6.7 percent in 2021 to 16.4 percent in 2025. The shares fell nearly 10 percent on the announcement. The Q3 2026 Grid outperformance (+14.8 percent organic) came from the division he built, which is the best current evidence for him.
  • Finance director. Jean-Christophe Juillard left in July 2025; Vincent Piquet (Renault's Ampere, General Electric) took over on 19 January 2026 and has since raised the low end of 2026 guidance and refinanced the Republic Wire bridge with a €500m five-year bond at 4.25 percent.
  • Operations. Stéphane Artaud (30 years at Saint-Gobain, industrial performance and capacity optimisation) becomes Chief Operating Officer on 1 November 2026, replacing an interim arrangement; announced just before the nine-month update.
  • Executive committee and board. A reorganised executive committee from January 2026 (12 to 13 members, five regions, new heads for data centres and large projects, Tim King from Southwire's integration team for North America); a board at 80 percent independence after the 2026 annual meeting (from 60 percent), with Antonio Cammisecra and Thierry Fournier (Saint-Gobain turnaround experience) added.
  • Shareholders. Quiñenco (through Invexans) cut its stake from about 28.8 percent over three years to 4.13 percent, ending the 2022 governance agreement and taking its three directors off the board. Bpifrance holds 5.2 percent, employees 3.9 percent.
  • What it signals beyond continuity: the outgoing chief executive and finance director sold €781k of shares between March and August 2025, and the board waived the presence condition on 31,794 of Guérin's unvested performance shares; neither is explained. In September 2026 the board proposed a further 12,600 performance shares for Hueber (18,600 in total), tied to 2028 organic growth, EBITDA, cash conversion, ROCE and relative shareholder return: the board is binding the new chief executive to the existing targets.

For the model. Treat the 2018 to 2025 delivery record as evidence about the business model, not about the current team; weight management delivery evidence from 2026 onward more heavily, and treat the Chief Operating Officer appointment as a forward remedy whose earliest observable effect is H2 2027.

C-12

To what extent have the strategy or the strategic priorities changed, and how well is the change articulated and resourced? (choices)

D5823rd of 35

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement. The strategy has not changed (a pure-play electrification company run on value over volume, aiming at the 2028 targets set in November 2024), but the priorities inside it have changed materially: from "restructure and simplify" to "intensify and amplify". The change is well articulated and reasonably resourced, but it now asks the organisation to execute several growth programmes at once, funded partly by debt, with acquisitions explicitly needed to reach the 2028 ambition.

  • The priorities, as now stated: scale Grid (European capacity up about 40 percent by 2028; €62m for 39 percent more medium-voltage accessories capacity from 2026 to 2028 in Europe and the United States, with US lead times down by up to 60 percent; a new medium-voltage site in Morocco through 2027 to 2028); build North America (Electro Cables in Canada, December 2025; Republic Wire, June 2026; North American current sales about €1bn); a data-centre vertical (a dedicated executive, 800-volt direct-current and superconducting products, the Stella Nova centre); Transmission judged on profitability rather than volume (Electra, Charleroi 525 kV land capacity from mid-2027, a high-teens margin target of 17 percent by 2028); recycling (Lens, 80,000 tonnes from 2027); and M&A as "a central capital-allocation priority" with "a rich worldwide pipeline".
  • Articulation. Good: the 2028 bridge is shown by segment (Transmission commercial growth and execution quality, Grid volume and industrial excellence, Connect volume and mix, plus additional M&A), from €728m in 2025 to €1,150m in 2028; advanced offers are targeted at more than 50 percent of Grid and Connect revenue by 2028.
  • Resourcing. €1.2bn of capital expenditure planned for 2025 to 2028 (capex was €383m in 2025 and €191m in H1 2026, about 6 percent of standard sales), net debt raised to €1,038m (1.4x on the company's ratio) for Republic Wire, a leverage ceiling of 1.0x by 2028, and stated M&A firepower of "more than €1.2bn" (Piquet) or "over €2bn" (Deutsche Bank). The tension is real: on consensus free cash flow (€309m, €439m, €534m for FY2026 to FY2028) and the dividend, leverage reaches about 1.0x by 2028 only if further acquisitions are modest.
  • What is not resourced: additional high-voltage subsea capacity. Prysmian and NKT are adding towers and vessels; Nexans is improving utilisation and economics of what it has (a choice consistent with value over volume, but one that a Prysmian expert describes as "limited capacity expansion" and that explains part of the share loss).
C-13

To what extent has financial guidance been raised, or have mid-term targets or capital-markets-day ambitions been set or raised? (choices)

C5415th of 35

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement. Guidance has been raised once in 2026, but not organically, and the mid-term targets have only been reaffirmed. The pattern is "guide light, raise at the half-year, confirm at the nine months", which is consistent with the company's conservative habit but does not yet amount to an organic upgrade cycle.

  • FY2026. Initial guidance in February 2026 was adjusted EBITDA of €730m to €810m, excluding the delayed Great Sea Interconnector, called "surprisingly light" by Jefferies. On 29 July it was raised to €770m to €840m; Bernstein judged the €35m midpoint rise "almost entirely" the seven months of Republic Wire. Free cash flow guidance was raised to €235m to €325m. On 22 October both ranges were confirmed despite a 4 percent revenue beat and organic growth twice consensus.
  • 2028. Adjusted EBITDA of €1,150m plus or minus €75m, Electrification organic sales growth of 3 to 5 percent a year (2024 to 2028), free cash flow conversion above 45 percent, ROCE above 20 percent, leverage at or below 1.0x and a payout of at least 30 percent: reaffirmed in February, July and October 2026, never raised. The February 2026 business deep-dive confirmed every target.
  • The gap that matters: consensus FY2028 adjusted EBITDA of €1,052m sits below the bottom of the company's range (€1,075m). The company's bridge explicitly includes "additional M&A", which consensus does not model; the gap is therefore mostly unannounced acquisitions rather than disbelief.

For the model. Do not treat the 2028 target as an organic forecast; the organic run-rate the company's own bridge supports is roughly €1,050m to €1,100m. Expect FY2027 guidance on 24 February 2027 to be set below consensus by habit.
