C-15

To what extent is the company reducing its perimeter or composition, through divestments, simplification or exits from low-return units? (actions)

B+686th of 35

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement. The perimeter reduction is complete and was large: it is the most finished of all the change forces, and it has improved the quality and comparability of earnings. Its cost is the loss of diversification and of about €200m of legacy EBITDA that M&A is now expected to replace.

  • AmerCable sold for $280m (January 2025), Lynxeo for €525m (June 2025) and Autoelectric to Motherson for €207m enterprise value (3 July 2026; about €708m of 2025 current sales, about 13,000 employees, about €55m of EBITDA, so about 3.8x). The Hanover land high-voltage plant was closed after under-utilisation. The metallurgy line is shrinking its external copper-wire sales by design (Other Activities -15.6 percent organic in H1 2026).
  • Industry and Solutions is classified as discontinued under IFRS 5, which is why history is restated (FY2024 adjusted EBITDA €804m as first reported against €571m restated; FY2025 €728m restated) and why the FY2025 brokers'-basis earnings per share of €8.08 is not a continuing base.
  • What remains: three Electrification segments (Transmission, Grid, Connect) and Other Activities; the headcount is now about 12,800 in 39 countries on the continuing basis.
  • Nothing further of size is left to sell; the next simplification lever would be footprint consolidation inside Connect Europe if construction stays weak (the Hanover precedent shows management will close plants).
C-16

To what extent is the company entering new geographic or product markets, and how sensibly? (actions)

F4634th of 35

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement. Nexans is entering two new markets at once: the US low-voltage and data-centre cable market through acquisition, and the data-centre power-distribution product market through innovation. The strategic logic of both is sound; the US entry is expensive, sub-scale and unproven, and its first quarter of contribution came in below expectations.

  • United States. Republic Wire (Cincinnati; about €520m of annual revenue; more than 200 employees; 18 national sales agencies and access to more than 250 agents) was bought for about €680m enterprise value plus a potential €43m earn-out, about 10.3x 2027 estimated EBITDA before synergies (about €66m) and 7.6x after €23m (also quoted as €23.6m) of run-rate synergies within three years, split evenly between commercial cross-selling and purchasing. The logic: a protected market (Section 232 tariffs; Buy America), local production (a 30 percent capacity expansion running seven days a week, fully online by December 2026), a channel for Canadian medium-voltage cable into data centres, and immediate high single-digit accretion to earnings per share. The weaknesses: Nexans enters at about 4 percent share against Prysmian and Southwire at about 60 to 65 percent, both vertically integrated with US rod mills; US tariffs stop it using its Canadian rod; former executives doubt Nexans's North American integration record; Bernstein measured Q3 Connect scope at 24.7 percent against 28 percent expected; and Bernstein cautions that data-centre selling carries lower gross margins than the acquired residential and commercial business.
  • Data-centre products. 800-volt direct-current architectures (the AmpaCity microgrid, July 2026), high-temperature superconducting cable for loads above 500 megawatts (priced at five to ten times conventional cable), the SupraMarine superconducting offshore consortium with Air Liquide and RTE (November 2025), pre-assembled accessories cutting field installation time by 50 percent. Sensible, small in revenue today, unquantified.
  • Smaller geographic moves: a medium-voltage plant in Morocco (2027 to 2028); Cables RCT in Spain; Electro Cables in Canada (about €125m of sales).

For the model. Hold Republic Wire at about €66m of EBITDA for 2027 rising to €80m to €90m by 2029 with synergies, and carry a downside case in which US low-voltage pricing normalises (Prysmian's own risk work puts a 60 percent probability on US low-voltage margin compression, with Atkore's margin halving from 30 to 15 percent as precedent).

C-17

To what extent is the company raising prices in real terms (above cost inflation), and are the rises real, merely nominal, or so far only latent or plausible and increasingly noted by management? (actions)

B589th of 35

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

The Investment Analyst's heading To what extent is the company raising prices in real terms (above cost inflation), and are the rises real, merely nominal, or so far only latent?

Judgement. Real price increases are evident in high voltage and, more modestly, in Grid; in Connect there are none and margins are falling. The most important point for the model is that in Transmission the price rise has already happened industry-wide, but Nexans has converted much less of it into margin than its peers, and the remaining conversion is mechanical (old contracts rolling off) rather than dependent on new pricing.

  • Transmission: real, partly realised. Industry contract value per kilometre for 525 kV high-voltage direct current doubled from about €0.62m (2019 to 2020 awards) to €1.44m (2024 to 2026), per DNB Carnegie; part is scope, most is price. Nexans's Transmission margin rose 195 basis points to 13.7 percent on flat sales in H1 2026 because "legacy dilutive framework agreements roll off" (Bernstein) and concessions were refused. Peers execute the same price regime at 21.2 percent (Prysmian, Q2 2026) and 20.2 percent (NKT, Q2 2026, with about 200 basis points of ramp cost inside). Management targets 17 percent by 2028.
  • Grid: real, modest. Prices are fully indexed to inputs; demand above capacity supports value-added accessories; the margin rose from about 6.7 percent in 2021 to the mid-teens and was 15.4 percent in H1 2026 (40 basis points below consensus on project mix). Customers are committing earlier and paying for certainty (expert, with lead times above 12 to 14 months).
  • Connect: not real. The margin fell 180 basis points in a year; European low-voltage pricing is described as "structurally vulnerable to normalisation" as shortages ease (former Prysmian finance director); US building-wire pricing is set by the duopoly.
  • Cost inflation that is not passed through: polymers, energy, labour and freight (see C-05). In Connect and Grid, these can absorb nominal price increases in 2027.
  • Latent: Electra's removal of third-party vessel charter costs, and the pricing of the next high-voltage awards, where entrants now bid.
C-18

To what extent is the company stepping up mergers and acquisitions, and how strategically sensible, disciplined and well integrated are they? (actions)

C-5417th of 35

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement. M&A has clearly stepped up and is now the declared primary use of capital. The deals are strategically coherent (all inside electrification, all adding regional manufacturing and channel density), integration has been good in Europe, and the one large deal, Republic Wire, was priced at a level that requires its synergies and US data-centre growth to deliver to earn the cost of capital. The worry is less any single deal than the capital-allocation pattern: buying at 10.3x EBITDA while the company's own shares trade at about 7x and buybacks are confined to anti-dilution.

  • Deals since 2024: La Triveneta Cavi (Italy, €520m enterprise value, 5.6x pre-synergy and 4.6x post-synergy EBITDA, about €800m of sales; synergy milestones delivered); Reka Cables (Finland; EBITDA doubled in two years under SHIFT); Centelsa (Colombia, about 9.5x); Cables RCT (Spain); Electro Cables (Canada); Republic Wire (United States, about €680m, 10.3x). Centelsa, Reka and others are now presented as a network of regional platforms serving data centres, critical buildings, solar and storage.
  • Discipline: good on European bolt-ons; weaker on Republic Wire (a 40 percent premium to Nexans's own multiple; about a 10 percent pre-tax return on entry, about 13 percent with synergies, against a group ROCE of 21.3 percent in 2025). Jefferies says further M&A is required to reach the 2028 targets; Hueber says the company will "continue to act on opportunities aligned with its strategy rather than wait for a more convenient time".
  • Integration capability: the SHIFT playbook is a proven integration tool in Europe; former executives (a former Nexans Canada finance director, a former director) doubt the North American record; Tim King's Southwire integration experience is the specific mitigant.

For the model. Do not add unannounced M&A to the base case; carry a sensitivity of €40m to €80m of acquired EBITDA by 2028 at 8x to 10x, financed with debt, which keeps leverage near 1.0x to 1.3x rather than falling below 1.0x.

C-19

To what extent is the company making more use of its balance sheet (for investment, acquisitions or returns), or stepping up shareholder returns (a buyback starting or increasing, a special or progressive dividend), or is management increasingly mooting either? (actions)

D4625th of 35

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement. The balance sheet is being used, for acquisitions and capacity, not for shareholder returns. Dividends grow steadily; buybacks are formulaic and anti-dilutive; nothing suggests a buyback programme while the M&A pipeline is "rich", even with the shares at the bottom of their valuation range.

  • Net debt rose from €265.6m at end-2025 (0.36x) to €1,038.2m at 30 June 2026 (1.4x on the company's ratio; FactSet's 1.51x includes leases). Debt is fixed-rate; the €500m bond due September 2031 carries 4.25 percent; a €400m sustainability-linked bond matures in April 2028 with a 50 basis point step-up feature; the rating is BB+; the syndicated facility covenant is 3.2x.
  • The 2025 dividend rose 11.5 percent to €2.90 (payout about 39 percent on the house's earnings definition); policy is at least 30 percent of recurring net income; consensus has €3.12, €3.40 and €3.79 for FY2026 to FY2028 (a yield of about 2.7 percent at €113.60).
  • The July 2026 buyback covers up to 176,005 shares for employee and executive plans; corporate officers and employees hold 5.3 and 3.9 percent; diluted shares are about 45.0m against 43.7m basic, so the share count drifts up.
  • Bernstein notes buybacks could become an alternative if acquisitions do not materialise; management has not mooted it.
C-20

What other material positive corporate change forces are present/plausible?

–64

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement. Four further positive forces are under way, and together they are the self-help the market is least likely to have priced: the Electra vessel's cost and tender effects, 525 kV commercialisation, the Grid capacity and accessories step, and digital operating tools.

  • Electra. 13,500 tonnes of cable capacity (about 35 percent more than Aurora), three turntables, the ability to lay four cables at once; it reduces costly third-party charters and improves scheduling and tender competitiveness. Bernstein judges it capable of supporting Transmission margin beyond the 2028 target if utilisation stays high.
  • 525 kV in production. Moving from qualification to delivery under TenneT's €1.7bn framework (LanWin2, then BalWin3) raises qualification barriers and gives Nexans references for the next German and Dutch corridors; Charleroi adds 525 kV land capacity from mid-2027.
  • Grid capacity and accessories. About 40 percent more European Grid capacity by 2028 and 39 percent more accessories capacity from 2026 to 2028; accessories earn structurally higher margins than base cable (about twice, on Prysmian's evidence), and Grid demand already exceeds supply.
  • Circularity and low-carbon supply as a procurement edge. Lens recycling (up to 80,000 tonnes from 2027, recycled copper content target 25 percent by 2028), the Hydro aluminium deal, CableLoop and the Vesto reuse partnership (October 2026), which matter in tenders that weight carbon and circularity, as Enedis did.
  • Plausible: a resumption of the Great Sea Interconnector (€1.2bn, Meridiam's 66 percent, a 3.7 percent regulated return premium) as pure upside to guidance; superconducting data-centre awards; the new Chief Operating Officer's purchasing and footprint mutualisation.
C-87

To what extent have key controlling actors (e.g. activists, major shareholders) indicated scope for change in strategy/approach?

D5011th of 35

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement. None has. There is no controlling actor left: Quiñenco's exit to 4.13 percent removed the anchor, Bpifrance's 5.2 percent is a passive, stabilising holding, institutions hold about 81 percent and no activist position is recorded. The register is now open enough that an activist could appear, and the valuation gap to peers (about a 30 percent discount on enterprise value to EBITDA; Nexans at about 7x 2027 EBITDA against Prysmian 10.7x and NKT 10.1x in August) is the kind that attracts one; the obvious activist asks would be capital returns instead of acquisitions, or a sale of the company. Structural protections cut both ways: one share one vote and a 20 percent cap on voting rights at extraordinary meetings prevent a single holder vetoing strategy.

C-21

Taken together, how strong, broad and well-resourced are the positive corporate changes under way?

D6023rd of 35

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement. The positive corporate changes are broad (perimeter, leadership, geography, capacity, acquisitions, digital tools, governance) and reasonably well resourced, but their character has changed: the large, completed, self-financed changes of 2018 to 2025 are behind the company, and the changes now under way are execution-stage programmes whose output is still ahead. Seven of the twelve internal forces rate as strong on breadth; on proof, only the perimeter change and the Transmission margin step are delivered. The resourcing is adequate for the organic programmes and stretched only if M&A continues at the Republic Wire pace.

  • Strongest and most proven: perimeter reduction (complete); Transmission margin conversion (+195 basis points in H1 2026); Grid growth on capacity (Q3 +14.8 percent).
  • Broad but unproven: the new leadership team; Republic Wire and the North American platform; the Grid and accessories capacity steps; data-centre products.
  • Offsetting: a cluster of negative developments (C-86) centred on the unsigned replacement order, the flat backlog, rising capital intensity and weaker disclosure.

For the model. The internal changes justify margin expansion in Transmission and volume growth in Grid with reasonable confidence, and a contribution from Republic Wire with less; they do not justify assuming the company closes the margin gap to peers or meets its 2028 target without further acquisitions.

C-22

What staircase of novel positive developments lies ahead: which new, dated things can the market look forward to within the next 6 months, in months 6 to 18, and in months 18 to 36; how novel is each; and how well-founded?

D4425th of 35

Layer 1 · Stock market narrative

Judgement. There is a credible, dated staircase, but most of its steps are already expected, and the one that matters most in the next six months (the signature of the replacement Mass Impregnated order) is a known item that has already slipped once. The novel steps, those the market does not yet price, are a Great Sea Interconnector restart, a first FY2027 guide that proves conservative in practice, superconducting data-centre awards, Grid growth sustaining above the guideline, and any shift towards capital returns. The staircase is strongest in months 6 to 18, when capacity lands and the 2028 path becomes measurable.

Within the next six months (to April 2027):

  • Replacement Mass Impregnated order signature and financial close (expected Q4 2026 or Q1 2027). Novelty: low (preferred bidder since July). Foundation: good but slipping; the most important near-term step because it underwrites 2026 guidance and the Halden margin.
  • Peer read-across: Prysmian Q3 (29 October 2026), Siemens Energy's 2030 targets (11 November), NKT Q3 (19 November). Novelty: medium for sector sentiment.
  • Full-year 2026 results and the first FY2027 guidance (24 February 2027): EBITDA in the upper half of €770m to €840m, second-half Transmission margin, free cash flow and working capital, Republic Wire's first synergy disclosure, the dividend (consensus about €3.10). Novelty: medium (the FY2027 guide will likely be set below consensus by habit; a guide at or above consensus would be novel). Foundation: good on EBITDA, uncertain on cash.
  • Capacity starts: first European Grid capacity additions (late 2026); Republic Wire's 30 percent expansion fully online (December 2026). Novelty: low; foundation: good.
  • Mallorca-Menorca EPCI contract after the six-month engineering phase (around April 2027). Novelty: low.
  • Great Sea Interconnector: completion of seabed surveys under the 5 August tripartite agreement and Meridiam's closing. Novelty: high if it leads to a restart; foundation: moderate.

Months 6 to 18 (April 2027 to April 2028):

  • H1 2027 results (July 2027): the first clean half without perimeter noise; Transmission margin at or above 15 percent would be the proof the market needs. Novelty: medium; foundation: good (vintage roll-off, Electra).
  • A capital markets update (expected by the sell-side in 2027, undated): revised 2028 targets or new 2030 targets; peers have used such events to set ambitions above consensus. Novelty: high if Nexans sets 2030 targets above the street.
  • Charleroi 525 kV land capacity in service (mid-2027); Lens recycling (2027); Mallorca-Menorca production; LanWin2 and BalWin3 deliveries. Novelty: low to medium; foundation: good.
  • Republic Wire synergies (about €11m expected in year one) and North American data-centre wins. Novelty: medium; foundation: moderate.
  • Superconducting or 800-volt direct-current data-centre awards from the gigawatt-scale European quoting pipeline. Novelty: high; foundation: early.
  • Further acquisitions. Novelty: medium; reception depends on price (another Republic Wire multiple would be read negatively).
  • Deleveraging towards 1.0x and a possible change in capital-return policy. Novelty: high if buybacks appear; foundation: weak today.
  • Against the staircase: NKT's Karlskrona start (Q2 2027) and entrants' plant starts raise the industry's capacity, and the 37 GW of European offshore auctions and transmission-operator re-phasing decide how the next award round prices.

Months 18 to 36 (April 2028 to October 2029):

  • The 2028 target year: EBITDA €1,150m plus or minus €75m, Transmission at 17 percent, ROCE above 20 percent, leverage at or below 1.0x. Novelty: low (known); foundation: moderate (requires acquisitions).
  • Full European Grid capacity (+40 percent) and the Morocco plant. Novelty: low; foundation: good.
  • The mass-impregnated line's order book beyond mid-2028. Novelty: medium; foundation: currently absent, the gap in the staircase.
  • The 2029 to 2030 capacity-parity debate will dominate sentiment; Nexans's lower high-voltage weight makes it relatively defensive in that phase.

Foundations

3

Value chain dynamics and industry structure

D5818th of 35

The Investment Analyst's group judgement: Industry and Value Chain

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

Industry and Value Chain

D5818th of 35

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The industry structure is one of the best in capital goods today in high voltage and medium voltage: a concentrated, capacity-constrained, disciplined oligopoly that has moved pricing power and working-capital funding from customers to makers. Nexans sits inside it but is the weakest of the three leaders on the measures that decide how much of that structure it can capture (backlog depth, capacity growth, margin conversion, share trend), and it is the most exposed to the two places where the structure is cracking first: Mediterranean and mass-impregnated subsea work, where a local challenger is winning, and US low voltage, where it is a small entrant facing an integrated duopoly. Its compensating strength is European Grid, where demand, frameworks and its own capacity step line up. The support is solid through 2028 and becomes fragile in a dated way from 2029 as capacity and entrants land; history shows that this sector's prices and multiples fall quickly when scarcity ends. For the model: assume structure-supported margins through 2028, a gradual flattening of Transmission award prices for post-2029 delivery, and no share gains in subsea.

Value chain dynamics

C-32How is customer demand behaving: to what extent does it support the thesis, and how fragile is that support?C+6411th of 35full answer under 1b External tailwinds
C-33

To what extent are the route to market and the intermediaries helping or hindering the company, and how fragile is that?

D5321st of 35

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. The route to market is neutral to slightly helpful: direct relationships dominate the profitable segments, and in Connect the distributors are powerful but integrated with Nexans through inventory systems. The new US agent channel is a genuine asset but untested. The main fragility is the distributor stock cycle, which can make Connect volumes look better or worse than end demand.

  • Transmission and Grid: sold direct to transmission and distribution operators and developers, under frameworks and tenders; no intermediary margin.
  • Connect Europe: through wholesale distributors (Rexel, Sonepar, Wesco; Sonepar is about 7 percent of receivables, the only customer above 5 percent), who take 10 to 15 percent markups, carry competing brands and price weekly on metal. SHIFT cut 16,000 low-value accounts and embedded inventory-management systems with the remaining 4,000 "platinum" partners, raising switching costs for distributors.
  • United States: Republic Wire's more than 250 long-standing sales agents now sell Nexans products; exclusive US distributor arrangements favour Prysmian; sales-agent productivity is unquantified.
  • Stock-cycle fragility: distributors' sales can rise because customers use more, refill shelves or pay higher prices; only the first lasts (Wisdom Library entry on supplier recoveries). Rexel's Q2 2026 split was volume +3.1 percent, cable prices +2.2 percent; copper at a record creates inventory incentives that can flatter one quarter. The Consumer should read Connect organic growth (measured at constant metal prices, which removes the price effect but not refill effects) with this in mind.
C-34

How are existing competitors behaving, does that help or hurt the company, and how fragile is the company's position against them?

F3431st of 35

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. Competitors are behaving rationally on price but aggressively on capacity and scope, and the net effect hurts Nexans's relative position: Prysmian is building a combined energy-and-data platform and a US one-stop shop, NKT is about to double its high-voltage delivery capability, and both are converting the price cycle into margin faster. Nexans's position is not fragile in absolute terms (its backlog and frameworks run through 2028), but it is relatively weaker in every head-to-head except European Grid.

  • Prysmian: Transmission margin 21.2 percent (Q2 2026), FY2026 EBITDA guidance raised twice to €2.8bn to €2.9bn, Atkore (about €3.3bn), Channell, a €5.5bn ten-year optical deal with Molex, three new vessels by 2027, 60 percent more US medium-voltage capacity by end-2026 against 2022; nearly half of group EBITDA American.
  • NKT: Transmission margin 20.2 percent (Q2 2026), guidance raised to €400m to €430m, €13.0bn backlog plus more than €2.5bn of TenneT commitments, Karlskrona and Cologne capacity in 2027, a second vessel (Eleonora, 23,000 tonnes) in 2027; widely expected to have the sector's fastest growth.
  • Cenergy (Hellenic Cables): an 18.7 percent group adjusted EBITDA margin in H1 2026 without owning vessels; Mediterranean awards in Nexans's Great Sea Interconnector region.
  • Sumitomo, LS Cable, Taihan: building European and US capacity; LS Cable's TenneT awards are the first entrant wins.
  • Southwire: more than $2bn modernisation; US incumbent against Republic Wire.
  • Helps Nexans: their discipline keeps prices high; their full order books keep customers committing early.
C-37What are regulators and political actors doing, does it help or hurt the company, and how fragile is that position?B+666th of 35full answer under 1b External tailwinds
C-38

On whom in the value chain are disruption forces acting (demand, competition, regulators and political actors, suppliers, any other actor), how is each acting, and how fragile do they leave the company's position?

D4424th of 35

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. Disruption is acting most on Nexans's customers (cost pressure and technology choices) and competitors (new qualified capacity), less on regulators and suppliers. The combined effect leaves Nexans robust in Grid, moderately fragile in Transmission beyond 2028 and fragile in US low voltage.

  • Demand side. Transmission operators: cost and affordability pressure leading to re-phasing, procurement coordination and technology substitution (overhead lines). Distribution operators: accelerating, law-dated replacement (switchgear gas bans from 2026, Dutch fast-track substations from 2027). Hyperscalers: architecture changes (800-volt direct current, superconductors) and financing stress. Developers: offshore wind economics.
  • Competition. Entrants crossing the 525 kV and vessel barriers; regional localisation multiplying plants that compete for the same regional demand (Sumitomo in Germany and Scotland, Prysmian's US build-out, Nexans's own Morocco and US moves), weakening the assumption that global scarcity guarantees pricing.
  • Regulators and political actors. Protectionism (helpful), overhead-line and affordability shifts (unhelpful).
  • Suppliers. Copper scarcity (favours integrated makers); polymer and energy shocks after Hormuz (hurts non-integrated cost lines); labour scarcity (hurts all).
  • Installers. Pre-assembled accessories cut field time by 50 percent; integrated cable makers are absorbing accessory specialists' wallet share (Pfisterer's engineering manager).

C-24

Where does the company rank by market share or size for its activity or product, and how far is it from the number one or two position?

F5427th of 35

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. Nexans is a top-three global cable maker by size and one of the three European high-voltage leaders, but its position is uneven by activity, and in the activity that carries the highest margins (high-voltage subsea) the distance to the leaders is widening rather than closing.

  • Group. About €6.1bn of standard sales (€7.8bn at current metal prices) in 2025 against Prysmian's €19.7bn (about three times larger) and NKT's €2.7bn of standard revenue; Nexans calls itself the world's largest vertically integrated cable maker (rod mills to installation vessels).
  • High-voltage subsea. Number three of the European triopoly. Prysmian holds an estimated 40 percent of global high-voltage cable and NKT about 25 percent; by backlog, Prysmian about €18bn to €20bn, NKT €13.0bn and Nexans €7.8bn. Nexans is estimated to have lost 3 to 4 percentage points of European subsea share in recent years, and it is adding the least capacity of the three.
  • Grid (medium-voltage distribution). A leading European position (Enedis, Nordic and Southern European utilities; North American utility frameworks), plausibly number one or two in several national markets; capacity-constrained.
  • Connect (low voltage). A significant European player (La Triveneta made it a leader in Italy); dominant in the Western Canada utility market (former Anixter director); about 4 percent of US building wire against Prysmian and Southwire at about 60 to 65 percent combined.
  • Distance from number one or two: in subsea, half NKT's backlog and about 40 percent of Prysmian's; in the United States, a distant third-tier player; in European Grid, at or near the top.
C-25

How concentrated is the market: do the top five hold more than 30 percent, and how steep is the share distribution (for example, do the top five hold five times the share of numbers six to ten)?

C+7610th of 35

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. Concentration is extreme in high voltage, high in US low voltage and moderate in European low and medium voltage. The share curve is steepest exactly where margins are highest, which is the structural reason the industry earns its current returns.

  • High-voltage subsea and extra-high-voltage systems. Prysmian, Nexans and NKT hold over 80 percent of European subsea transmission (about 75 percent globally); outside China only five or six companies can both manufacture and install extra-high-voltage cable; below them the curve drops to near zero (entrants without vessels). The top five hold effectively the whole Western market, far above the 30 percent test, and the top five dwarf numbers six to ten.
  • US low voltage. The top two hold about 60 to 65 percent, the top three about 70 percent after Prysmian's Encore Wire deal.
  • European low and medium voltage. The first three to five competitors typically hold more than 50 percent of each major national market (former Nexans M&A director), but the European market as a whole is fragmented, distribution is concentrated (Rexel, Sonepar, Wesco) and customers can switch suppliers easily.
  • Global cables and wires (about €116bn in 2023, rising to about €157bn by 2028) are fragmented.
C-26

How stable are market shares over time, and what share have new entrants taken over the last three to five years?

F4027th of 35

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. Shares at the top of the high-voltage market have been stable for a decade, but for the first time they are drifting, and the drift is away from Nexans. Entrants' share of Western subsea is still small, but the barrier that kept it near zero (qualification at 525 kV plus vessel access) has now been crossed by at least two entrants.

  • Incumbent drift. An estimated 3 to 4 percentage points of European subsea share moved from Nexans to NKT, Asian suppliers and others; NKT's backlog grew from €4.7bn (late 2022) to €13.0bn while Nexans's grew to €7.8bn.
  • Entrants, last three to five years: Korean makers (LS Cable, Taihan) took share in North American land high voltage by undercutting; LS Cable has now won European 525 kV subsea awards (TenneT's BalWin4 and LanWin1) and is building a UK plant (2027), a US plant (2028) and a vessel (H1 2028); Hellenic Cables doubled Corinth subsea capacity, qualified at 525 kV (July 2026) and won €2.65bn of Greek awards; Sumitomo opened Nigg (September 2026) and owns Südkabel. In Western subsea their share of current deliveries is still low, but their share of awards for post-2029 delivery is rising.
  • Low voltage: stable shares in Europe; consolidation in the United States.

For the model. Share stability can be assumed for deliveries through 2028 (the backlog); for awards delivering after 2029, assume Nexans holds or slightly loses share, which caps Transmission volume growth beyond the current capacity.

C-27

To what extent does market share consolidate over time towards the largest competitors, and towards the most advantaged ones (not necessarily the largest or most established), and is this company among them?

F4028th of 35

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. In high voltage, share consolidates towards whoever has qualified capacity and vessels ready when transmission operators award, which in this cycle meant Prysmian and NKT, who invested earlier and larger. In US low voltage it consolidates towards the vertically integrated duopoly. In European medium voltage it is drifting towards those adding capacity and accessories, where Nexans is among the advantaged. Nexans is therefore among the gainers in Grid only.

  • High voltage rewards capacity commitment ahead of awards: NKT's €2bn programme (Karlskrona, Cologne, Eleonora) and Prysmian's fleet and towers converted directly into backlog. Nexans doubled Halden in 2024, added Electra and Charleroi, but chose not to build speculative subsea capacity, consistent with value over volume and with its 2012 to 2018 experience (about €1bn of capex with negligible financial impact).
  • US low voltage: local rod mills and scale (Prysmian, Southwire) set cost leadership; Nexans's Canadian rod is tariff-blocked.
  • European medium voltage: frameworks are lengthening and favour suppliers with capacity, accessories and low-carbon credentials, where Nexans won (Enedis doubling volumes; Alliander went to NKT and Hellenic).
C-28

What is competitors' attitude to competition: do they put margin and return on capital first, or volume first; are they benign and cooperative, or willing to suffer losses; and do industry prices fall when volume falls?

C6614th of 35

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. Today the attitude of all three European leaders is explicitly margin-first and benign, enforced by sold-out capacity: they take orders selectively, avoid spot pricing, demand down-payments and keep backlogs flat by choice. History says this attitude is conditional: in the 2015 to 2020 capacity surplus, high-voltage prices fell with volume and margins went below 5 percent at the trough, with losses at NKT in 2019 to 2020 and profit warnings at Nexans (2013, 2018) and Prysmian (Western Link, 2018).

  • Prysmian: "We don't expand capacity beyond what comes from the demand secured through down payment"; "the party is over... we wanted to confirm our leadership"; backlog deliberately held at €14bn to €16bn in earlier statements.
  • NKT: demands "confirmed volumes" before organic expansion; "not disappointed" to lose share on the wrong projects.
  • Nexans: "We prefer to have 1.4 percent organic growth with good improvement in our margin... than dilute ourselves" (Guérin); "extremely selective... we don't want to consume cash for projects which are not accretive" (Hueber).
  • Entrants are the exception: LS Cable has bid low in US land high voltage; entrants need reference projects and have an incentive to price for share.
  • Do prices fall when volume falls? In high voltage today, no, because volume is allocated rather than fought over; in the last surplus, yes. In low voltage, prices follow metal and distributor stock cycles, and European low-voltage pricing has softened in the construction trough.
C-29

How stable is that attitude, and how has it changed over the last eighteen months to three years: do competitors show 'Michael O'Leary' instability (hard to predict, situation-dependent, reactive), and would they launch a 'shark attack' on industry pricing if they sensed weakness?

D5021st of 35

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

The Investment Analyst's heading How stable is that attitude, and how has it changed over the last eighteen months to three years; are competitors 'Michael O'Leary' unstable, and would they launch a 'shark attack' on industry pricing if they sensed weakness?

Judgement. The attitude has become more disciplined over the last three years (longer frameworks, down-payments, capacity reservation fees, prices per kilometre doubled) and is stable for as long as capacity is short. The instability risk is not among the three incumbents but at the edges: entrants needing references, and NKT's large new capacity, which must be filled from 2027 to 2028 for deliveries after 2030. A shark attack is plausible in two places: US low voltage, and post-2029 high-voltage slots.

  • Last 18 months to three years: utilities moved from two-year to six-and-seven-year frames; slot reservations became standard; all three leaders' Transmission margins rose in H1 2026.
  • Where the attitude could break: NKT's Karlskrona capacity (commercial from Q2 2027, full volume 2028) must find awards beyond its €13.0bn backlog; Morgan Stanley's model shows supply reaching parity with demand around 2030 and about 15 percent oversupply if European offshore wind runs at 13 GW a year; Asian entrants gain about 9 points of share in that model. The first evidence of how the next price round clears will be the 525 kV awards now being contested.
  • US low voltage: Prysmian and Southwire have the means (local rod mills, scale) and the motive (defending a profit pool) to undercut a new entrant; Prysmian's own risk work rates US low-voltage margin compression its single most probable failure at 60 percent. Republic Wire is the obvious target.
  • Behavioural unpredictability ("Michael O'Leary" instability): not shown by any incumbent; the industry's conduct has been predictable for three years.
C-31

Taken together, how supportive is the industry structure, and how fragile is that support?

C-6016th of 35

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. The structure is strongly supportive today in high voltage and medium voltage (concentrated, capacity-constrained, disciplined, with pricing power moved to the maker) and weakly supportive in low voltage. The support is fragile in a dated way: it rests on scarcity, and scarcity erodes as incumbents' and entrants' capacity lands in 2027 to 2030. The fragility is greater for Nexans than for its peers, for three reasons: its backlog is shorter (it needs awards in the less favourable window sooner), its distinctive line uses a technology the market is moving away from, and its US entry sits inside someone else's oligopoly.

  • Supportive through 2028: the backlog prices are fixed, indexed for metals and mostly protected by down-payments; frameworks lock in Grid volumes; capacity is booked.
  • Fragile from 2029: award prices for post-2030 delivery will be set with more qualified bidders; transmission operators are beginning to phase projects on cost; the German overhead-line pivot cuts underground demand.
  • Mitigant: Nexans is the least dependent of the three on high voltage (about 20 to 25 percent of EBITDA from high voltage, against 75 to 80 percent at NKT), so normalisation after 2029 hurts its group earnings less.
C-35To what extent are new entrants (from adjacent markets; standard entrants; orthogonal or innovative entrants) entering or threatening this market, and how much of a threat is each type?F3830th of 35full answer under 1a External headwinds/threats
C-36

To what extent are substitutes or alternatives (other ways for customers to solve the problems the company's products solve) a threat, and how is that changing?

D5022nd of 35

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. Substitutes are a low near-term threat that grew slightly in 2026, mostly through policy rather than technology.

  • Overhead lines instead of underground cable: Germany's 2026 pivot for unawarded direct-current corridors (see C-03), and transmission operators' cost focus elsewhere.
  • Getting more from existing lines: reconductoring with high-temperature low-sag conductors and grid-enhancing technologies (31 US projects selected in September 2026 with over 1,500 miles of reconductoring); Nexans sells conductors and monitoring, so this partly offsets.
  • Less copper per data-centre megawatt: 800-volt direct-current busways replacing busbars; superconducting cable carries up to ten times the power with about 19 percent copper content against 68 percent for conventional cable; Nexans participates in both.
  • Bypassing the grid: behind-the-meter generation and microgrids for data centres (Morgan Stanley) reduce distribution-cable demand at the margin.
  • Aluminium for copper: accelerating in overhead lines at a copper-to-aluminium price ratio near 4.0; Nexans makes both.

Strong business SRCA and BTE; management; financial profile

4a

Business quality

D5518th of 358 answers
Judgement55C-4154C-4254C-4366C-4450C-4560C-4625C-4758
Group judgement

Company - Competitive advantage

D5518th of 35

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

Nexans has a genuine but shared competitive position: it owns the assets that define the high-voltage tier (vessels, qualification, integration from rod to installation) and a strong European Grid franchise, and it has proved real pricing power in both. What it lacks is advantage over its two closest rivals: it is smaller than Prysmian, slower than NKT in adding capacity, behind both in margin conversion, and a minor player in US low voltage. Its value-over-volume model maximises returns on what it has but cedes share and invites entrants. The improvement ahead is largely mechanical (contract vintage, Electra, Grid capacity, synergies), which makes the next three years' margin path more predictable than heroic, but it does not close the gap to peers. For the model: margins rise on mechanics through 2028; no share gains in subsea; Grid as the segment where advantage is widening.

C-41

Which sustainable relative competitive advantages or moats does the company hold (customer-related; production-related; scale-based; regulatory or political; culture or knowledge), how strong is each, and are they widening or narrowing?

D5422nd of 35

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement. Nexans holds real hard advantages in high voltage and medium voltage, but they are advantages it shares with two or three peers rather than advantages over them; its soft advantages (the SHIFT selectivity discipline, project culture) were built by a team that has largely left. The moat is widening in Grid, stable to widening in high-voltage capability, narrowing in high-voltage share and scale relative to Prysmian and NKT, and largely absent in low voltage.

  • Customer-related (qualification, track record, frameworks): strong in Transmission, moderate in Grid, weak in Connect; stable to widening. Transmission operators prioritise decades of delivery record over price; switching supplier mid-project is practically impossible before 2028 for lack of alternative extrusion capacity; 525 kV production references (LanWin2) extend the lock-in. Grid frameworks of six to seven years and embedded distributor inventory systems add moderate stickiness. In Connect, contractors substitute suppliers freely.
  • Production-related (vessels, extrusion lines, rod mills, recycling): strong in subsea, widening in capability, narrowing in relative capacity. Three cable-laying vessels (Electra, 13,500 tonnes, booked to end-2028), the mass-impregnated line at Halden (a scarce capability whose technology is slowly being displaced), 525 kV extruded capability, four rod mills processing up to about 600,000 tonnes of wire rod (a cost and security edge NKT and Cenergy lack), Lens recycling. Relative to peers, though, Prysmian runs six vessels after its programme and NKT's Karlskrona hub will be the largest high-voltage offshore cable site in the world.
  • Scale: moderate and narrowing against Prysmian. Nexans is about one-third of Prysmian's size; Prysmian consumes 2 to 3 percent of global copper, buys from 7,250 Tier 1 suppliers and secures procurement discounts on "double or triple the material volume" (Prysmian strategy director). In the United States Nexans has no scale at all.
  • Regulatory and political: moderate and widening. Security exclusion of Chinese suppliers from Western subsea; the Net-Zero Industry Act's resilience criteria; US local-content rules favouring Charleston (the only operating US high-voltage subsea plant) and Republic Wire; low-carbon credentials (Hydro aluminium) that win tenders.
  • Culture and knowledge: real but at risk. SHIFT (about €300m of structural EBITDA from 2018 to 2024; customer base cut from 20,000 to 4,000 accounts; Reka's EBITDA doubled in two years) is a proprietary operating discipline; it depends on the people who run it, and the chief executive, finance director and several divisional heads changed in 2025 to 2026.

For the model. The moats justify durable returns above the cost of capital and protection of the backlog margins through 2028; they do not justify convergence to Prysmian's or NKT's Transmission margins, nor share gains.

C-42

How large is the company's scale relative to competitors and other actors (revenue, headcount and so on), and what does that scale confer?

C5415th of 35

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement. Nexans has enough scale to be one of the five or six companies that can make and lay extra-high-voltage cable, and enough to carry project guarantees and fund a capacity programme, but not enough to lead on cost or capacity in any segment except perhaps European Grid. Scale confers tier membership, not tier leadership.

  • Size: about €6.1bn of standard sales, €7.8bn at current metal prices, about 12,800 employees in 39 countries on the continuing basis (about 25,700 including the businesses since sold). Prysmian: €19.7bn of revenue, about 34,000 employees, 107 to 109 plants, 27 to 30 research centres. NKT: €2.7bn of standard revenue, about 6,600 employees. Cenergy: €2.1bn. Siemens Energy (grid equipment comparator): €39.1bn.
  • What it confers: qualification and balance-sheet capacity for multi-billion turnkey projects; procurement security through internal rod mills (about half of copper needs); a fleet that removes charter costs; the capacity to fund €1.2bn of capex and a €680m acquisition while staying around 1.4x leverage; breadth for cross-selling (cable plus accessories plus installation plus services).
  • What it does not confer: procurement leadership against Prysmian; the capacity to build ahead of awards on NKT's scale; cost leadership in US low voltage against integrated incumbents.
  • The customer side of scale: no customer above 10 percent of sales (Sonepar about 7 percent of receivables); concentration risk is low.

C-44

How high is the company's surplus customer utility against competing or alternative offerings, and is it leading, or at least demonstrably not inferior? (Hard to prove, easier to disprove; the aim is to disprove inferiority at Early-Stage and Deep Dive, for example Sophos, Bytes.)

F5030th of 35

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement. Nexans's offering is demonstrably not inferior in high-voltage systems and European Grid, and has signs of leadership in installation capability and low-carbon content; it is not demonstrably non-inferior in US low voltage, and it is inferior in the combined energy-and-data offer that data-centre customers increasingly buy.

  • Evidence of non-inferiority or leadership: NKT called the Aurora vessel launch "a material escalation in offshore installation competitiveness"; Electra exceeds Aurora's capacity by about 35 percent; the Malta-Sicily cable was manufactured within 13 months of signing; contractors call Nexans's European Grid connections "better products"; Enedis chose Nexans on security of supply, carbon and circularity; an offshore wind director identifies Nexans as differentiated in floating wind and deep-water applications; Nexans moved 525 kV into production for TenneT.
  • Evidence against: no major utility publicly cited Nexans as a standout partner in the last twelve months; a medium-voltage contractor reported quoting struggles through traditional channels; a former Nexans director says it is uncompetitive on price and delivery in commoditised US utility cable; data-centre buyers favour Prysmian's broader product stack (fibre plus energy), and Nexans has no optical fibre; customers can split integrated projects and award components separately (Prysmian transmission expert), which limits the value of a one-stop offer.
C-45

To what extent does the company have, and use, demonstrable pricing power with minimal erosion of its relative surplus customer utility, shown by evidence of application rather than conjecture?

B608th of 35

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement. Pricing power is demonstrated by application in two of three segments, but Nexans has used it less than its peers in the segment where it is greatest. In Transmission the industry's price per kilometre has doubled and Nexans's margin is rising, yet at 13.7 percent it is 650 to 750 basis points below Prysmian and NKT on the same price regime; in Grid pricing power is real and indexed; in Connect it is absent.

  • Transmission: industry 525 kV contract values per kilometre doubled (about €0.62m to €1.44m between 2019 to 2020 and 2024 to 2026 awards); down-payments of 10 to 15 percent at award; full metal indexation with no lag on major contracts; Nexans's margin +195 basis points to 13.7 percent on flat sales in H1 2026 as dilutive legacy frameworks rolled off; management guides to 17 percent by 2028. The gap to peers reflects older, lower-priced contracts still in execution, under-absorption at Halden after the Great Sea Interconnector stopped, and charter costs that Electra now removes, so part of the gap closes mechanically.
  • Grid: prices fully indexed to inputs; demand above capacity; the margin rose from 6.7 percent (2021) to the mid-teens; customers accept earlier commitments for capacity certainty.
  • Connect: margin down 180 basis points in a year; non-metal input inflation not passed through quickly.
  • Erosion of customer utility: minimal so far in Transmission and Grid (customers keep committing), but entrants winning awards and transmission operators phasing on cost are early signs that the price ceiling has been reached for new awards.
C-46

To what extent does the company share its scale economies with customers, stated or de facto, in its messaging, strategy, culture or values?

F2529th of 35

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement. It does not; Nexans's model is the opposite, value over volume. It maximises margin per customer and per unit of capital, culls low-value accounts and refuses volume that dilutes margin. That has been right for returns, and it has a cost the investment case must carry: it cedes share in a growing market (3 to 4 points of European subsea share lost; Transmission volume flat while peers grow double digits) and leaves room for entrants who will price for references.

  • Evidence: customer base cut from 20,000 to 4,000 "platinum" accounts; "we prefer 1.4 percent organic growth with good improvement in our margin... than dilute ourselves"; selective high-voltage order-taking; an organic growth target of 3 to 5 percent in a market growing faster.
  • No messaging, pricing policy or value statement suggests passing scale gains to customers; the E3 model (economics, environment, engagement) is about the company's own conduct.
C-47

To what extent do gravitational or mechanical forces, rather than heroic company dynamism, improve the company's competitive advantage and its financial outputs against peers with each period that passes?

C+588th of 35

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement. Some mechanical forces work for Nexans over the next two to three years, and they are why its margins should rise even without heroics: the roll-off of low-priced legacy Transmission contracts, Electra replacing chartered vessels, Grid frameworks and capacity filling, and Republic Wire's synergies. But the gravitational forces that compound advantage relative to peers (capacity commitment, backlog depth, procurement scale) favour Prysmian and NKT, so the mechanical improvement is absolute rather than relative.

  • Working for Nexans: contract vintage (higher-priced 2023 to 2026 awards replacing older ones through 2028, the sector's "contracted margin" mechanism); Electra's charter saving and scheduling control; accessories attach rates rising as capacity lands; recycling lowering metal cost and carbon; copper scarcity favouring the integrated rod-mill owner.
  • Working against, relative to peers: NKT's and Prysmian's larger, newer capacity converts more of the same price regime into backlog; Prysmian's scale and integration in the United States; entrants gaining references each year.
  • Financial outputs since 2018 compounded through selection and restructuring (heroic, management-driven) more than through mechanical ratchets; the next phase relies more on mechanics.

C-55

How big is the company's ambition or vision (a force: growth as a priority, outcompeting, innovating, adapting, pushing), particularly in relation to peers?

D4425th of 35

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Judgement. The ambition is real but measured and margin-led, and it is the smallest of the European cable leaders in capacity and growth terms. Hueber's stated move "from execution to expansion" raises the ambition on acquisitions and Grid capacity, not on high-voltage share.

  • Nexans: Electrification organic growth of 3 to 5 percent a year (2024 to 2028), adjusted EBITDA of €1,150m by 2028 (including acquisitions), Transmission margin to 17 percent, a data-centre vertical, North America to about €1bn of sales, a "rich" M&A pipeline.

C-56

How great and smart is the strategy, with right and bold strategic priorities (an enabler): widening the moat over the mid term, growth as a priority, and a dynamic strategy that can capitalise on a plausible range of environments?

F5229th of 35

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Judgement. The strategy is smart and robust (it would hold up well in a downturn) but not bold, and it widens the moat only in Grid. Its central trade-off, protecting returns rather than chasing high-voltage capacity, is defensible given the post-2029 supply outlook, but it concedes the segment with the highest margins to faster-moving peers and leaves the company more dependent on acquisitions for growth.

  • Right: the pure-play focus; selectivity; the Grid and accessories capacity step into law-dated distribution demand; Electra (owning installation capacity); recycling and low-carbon supply as a procurement edge; North America for a protected, growing market.
  • Less right or unproven: paying 10.3x for a US low-voltage entrant against an integrated duopoly at a possible peak in US building-wire margins; concentrating Halden on mass-impregnated technology the market is slowly leaving; not matching peers' extruded high-voltage capacity build.
  • Dynamic across environments: the balance of three engines and a modest capex profile make the strategy resilient to a high-voltage normalisation after 2029 (the bear case for NKT); it is less well placed to capture an extended scarcity window.
C-57

How great is the chief executive, judged by actions rather than conjecture (an enabler, not an ossifier)?

D5621st of 35

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Judgement. Julien Hueber looks like a capable, disciplined operator and an enabler, on one year of evidence: he kept the strategy, rebuilt the executive committee within three months, protected guidance through the Great Sea Interconnector loss, found a replacement order at preferred-bidder stage within a quarter, closed and began integrating Republic Wire, and hired a Chief Operating Officer. His best evidence is his old division's record; his weakest is that he has not yet faced a downcycle or a US integration.

  • Actions: Grid and Connect Europe margin from 6.7 percent (2021) to 16.4 percent (2025); APAC stabilised through 2018 to 2021 by exiting unprofitable lines; a January 2026 executive committee reorganisation with heads for data centres and large projects; insistence on selectivity ("we don't want to consume cash for projects which are not accretive"); H1 2026 beat by 7 percent with guidance raised; Q3 Grid growth of 14.8 percent.
  • Against or untested: the replacement order is still not signed three months after preferred-bidder status; the July raise was acquisition-led; Republic Wire's first-quarter scope fell short; the nine-month release withheld profit data; he refuses comment on the Greek government's role in the Great Sea Interconnector; his 2026 fixed pay (€750k) is below the predecessor's, with a heavy variable and equity component.

C-58

How well resourced and invested is the whole business (a buffer; an ant colony: built yesterday for resilience)?

C+6210th of 35

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Judgement. Well resourced and investing ahead of demand in the segments where it chooses to grow, with a sensible balance between capacity and balance sheet; the deliberate exception is high-voltage subsea capacity.

  • Capex of €383m in 2025 (6.3 percent of standard sales) and €191m in H1 2026 (5.9 percent); Electra (€380m, delivered on time and budget); about 40 percent more European Grid capacity by 2028; €62m for accessories; about €90m for Charleroi (in service mid-2027); a new medium-voltage plant in Morocco; Lens recycling (about €90m, from 2027); Republic Wire's 30 percent expansion.

  • Balance sheet: leverage 1.4x, fixed-rate debt, BB+; the EIB lent €250m for recycling and research.
  • Gap: the subsea capacity peers are adding; experts describe Nexans as "trailing competitors in capacity expansion".

C-63

Explain, with the evidence, the extent to which the company puts shareholders first, ahead of management, and how prudent its governance is (scaffolding put in place even where it was not needed): what does the evidence show, and why?

D4620th of 35

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Judgement. The structure is shareholder-friendly and prudent; the behaviour around the 2025 leadership change and the capital-allocation stance are less so. The evidence shows a company whose scaffolding (voting rights, board independence, compliance, incentive design) was built carefully, while discretionary decisions have tended to favour management continuity and growth over returns of capital.

Shareholders first and prudent scaffolding:

  • One share one vote since 2011; double voting rights rejected by shareholders in 2014 despite the Florange Act; a 20 percent cap on voting rights at extraordinary meetings.
  • A board 80 percent independent after the 2026 annual meeting, with an all-independent audit committee and a designated climate director; 94.4 percent attendance.
  • Long-term incentives with zero vesting if total shareholder return lags the Eurostoxx 600 industrials; a 45 percent cash-conversion floor; a chief executive shareholding requirement (25 percent of vested shares, at least 15,000 shares); anti-hedging undertakings.
  • Severance capped at two years' pay and conditional on 80 percent average achievement.
  • Compliance: whistleblowing in 17 languages, 100 percent compliance training completion, tender-risk procedures (scaffolding put in after the 2014 €70.6m cartel fine).

Management or growth ahead of shareholders:

  • No clawback provisions; the presence condition waived on 31,794 of the departing chief executive's performance shares, plus a €2.72m termination indemnity and €1.4m non-compete payment.
  • Pre-departure share sales of €781k by the outgoing chief executive and finance director, unexplained.
  • Acquisitions at 10.3x EBITDA preferred to buybacks with the stock at about 7x; buybacks limited to anti-dilution.
  • Auditor tenure of 20 and 11 years; finance director pay not individually disclosed.

Why: the board's choices consistently favour continuity and the growth plan, which management's incentives (EBITDA and cash conversion targets for 2028) also favour; capital return is treated as residual.

C-64

To what extent is the company an enlightened capital allocator and a dynamic repurchaser of its shares, not just an operator or generator, with a record of allocating capital at a high return on new invested capital?

D4426th of 35

The Investment Analyst's heading To what extent is the company an enlightened capital allocator and a dynamic repurchaser of its shares, with a record of allocating capital at a high return on new invested capital?

Nexans has been an enlightened allocator on the perimeter and organic capex, with a high-return record, but it is not a dynamic repurchaser, and its most recent large allocation (Republic Wire at 10.3x while its own shares trade at about 7x enterprise value to EBITDA and at the 5th percentile of their five-year price-to-earnings range) runs the multiple arbitrage the wrong way.

  • High-return record: ROCE from about 10 to 21.3 percent between 2020 and 2025 while capex rose; La Triveneta bought at 4.6x post-synergy EBITDA; disposals of AmerCable ($280m) and Lynxeo (€525m) at attractive prices; Electra delivered on budget.
  • Weaker: Autoelectric sold at about 3.8x EBITDA; Republic Wire at 10.3x (about 10 percent pre-tax return at entry); stated preference for further M&A.
  • Repurchases: formulaic, anti-dilutive (176,005 shares in July 2026; €24m to €48m a year of buybacks over FY2024 to H1 2026 against share-plan issuance); no opportunistic programme; the dividend is progressive (payout at least 30 percent).

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

Nexans is now a good, not great, industrial: returns of about 20 percent on capital employed in a normal year, cash conversion that is good over a cycle but tied to project down-payments, and margins that have doubled in five years yet still sit below every cable peer. Its earnings growth for 2027 to 2028 is reasonably well underpinned, roughly 13 percent a year in EBITDA and about 20 percent a year in earnings per share from the FY2026 trough, but that growth comes from margin and from reversing below-EBITDA drags, not from revenue, and the earnings base carries noise (metal revaluation, reorganisation exclusions, capitalised integration costs, a brokers' basis above net income per share) that the model must strip out. Operating leverage is moderate except at Halden; financial leverage is comfortable; the cuttable cost pool is small. The largest single lever is Transmission margin conversion, which is mechanical through 2028 if the plants are loaded. For the model: EBITDA about €810m, €910m to €920m and €1,020m to €1,040m for FY2026 to FY2028; earnings per share on a net-income basis about €6.95, €8.45 and €10.10; free cash flow about €270m, €380m to €420m and €450m to €500m.

C-50

How much immediate scope is there to deploy capital or operating spend at a high return on new invested capital for three to five years, and what does that mean for mid- and long-term potential and the fair multiple?

D5419th of 35

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement. Nexans has a real but bounded reinvestment runway: about 5 to 6 percent of sales a year in capex at high returns (Grid capacity, accessories, recycling, 525 kV land capacity) plus acquisitions at lower returns. That is well below the 20 percent of sales that would justify a compounder's multiple, and it argues for a fair multiple around the stock's own long-run median rather than a premium.

  • Organic: €1.2bn of capex planned for 2025 to 2028 (€383m in 2025, €191m in H1 2026), plus €62m for accessories, about €90m for Charleroi, the Morocco site and the €90m Lens plant. Returns on this organic capital have been high: ROCE doubled from about 10 percent (2020) to 21.3 percent (2025) while capex rose; core Electrification ROCE was 26.3 percent in 2024; Grid capacity goes into a market where demand exceeds supply and lead times can fall by up to 60 percent.
  • Inorganic: firepower of "more than €1.2bn" to "over €2bn"; returns at entry well below the organic record (Republic Wire about 10 percent pre-tax, about 13 percent with synergies; La Triveneta about 20 percent post-synergy at 4.6x).
  • Not available: large high-return subsea capacity additions without customer pre-funding, given the post-2029 supply outlook.
  • Implication for the multiple: the stock's ten-year median 24-month-forward price-to-earnings ratio of 12.3x is a reasonable anchor; a premium needs either a longer runway or proof that Transmission returns stay high after 2029.

Target above 20 percent by 2028. Post-tax, at a 30 percent tax rate, about 15 percent in 2025 and about 10 to 11 percent at the H1 2026 run-rate.

C-54

How clean is the earnings base: how large is the gap between adjusted and reported earnings; do one-offs, capitalised costs or other items flatter it; and does the tax rate or any other non-obvious leakage erode earnings per share?

C-76

How material is the financial leverage or gearing: how much does it amplify earnings per share and risk, and how comfortable is it?

Financial leverage is modest and comfortable, and it amplifies earnings per share only slightly; its real effect is to remove the balance-sheet optionality the company had in 2025 and to add about €30m to €45m a year of interest cost that turns EBITDA growth into an earnings fall in FY2026.

  • Earnings sensitivity: each 100 basis points on floating debt is small (the debt is fixed); each €500m of additional acquisition debt at about 4.5 percent costs about €0.35 of earnings per share before the acquired EBITDA.

Pricing power

P-04

After assessing the pricing power in greater detail, how is price likely to contribute to revenue and margin over the next 18 months to four years?

A543rd of 35

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement. Price contributes very differently by segment, and its total contribution to organic growth falls from about 1.5 to 2.5 points a year in FY2026 and FY2027 to about 0.5 to 1 point by FY2029 and FY2030. Its margin contribution is concentrated in Transmission and is already "in the bank": about €60m to €75m of the €94m rise in Transmission EBITDA between FY2025 and FY2028 comes from executing contracts priced in 2023 to 2026 in place of legacy contracts, with Electra's removal of charter costs adding most of the rest; it does not depend on any new price increase. After 2028 the price contribution in Transmission turns flat to slightly negative, because the next awards (above all a new order for Halden's mass-impregnated line after mid-2028) will clear against newly qualified bidders, which is why my Transmission margin stops rising at about 16.5 percent. In Grid, price is real but modest (about 1 to 2 percent a year, indexed for metals, plus accessories mix worth about 50 basis points of margin by 2028). In Connect, price runs at or below cost: unindexed polymers, compounds and energy reach costs before price lists move, which I put at a net €15m to €25m headwind in FY2027; and Republic Wire's US prices sit at a tariff-supported peak with more downside than upside. Separately and importantly for reading the revenue line, metal price is passed through: copper at a record raises current-metal revenue by about 17 percentage points in FY2026 (from 9.4 percent growth in standard sales to 26 percent at current metal prices) with no profit effect.

Transmission: real price, already contracted, converting mechanically.

  • The price regime: 525 kV direct-current contract value per kilometre roughly doubled from about €0.62m (2019 to 2020 awards) to €1.44m (2024 to 2026 awards) at NKT (DNB Carnegie, 6 August 2026); about half of the increase is broader turnkey scope and half is margin (DNB Carnegie, 1 September 2026).
  • Why Nexans trails at 13.7 percent (H1 2026) against Prysmian's 21.2 percent and NKT's 20.2 percent: legacy, inflation-exposed framework contracts still in execution (exiting through late 2026), under-absorption at Halden after the Great Sea Interconnector stopped, chartered-vessel costs that Electra now removes, and mix (more land and US work). The first three close mechanically; the fourth does not.
  • My path closes about half of the gap: from about 750 basis points (H1 2026) to about 450 basis points by FY2028 (16.6 percent against peers' current 20 to 21 percent). The residual reflects utilisation risk on one technology line, smaller scale and mix; I see no evidence that Nexans's newest contracts are priced below peers', but no disclosure proves they are priced at peers' levels either.
  • Contract terms protect the backlog margin: full metal indexation with no lag on major contracts, down-payments of 10 to 15 percent at award, non-refundable reservation fees and cost recovery on cancellation; the Great Sea Interconnector case showed it (IPTO had paid €251.4m of milestones by April 2025, and the manufactured cable belongs to the customer and is paid for). The risk to the backlog margin is execution (liquidated damages), not price.
  • After 2028: Hellenic Cables (525 kV qualified July 2026; €1.5bn Corinth-Kos and €1.15bn island framework awards from IPTO, Nexans's Great Sea Interconnector customer), LS Cable (TenneT 525 kV awards; a "price-competitive strategy"), Sumitomo (Nigg) and Taihan are bidding; Morgan Stanley dates supply-demand parity to about 2030. Mass-impregnated technology is slowly being displaced by extruded cable, and only Prysmian and Nexans make it for deep water, so the next mass-impregnated award is likely to clear at a lower price than the current order but above extruded commodity work. I hold the Transmission margin flat at 16.5 to 16.3 percent for FY2029 and FY2030.

Grid: modest real price, mix-led margin.

  • Prices are indexed for metals; non-metal inputs (45 to 50 percent of medium-voltage variable cost at peers, per a former Prysmian vice president) are recovered through framework renegotiation with a lag. Utilities are paying for certainty (earlier commitments, longer frames, lead times above 12 to 14 months), and Enedis selected Nexans partly on carbon and circularity.
  • Accessories earn about twice the margin of base cable and grew 34 percent in 2024, adding about a point to the Grid margin; the 39 percent capacity step continues that. I credit about 50 basis points of Grid margin to mix by FY2028, against about 40 basis points of start-up and input-cost drag in FY2026 and FY2027.
  • Revenue contribution: about 1 to 2 points of Grid's 8 to 9 percent growth in FY2026 to FY2028; the rest is volume.

Connect: no real price; input costs ahead of price.

  • The Connect margin fell from 13.6 to 11.8 percent between H1 2025 and H1 2026 (Nordic weakness, dilutive regional mix, Australian pricing pressure, La Triveneta integration). European low-voltage pricing is "structurally vulnerable to normalisation" (a former Prysmian finance director); distributors (Rexel, Sonepar) price weekly and push for volume discounts in a downturn.
  • The precedent for unindexed inputs: in 2010 Nexans recovered only about 70 percent of a 25 percent polymer price rise, at a cost of about €70m of EBITDA; in H1 2018 a one-to-twelve-month lag cost about €10m. With Brent near $111 and polymer shortages after Hormuz, I carry about €12m to €15m of net headwind in Connect and about €5m to €8m in Grid in FY2027, recovered in FY2028.
  • United States: Section 232 (50 percent on imported copper wire and cable) lifted the US copper wire producer price index 27 percent year on year (Jefferies, 7 April 2026); Southwire and Prysmian hold about 60 to 65 percent of building wire and set price. Atkore's margin halved from about 30 to 15 percent when supply normalised; Prysmian's own risk work puts a 60 percent probability on US low-voltage margin compression. Republic Wire's price is therefore a downside risk rather than a growth driver; I hold its EBITDA margin flat on revenue, with synergies as the only margin gain.

Metal: price in revenue, not in profit.

  • With copper and aluminium held at about the second-half 2026 level, revenue at current metal prices is 1.50 times standard sales from H2 2026 (1.46 times in H1 2026, 1.28 times in FY2025). A 10 percent move in copper moves current-metal revenue by about 4 to 5 percent, working capital in the same direction, and reported (not adjusted) profit through the Core exposure revaluation; about 80 percent of direct costs are passed through or hedged and 83 to 85 percent of sales are indexed.
