Group judgement

Financial forecasts

D5417th of 35

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The financial forecasts describe a better company than the share price implies but a less exciting one than consensus EPS implies. Operationally the path is credible and mostly mechanical: EBITDA rises by about €300m between FY2025 and FY2028 from three identifiable sources (Transmission conversion, Republic Wire and Grid capacity), and my EBITDA sits within 1 to 4 percent of consensus. Below EBITDA, the picture is weaker than consensus and the first set: depreciation, interest and a 30 percent tax rate make FY2026 a flat earnings year on a like-for-like basis, the brokers' EPS basis sits about 9 percent above adjusted net income per diluted share, and free cash flow converts only 28 to 41 percent of EBITDA because capital spending, leases and reorganisation cash stay high while down-payments no longer help. The growth is front-loaded and concentrated: FY2027 and FY2028 carry most of it, and 61 percent of the FY2025 to FY2028 EBITDA increase rests on two items each with one dated binary risk (the replacement order; US pricing). After FY2028 there is no large driver inside the current perimeter, so the medium-term EPS path depends on how management uses a balance sheet that deleverages to about 0.5 times by end-2028; its stated preference for acquisitions over buybacks is the most consequential forecast choice the company has left, and the arithmetic favours buybacks at today's price. For the third set, the predictability is good for FY2026, fair for FY2027 (one unsigned order, one new team, one input-cost step) and lower beyond; for the fourth set, the expectation gap is small at EBITDA, larger at EPS (mostly basis and tax) and largest at free cash flow, and the FY2027 guide on 24 February 2027 is the event most likely to move consensus, probably downward by a few percent.

After assessing below-operating-profit cost items (e.g. tax, interest, one-offs, minority investments, etc.) and share buybacks or issuance in greater detail, how is it likely to shape earnings per share, net income and free cash flow over the next 18 months to four years, versus the last four to six years?

F3427th of 35

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement. Below EBITDA, the next four years reverse the pattern of the last five. From FY2020 to FY2025 the items below EBITDA were small and stable (D&A €154m to €178m until 2024, net interest €22m to €59m, tax about 24 to 31 percent after 2021, a flat share count), so earnings tracked EBITDA. FY2026 is the trough of drop-through: EBITDA rises about €77m, but D&A (+€41m excluding acquired intangibles), net financial items (+€40m to €47m) and a higher tax rate absorb it, and adjusted net income is flat at about €287m. From FY2027 the items stabilise and then shrink relative to EBITDA, so adjusted net income grows faster than EBITDA (+26 percent, +19 percent, +9 percent and +7 percent in FY2027 to FY2030 against EBITDA growth of +13, +11, +5 and +4 percent). Free cash flow behaves differently from net income: it converts only about 66 to 90 percent of adjusted net income (and 28 to 41 percent of EBITDA) through FY2030, against more than 100 percent of net income in FY2024 and FY2025, because capital spending, lease payments, reorganisation cash and the end of the down-payment tailwind sit below EBITDA but outside adjusted net income. Share buybacks only neutralise dilution and do not move EPS. The gap between reported and adjusted earnings stays large in FY2026 (Core exposure gains, reorganisation, acquired-intangible amortisation, discontinued operations, a foreign-exchange hedging loss) and narrows to about €0.5 to €0.8 a share from FY2027 if copper is stable.

C-78

Beyond cutting costs, how much plausible scope is there to improve the financial outputs over the mid term; do peer precedents show it is achievable; would it come from a few big areas or many small ones; and is it congruent with the stated strategy?

A741st of 35

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement. There is substantial, plausible scope, concentrated in three big areas, each with peer or internal precedent and each congruent with the stated strategy: the Transmission margin, Grid volume on new capacity, and Republic Wire's synergies. Together they could add about €250m to €320m of EBITDA between FY2025 and FY2028 organically, against the company's bridge of +€422m including further acquisitions.

  1. Transmission margin, from 13.7 percent (H1 2026) towards 16 to 17 percent by 2028 (about +€70m to €100m). Mechanisms: legacy contract roll-off, Electra replacing charters, a loaded mass-impregnated line, 525 kV work at 2024 to 2026 prices. Precedents: NKT from 15.3 percent to 20.2 percent in one year on contract vintage; Prysmian from the low teens to 21.2 percent over 2021 to 2026. Bernstein sees Electra supporting margin beyond the 2028 target. The binding condition is loading, not price.
  2. Grid volume on capacity (about +€50m to €70m). About 40 percent more European capacity by 2028 and 39 percent more accessories capacity into a market where demand exceeds supply; the precedent is Grid's own margin rising from 6.7 percent (2021) to the mid-teens under the current chief executive.
  3. Republic Wire and North American Connect (about +€50m to €60m beyond FY2026's seven months). A full year of the acquired EBITDA (about €66m on the 2027 entry estimate) plus synergies of €23m within three years, half purchasing and half cross-selling of Canadian medium-voltage cable into data centres.
  4. Many smaller areas (about +€30m to €50m): La Triveneta's restructuring towards premium verticals, advanced offers rising to more than 50 percent of Grid and Connect revenue, SHIFT AI, recycling.

Offsets to net against: Connect Europe mix and unindexed input inflation (about -€20m to -€50m), currency translation, and the post-2028 utilisation question at Halden.

C-80

To what extent has the core growth engine decelerated over the last twelve months: is the revenue growth rate running below the company's own normal rate, and is the input change force itself slowing?

F3630th of 35

Layer 1 · Risks

Judgement. The output has decelerated and the input has split. Group organic growth fell from +8.3 percent in 2025 to +1.5 percent in H1 2026 and +3.9 percent for the nine months, below the company's recent normal; Electrification's nine-month +5.3 percent is inside its 3 to 5 percent framework. The input force is accelerating for Grid, stable for Connect North America, weak for Connect Europe, and slowing in the one place that matters most for the margin story: high-voltage order intake and backlog growth.

  • Output: Transmission organic growth went from +33 to +40 percent in late 2025 to -0.1 percent in H1 2026 and -4.0 percent in Q3 2026 (planned phasing plus the Great Sea Interconnector halt); Grid +4.9 percent in H1 then +14.8 percent in Q3; Connect +7.3 percent in H1 and +10.5 percent in Q3; Other Activities -15.6 percent in H1 then +24.7 percent in Q3 on a distorted comparison.
  • Input, Transmission: the backlog has not grown in nine months (€7.7bn to €7.8bn); no award is visible for the mass-impregnated line beyond mid-2028; transmission operators are phasing projects on cost; the industry refers to an "air pocket" in high-voltage direct-current awards; Nexans has lost share. This is a slowing input, even if it is a pause rather than a reversal.
  • Input, Grid and data centres: accelerating (utility capex, frameworks, data-centre load).
  • Input, Connect Europe: construction still contracting.

For the model and for risk. The revenue deceleration of 2026 is mostly planned and should reverse in 2027; the risk is that Transmission's slower order intake turns a planned pause into a utilisation gap in 2028 to 2029.

C-67

How do the sell-side's forecasts for the next one to three years compare with the last one to five years, for the main financial statement line items in particular and the granular working up to them, and how credible is the gap given the change forces and foundations found above?

D4420th of 35

Layer 1 · Sell-side estimates

Judgement. Consensus asks for EBITDA growth of 12 to 15 percent a year for three years against about 5 percent a year (restated, continuing) over FY2023 to FY2025, with the step coming from margin, Republic Wire and a reversal of below-EBITDA drags. The aggregate is credible for FY2026 and FY2027 and slightly high for FY2028. The more useful finding is in the mix: consensus looks conservative on Grid, demanding on Connect and dependent on unsigned orders for Transmission revenue, and it looks light on capex and tax, so consensus free cash flow and earnings per share carry more risk than consensus EBITDA.

Line by line (consensus median FY2026, FY2027, FY2028 against history):

  • Revenue: unusable as published because brokers mix standard-sales and current-metal bases (see reading note 3). On standard sales, the brokers who use that basis have about €6.6bn to €6.8bn for FY2026 (+9 to 11 percent, mostly Republic Wire scope), €7.1bn to €7.5bn for FY2027 and €7.4bn to €8.0bn for FY2028, against €5.54bn (FY2024) and €6.10bn (FY2025); organic growth implied is about 4 to 6 percent a year, against the company's 3 to 5 percent framework and 5.1 and 8.3 percent in 2024 and 2025.
  • Adjusted EBITDA: €817m, €936m, €1,052m (+12, +15, +12 percent) against €665m (FY2023), €571m (FY2024 restated) and €728m (FY2025). Margin on standard sales about 12.2, 12.9 and 13.8 percent against 11.9 percent. Broker range FY2027 €898m to €1,012m, FY2028 €1,031m to €1,199m.
  • Depreciation and amortisation: €268m, €278m, €293m against €254m: plausible.
  • EBIT: €526m, €619m, €740m against FactSet adjusted €463m.
  • Interest and other below EBIT (implied): about €105m, €89m, €86m against €53m net interest in FY2025: plausible given acquisition debt.
  • Tax (implied from adjusted net income over pre-tax profit): about 27 to 29 percent including minorities, against a reported 30.9 percent in FY2025 and 23.7 to 30.9 percent over three years: slightly optimistic.
  • Earnings per share (brokers' basis): €7.03, €9.13, €10.88 against €8.08 (contaminated base); broker range FY2027 €7.72 to €11.78 and FY2028 €9.94 to €13.59, a dispersion three times that of EBITDA; brokers' figures sit about 9 percent above adjusted net income per diluted share.
  • Free cash flow: €309m, €439m, €534m against FactSet's €269m (FY2025; company €344m); capex €381m, €309m, €283m against €383m: capex looks light given the accessories, Morocco, Lens and Charleroi additions on top of the original €1.2bn plan.
  • Dividend: €3.12, €3.40, €3.79 against €2.90: consistent with a payout of at least 30 percent.

The granular working (FactSet segment consensus, mean, one to three years forward; fewer brokers, treat as indicative):

  • Transmission: EBITDA €228m, €272m, €345m on revenue €1,693m, €1,864m, €2,053m (margins about 13.5, 14.6 and 16.8 percent). The margin path matches the company's 17 percent ambition; the revenue path (+10 percent a year in 2027 and 2028) needs the replacement mass-impregnated order, 525 kV deliveries and Mallorca-Menorca to land on time. Credible on margin, at risk on volume.
  • Grid: EBITDA €225m, €248m, €263m (margins about 16 percent), growth of roughly 3, 10 and 6 percent from an FY2025 base of about €218m (30 percent of group EBITDA). With Q3 organic growth of 14.8 percent, about 40 percent more capacity by 2028 and 39 percent more accessories capacity, this looks conservative by perhaps €15m to €30m by FY2028.
  • Connect: EBITDA €349m, €416m, €447m (margins about 12.0, 12.6 and 13.0 percent) from about €291m in FY2025. FY2026 implies H2 Connect EBITDA up roughly 40 percent including Republic Wire's €25m to €30m, or about 20 percent organically, with Nordic construction still weak and polymer costs rising; FY2028 at 13 percent implies a European recovery that Rothschild & Co Redburn does not expect before 2028. This is the most demanding line.

Credibility verdict against the forces and foundations. The external forces support the Grid and Transmission margin assumptions; the competitive and construction evidence argues the Connect margin path is too high; the order-intake evidence argues the Transmission revenue path is slightly early; capex and tax are light. My judgement is EBITDA of about €810m, €915m and €1,030m, and free cash flow and earnings per share 5 to 10 percent below consensus in FY2027 and FY2028.

P-09

After assessing the other questions/items in the set in greater detail, what is the net likely impact to the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, versus the last four to six years?

C5613th of 35

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement. The net effect is a company whose earnings grow faster than in the last three continuing years but whose growth is narrower, more mechanical and shorter-dated than in 2020 to 2025, and whose cash lags its earnings. Standard sales grow at about 5.9 percent a year from FY2025 to FY2030 (about 4.5 percent organic, plus Republic Wire), in line with the last five years' organic pace; adjusted EBITDA grows at about 8.7 percent a year (11.7 percent a year to FY2028, then 4.3 percent), and the margin on standard sales gains about 170 basis points (11.9 to 13.6 percent) against about 580 basis points in 2020 to 2025; adjusted EPS grows at about 12 percent a year from a flat FY2026; free cash flow converts about 30 to 41 percent of EBITDA against 47 to 68 percent in the down-payment years. The most important structural finding is concentration: about 61 percent of the €285m EBITDA increase from FY2025 to FY2028 comes from two items, Transmission margin conversion (+€94m) and Republic Wire (about +€80m of scope and synergies), each of which carries one identifiable binary risk (the replacement mass-impregnated order; US low-voltage pricing). Grid capacity adds €64m (22 percent) with the best visibility, and Connect's organic business adds only about €42m (15 percent) over three years. After FY2028 no driver of comparable size is visible inside the current perimeter, so the FY2029 to FY2030 path depends on how the balance sheet is used.

How the questions in this set net out on the line items.

  • Operating leverage (P-01) and pricing (P-04) together explain the margin path: mechanical gains in Transmission through FY2028, mix gains in Grid from FY2028, input-cost drag in Connect in FY2027, then a plateau.
  • Cost elimination (P-03) adds little net, about €30m to €40m by FY2029, mostly inside Republic Wire's synergies.
  • Feedback loops (P-05) set the timing: reinforcing in the profit and loss account to FY2028, balancing in cash now and in margin from 2029.
  • Below-EBITDA items and financial leverage (P-02, P-08) explain why FY2026 earnings per share are flat, why EPS grows faster than EBITDA afterwards, and why free cash flow lags both.
  • Track record (P-06) sets the weight: high for FY2026, moderate for FY2027, lower beyond.

Against the last four to six years, the three differences that matter for the next set.

  1. The source of improvement has moved from management action to mechanics. 2018 to 2025 was a self-help story (SHIFT, portfolio rotation, selectivity) that could surprise; 2026 to 2028 is mostly dated and contracted (vintage, vessels, capacity, synergies), which narrows the cone but also removes the scope for large positive surprises within the perimeter.
  2. The earnings path is more concentrated and has dated binary points. The replacement order (expected by the first quarter of 2027), the FY2026 results and FY2027 guidance (24 February 2027) and the H1 2027 Transmission margin (July 2027) decide most of FY2027 and FY2028.
  3. Cash no longer confirms earnings. In 2023 to 2025 free cash flow ran ahead of earnings on down-payments; from 2026 it runs behind, which matters because the company's own target (above 45 percent conversion in 2028) and the market's confidence both lean on cash.

Memo topic 7, Scope for upgrades over 6 to 12 months: the Rater's score

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

The fourth set as a whole

D3525th of 35

Layer 4 · The fourth set as a whole

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

Guidance, consensus and the narrative reflect our assessment of the operating business well and our assessment of its earnings and cash poorly, and the share price reflects both better than either. On guidance, there is no favourable gap for the next event: FY2026 will be met near its midpoint while the market expects the top end, the first FY2027 range will probably be set 2 to 4 percent below the consensus median and may exclude the replacement order if it is still unsigned in February, and the 2028 ambition is an organic-plus-acquisitions figure that will be reframed rather than raised; the favourable element is the company's habit of raising at the half-year, which makes a July 2027 raise a better-than-even prospect if the order and the H1 Transmission margin arrive. On consensus, the gap is unfavourable on every line for the February event (EBITDA trimmed, EPS cut 3 to 6 percent, free cash flow cut 15 to 25 percent for FY2027) and the net breadth of revisions, already negative, will probably stay negative until July; the out-years will be held until February 2028 and then depend on capital allocation. On the narrative, the sell-side knows the segment story and the sector risks and does not know, or has not written up, the three financial dynamics that produce most of the EPS and cash gap (the earnings basis, the depreciation and tax step, the cash target's full-basis definition), nor the one technology fact (the Mallorca-Menorca award is extruded, so the mass-impregnated line still awaits its order) that Bernstein alone has reported; investor commentary beyond the brokers is unobserved. What is not reflected on the positive side is also real: the near-certain conversion of the preferred-bidder position on the sector's record, Grid's capacity step still below the street's segment numbers, a Transmission margin step that is contracted rather than hoped for, and a balance sheet that deleverages to about 0.5x by 2028 on the organic path with the EPS option (buybacks adding 7 percent, acquisitions 4 percent) unpriced. For Richard's judgement on the investment case: the expectation gap is unfavourable at the level of the next two earnings events and the brokers' models, and favourable at the level of the share price, which at the 5th percentile of its five-year multiple range and a 30 percent discount to peers already discounts a worse outcome than our base case; the case over the next six months is therefore one of waiting for February's cuts to be absorbed, and over months six to eighteen one of July's proofs being delivered. The memo should present it that way: a stock whose estimates must still come down a little before its multiple can go up a lot, and whose timing risk sits in one document and one margin print.

Guidance

I-01

How likely is company guidance to be raised, met or reduced at the next few earnings events (all such events over the next 18 months), and what would drive each outcome? Secondarily, how likely is any guidance, mid-term target or ambition for 18 months to three years out to be raised, met or reduced?

C-5016th of 35

Layer 4 · Guidance

Judgement. Guidance will be met, not raised, for FY2026; the first FY2027 guide on 24 February 2027 will most probably be set with a midpoint 2 to 4 percent below the consensus median of €936m and will then be raised once, at the half-year in July 2027, if the replacement order is signed and the H1 Transmission margin prints at 15 percent or better; and the 2028 ambition of €1,150m plus or minus €75m will neither be raised nor formally cut inside 18 months but will be reframed, most likely at the FY2028 guide in February 2028, into an organic figure below the bottom of the range unless an acquisition of €1bn or more has been made by then. The pattern that matters for the memo is that the company's guiding habit (guide to what is contracted, raise at the half-year, confirm at the nine months) makes a "reduced" outcome at any single event improbable (I put it at 10 to 15 percent per event) and makes a "raised" outcome depend almost entirely on one document, the order, whose signature is probable but whose timing is not; and that the one target the company is most likely to miss is not an earnings target but the full-basis cash conversion of 45 percent in 2028, which the new evidence shows is defined more demandingly than the second set assumed.

Event by event over the 18 months.

Event Date What is guided or at stake Most likely outcome (probability) What drives each outcome
FY2026 results 24 February 2027 adjusted EBITDA €770m to €840m; free cash flow €235m to €325m met at or slightly above the €805m midpoint (60 percent at or above; 30 percent in €790m to €805m; 10 percent below €790m; above €825m 20 percent); free cash flow inside the range (80 percent), near the midpoint on the company's basis H2 Transmission margin (legacy roll-off, Electra's first full half) against a Q4 organic decline that may reach -10 to -15 percent on the +40 percent comparison; Grid and Connect momentum; Connect H2 margin above 12 percent as management indicated; Other Activities; working capital neutral in H2
First FY2027 guidance 24 February 2027 none yet; consensus €936m (median), €938m (mean); brokers €898m to €1,012m; Deutsche Bank expects a guide of about €880m to €940m a range with a midpoint of about €905m to €920m, below consensus (70 percent); a midpoint at or above €936m (20 percent); a midpoint below €890m, most likely if the order is unsigned and excluded by habit, as the Great Sea Interconnector was (10 percent) the order's status; the company's stated preference for guiding on contracted work; the 2027 energy and polymer cost step; Grid capacity timing; the first synergy figure
Q1 2027 sales late April 2027 no guidance change expected confirmation (85 percent) Transmission organic growth turning less negative on the easy H1 2026 comparison (-0.1 percent); Grid above 8 percent

FY2027 results and first FY2028 guide February 2028 the 2028 ambition becomes an annual guide an organic FY2028 range of about €1,000m to €1,100m, below the €1,075m bottom of the ambition unless acquisitions have closed (55 percent); a range inside the ambition because acquisitions have been made (35 percent); a range at or above the midpoint (10 percent) acquisitions announced in 2027; the Transmission margin trajectory; Grid capacity landed

The FY2026 outcome, and why a raise is no longer available. There is no reporting event between now and 24 February 2027 at which guidance could be raised, so FY2026 is a question of where in the range the year lands. The second set's €805m assumes H2 EBITDA of €417m (+17 percent on H2 2025's €356m, of which about seven points are Republic Wire); Deutsche Bank's €821m and the FactSet median of €817m assume about €430m; management says it is "working hard to deliver better than the midpoint" and Deutsche Bank reports it "targeting the top end". Against that, Bernstein warned that consensus already sits above the midpoint and needs about 140 basis points of H2 Transmission margin improvement, the Q3 Transmission miss was on volume (-4.0 percent against -1.7 percent expected) with Q4 worse, and Republic Wire's scope ran 3 points light. My central expectation is €805m to €820m: met, not beaten by enough to count as a surprise. On free cash flow, the second set's full-basis €239m is about €280m on the company's definition, the midpoint; the guide is likely to be met, with the H2 working-capital movement the swing (the H1 inflow of €121m is not repeated; a Q4 down-payment on the replacement order, if signed by December, would put the year in the upper half).

The FY2027 guide, and the market's likely reading of it. Three facts set the base rate. First, the company has set each initial range since FY2022 at or below where the year landed, and the FY2026 initial range was 2 percent below consensus at the midpoint and called "surprisingly light"; the shares fell. Second, the company excluded the Great Sea Interconnector from the FY2026 guide rather than assume it, and the same logic applies to any unsigned order in February 2027: if the replacement order is not signed by then (probability about 35 percent on the 9.6-month conversion average, which points to April), the guide is likely to exclude its loading effect, which would put the midpoint near €880m to €900m. Third, the second set's €909m and Deutsche Bank's expected €880m to €940m range already sit below consensus, so a guide at that level is "in line with the better-informed brokers and below the median". The likely market reading is the one the first set's C-84 described: an initial sell-off on a light guide, then a re-rating on the half-year raise if it comes. T-I1's counter-point is also right: if the market has learned to discount the sandbagging, a light guide would fail to rebase expectations, and the stock would then be exposed to a later miss; I think this is the minority case, because the October reaction showed the market is not discounting a sandbag, it is discounting an execution gap.

The 2028 ambition and the mid-term targets, one by one.

  • Adjusted EBITDA €1,150m plus or minus €75m (includes M&A): organic paths are €1,013m (second set), €1,052m (consensus), €1,088m (Jefferies), €1,096m (Deutsche Bank), all below the €1,075m bottom. The ambition is met only with acquisitions: about €140m of acquired EBITDA for the midpoint, €60m to €70m for the bottom. Management's "rich pipeline" and its tolerance of 2.5x leverage for the right deal make a €0.5bn to €1.3bn acquisition by 2028 more likely than not (55 to 60 percent), which would put the bottom of the range within reach; a formal reduction before 2028 is unlikely (10 percent) because the target is in the chief executive's incentives and the bridge is explicit about M&A the most likely course (55 percent) is a quiet reframing when the FY2028 guide is set. Probability the ambition is raised: under 10 percent (no capital markets day scheduled; peers raised theirs at such events).
  • Transmission margin 17 percent by 2028: met or nearly met (60 percent), because the backlog supports it (Deutsche Bank) and the second set's 16.6 percent is within rounding; the risk is a Halden gap after mid-2028 showing in H2 2028.
  • Electrification organic growth 3 to 5 percent a year, 2024 to 2028: met at the top of the range (70 percent), Grid-led; it could be exceeded.
  • Free cash flow conversion above 45 percent in 2028 (full basis, flat working capital): the target most likely to be missed (65 percent): the second set's path gives about 40 percent on the company's basis even with the flat working capital the company assumes, because capital spending stays at about 4 percent of standard sales and interest and tax have risen; meeting it needs either EBITDA at the ambition with no working-capital outflow or capital spending about €60m below the second set's. A miss here would be read by the market as confirmation of the cash concern Bernstein and Jefferies have raised.
  • ROCE above 20 percent: likely missed on the organic path (second set 18 to 19 percent) and more so with acquisitions at Republic Wire multiples (60 percent miss).
  • Leverage at or below 1.0x by 2028: met with ease absent acquisitions (85 percent); the company has said it would go to 2.5x for the right deal, so this is the target least informative about intent.
  • Payout at least 30 percent: met comfortably (the second set's payout is 40 to 49 percent); a dividend of about €3.10 for FY2026 is the likely February announcement.

What would change the picture. A raise of FY2027 guidance in July 2027 becomes likely (above 60 percent) if the order is signed before February and the H2 2026 Transmission margin prints at or above 15 percent; a reduction of FY2027 guidance (now 10 percent) becomes material if the order slips past June 2027 and Connect's European margin falls below 11 percent, in which case the H1 2027 event would be a cut of €20m to €30m. A raise of the 2028 ambition would need a capital markets day, which the company has said it is not planning, or an acquisition large enough to lift the organic bridge; neither is in my base case for the 18 months.

Score reasoning. The question's score measures how favourable the gap is between our assessment and what guidance reflects. Our FY2026 sits at the midpoint the company guides and the market already expects the top end, so no favourable gap; our FY2027 sits where the first guide will probably sit, so no gap either way; our 2028 organic figure sits below the ambition, an unfavourable gap that the market has largely absorbed (consensus is also below); the single favourable element is that the conservative guiding habit makes a mid-2027 raise a better-than-even prospect, and that the market tends to re-rate on it. Net: slightly below neutral.

Sellside sales

P-09After assessing the other questions/items in the set in greater detail, what is the net likely impact to the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, versus the last four to six years?C5613th of 35full answer under 7b Margins/growth vs. past
I-02

How likely are sell-side consensus forecasts, and the net breadth of revisions, to move higher or lower over the next few earnings events (over the next 18 months): to be upgraded, cut or held, and why? Secondarily, how likely are the consensus forecasts for 18 months to three years out to be upgraded, cut or held?

D3022nd of 35

Layer 4 · Sell-side estimates

Judgement. Consensus EBITDA will be trimmed, consensus EPS cut and consensus free cash flow cut materially through the February 2027 results, and the net breadth of revisions, already negative (FY2027 EPS -3 percent over one month; the dashboard's revision-momentum score down from 7 to 1 since September), will stay negative into and through that event; the turn, if it comes, comes with the half-year results in July 2027 and depends on the same two facts as the guidance raise (the order signed; a Transmission margin at or above 15 percent). The reason the direction is clear is that the three lines carry three different kinds of error: EBITDA consensus is 1 to 4 percent above our figures because of Transmission volume and Connect's margin (an operating disagreement the February guide will settle mostly our way); EPS consensus is 5 to 10 percent above our adjusted net income because of depreciation, tax and the share count (a definitional error that the February accounts will expose line by line); and free-cash-flow consensus (€309m, €439m, €534m) is above the company's own FY2026 range and far above any FY2027 figure built with leases, reorganisation cash and a flat backlog (a modelling error that the FY2026 cash print and the FY2027 guide will correct). For 18 months to three years out, FY2028 consensus (€1,052m of EBITDA, €10.88 of EPS) is more likely to be held than cut in the next 18 months, because brokers leave out-years alone until the company reframes 2028, and the reframing is a February 2028 event; FY2029 consensus rests on one broker and is not a consensus.

The revisions, line by line and event by event.

Why the February cuts are probable rather than possible. First, the company's habit: an initial FY2027 range with a midpoint below €936m (70 percent) mechanically pulls the median down, because most of the twelve brokers on FactSet mark to the midpoint plus a conventional margin; the four brokers already at €898m to €902m (Deutsche Bank, AlphaValue, CIC) will not move, and the brokers above €960m (Jefferies €966m, the restricted houses at €965m, Goldman €1,012m) will. Second, the accounts: the FY2026 statements will show total depreciation of about €310m to €320m (Deutsche Bank €318m) against a consensus line of €268m, a tax rate near 31 to 32 percent against an implied 27 to 29 percent, and the final Republic Wire purchase-price allocation; each of these is a mechanical cut to EPS for the brokers who have not modelled it, which OWN-2 shows is at least Hana (depreciation of €188m) and probably several of the houses that put FY2027 EPS above €10. Third, cash: the FY2026 cash print will land near €280m against a consensus of €309m, and the company will give a FY2027 cash range for the first time; given a flat backlog, no down-payment tailwind and capital spending the company has itself said runs above its original plan (accessories, Morocco, Lens, Republic Wire), that range will sit well below €439m. The one line where a February upgrade is plausible is Grid: consensus Grid EBITDA of €248m for FY2027 is below the second set's €255m and the Q4 print will probably show double-digit organic growth again.

Why the breadth is more likely to turn in July than in February. The revision breadth is the count of brokers raising less those cutting, and it has two drivers at Nexans: the guide and the proofs. February supplies the guide (negative by habit) and the first proofs (the H2 2026 Transmission margin, the cash print), and the proofs are likely to be adequate rather than exciting (a margin near 14.8 percent, cash near the midpoint). July supplies the first clean half (no perimeter noise), the order (signed by April on the conversion average), an easy Transmission comparison, Grid capacity landing and, if all three hold, the raise. The dashboard's revision-momentum score of 1 is consistent with a stock between two events; the first set's observation that the ratings base (69 percent Buy; 10 of 12 visible houses Buy or Overweight; Equita Hold; Bernstein Market-Perform) has little room to become more bullish means that breadth cannot improve through ratings and must improve through estimates, which requires July's proofs. I expect one or two rating downgrades around February on a light guide (Jefferies, which upgraded on 11 October on "improving transmission fundamentals", is the house most exposed to a disappointment on the order) and target-price cuts of 5 to 10 percent from the current mean of €168.

The 18-months-to-three-years horizon. FY2028 consensus EBITDA of €1,052m sits €39m above the second set and €23m below the bottom of the company's range; the brokers are, in effect, modelling the organic bridge without acquisitions and without the full Connect recovery, which is sensible. It will be held until the company reframes 2028 (February 2028) or announces an acquisition, whichever comes first; an acquisition of €0.5bn at 9 to 10 times would add about €50m to consensus FY2028 EBITDA and €0.35 to €0.40 to EPS, so the direction of the out-year revisions depends more on capital allocation than on operations, which is the second set's P-02 point applied to the street. FY2028 EPS consensus of €10.88 is the most exposed out-year figure, for the below-EBITDA reasons, and I expect it to be cut by 5 to 8 percent over the window even if EBITDA holds. FY2029 figures (EBITDA €1,124m, EPS €12.98) come from one broker and should not be used.

What would produce upgrades instead. The order signed before 24 February with its value disclosed; FY2026 EBITDA at €820m or better with H2 Transmission above 15 percent; a FY2027 guide with a midpoint at or above €930m (which would mean management has broken its habit, or has the order and Grid capacity both in hand); and a capital-return change. The joint probability of the first three is about 20 to 25 percent, which is the probability that the February event produces net upgrades rather than net cuts. The sector read-across could also help: if Prysmian (29 October) and NKT (19 November) print rising Transmission margins and comment on firm award pricing, the brokers' confidence in Nexans's vintage effect will rise and the February cuts will be smaller.

Score reasoning. The gap between our assessment and consensus is unfavourable on all three lines over the next event, moderately unfavourable over 18 months, and the breadth is already negative; the mitigation is that the market has largely priced the cuts (a 5th-percentile multiple while FY2027 EPS has fallen only 3 percent in a year) and that the July turn is a better-than-even prospect.

11d

Current multiple

C6214th of 36Memo topic 11, The fair multiple: the Rater's score1 answer
D-0748
D-07

To what extent do the share price and the multiple move with sector and industry peers rather than idiosyncratically (on a rolling six-month basis), and so how far is the multiple company-driven rather than industry-driven?

C-4816th of 35

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement. The share price now moves with the sector more than at any time in the last two years, and the multiple's level is set by the company: the direction is industry-driven, the discount is company-driven. On the latest six-month window the peer basket explains 57 percent of Nexans's daily variance (correlation 0.76, beta 1.03), up from 20 to 26 percent in late 2024 and early 2026 and the highest reading in the 24-month series; the idiosyncratic share is 43 percent. But over the same six months Nexans fell 24.3 percent against the basket's 11.9 percent, so about half of its fall was its own (the Great Sea Interconnector overhang, the Republic Wire price, the Q3 Transmission miss and the absent order, the French sovereign premium), and the level at which it now trades (10.4x two-year-forward consensus earnings, the 5th percentile of five years and the 22nd of ten) is about a 30 percent discount on EV/EBITDA to Prysmian and NKT (Jefferies, 11 October 2026) that has persisted, with variations, since the 2018 crisis. The correct reading for predictability is therefore two-sided: over the next 18 months most of the daily and monthly path of the shares will be set by the cable group's regime (the capacity-overhang narrative, the rates and risk-off regime, the artificial-intelligence financing question), which Nexans cannot influence, while the gap between Nexans's multiple and its peers' will close or widen on Nexans's own three events, which it can. The multiple is, in short, industry-driven in motion and company-driven in level, and the company-driven part is the part that offers the return.

The co-movement evidence, and what it says about the regime.

  • The 24-month series shows three phases: an idiosyncratic phase (R-squared 20 to 26 percent, November 2024 to March 2025) when Nexans traded on its own transformation; a rising phase through the summer of 2025 (to 54 percent in September 2025) as the whole group re-rated on the scarcity theme; a sharp fall back to 22 to 35 percent from October 2025 to February 2026, which coincides with the Great Sea Interconnector halt, the chief executive change (shares down nearly 10 percent on the announcement) and the "surprisingly light" FY2026 guide, all company events; and a steady rise since to 57 percent as the group de-rated together on rates and capacity worries. The beta has settled at about 1.0 after reaching 1.24 in October 2025. The stock has become a sector proxy again precisely because its own news flow has gone quiet (no profit data since July) and the sector's has not.
  • By peer, Nexans moves most closely with ABB (0.77), Prysmian (0.74), NKT (0.72), Siemens Energy (0.69), Legrand and Schneider (0.67): the large-capitalisation electrification complex, not the cable pure plays alone. Correlation with Cenergy is only 0.50 and with Pfisterer 0.50; with the small-capitalisation names (Alfen, Arteche, Fastned, Kempower, Landis+Gyr) it is 0.13 to 0.37. The practical implication is that the sector risk the Consumer should carry is the large-capitalisation grid and electrification complex's multiple, which is itself at high percentiles for Siemens, Schneider, ABB, Arteche and Cenergy (73rd to 98th) and low for Nexans, NKT, Legrand and Pfisterer (0 to 22nd). Within the cable group, the dispersion is striking: Cenergy at the 84th percentile, Prysmian at the 41st, NKT at the 15th, Nexans at the 22nd of ten years (5th of five). The group does not share one multiple; it shares one direction.
  • The dashboard's technical momentum rating for every stock in the sector is D or F (relative strength percentile 18 for Nexans; price 16 percent below the 200-day average; 50-day average €134 against a price of €114): the whole group is in a down-phase, which supports the reading that the recent fall is mostly regime.

What is company-driven in the multiple, and how much. The 30 percent discount to Prysmian and NKT on EV/EBITDA, and the roughly 25 to 40 percent discount on forward earnings (Nexans 10.4x against Prysmian's 13.9x and NKT's 14.8x on the dashboard's current P/E), is attributable to identifiable company factors that the sell-side names consistently (T-I2): execution history (2013, 2018); the thinnest backlog of the three and the Great Sea Interconnector hole; a Transmission margin 650 to 750 basis points below peers; cash conversion normalising while NKT's and Prysmian's cash is supported by down-payments; a new team; a French listing in a sovereign-stress year; and, less often named, the EPS-basis confusion that leaves the market unable to agree a normalised earnings run-rate (Bernstein's €7.91 against Deutsche Bank's €6.64 for FY2026). Each of these is either resolvable by Nexans within 18 months (the order, the margin print, the cash print, the team's record) or structural (backlog depth, scale). Jefferies's own history of the discount (about 30 percent "persisting due to historical execution issues") says it narrows on proof and widens on doubt, which is what October showed.

Implications for the forecasts' predictability and for the memo's multiple.

  • For predictability: a beta of 1.0 and R-squared of 57 percent mean that even if Nexans delivers the second set's FY2027, the share price in 18 months is as likely to be set by whether the cable group's multiple has recovered from its current down-phase as by the delivery itself; the first set's C-82 (peer read-across, capacity-overhang headlines, the artificial-intelligence financing question, French politics into the 2027 election) lists the sector risks, and all are live. This argues for the memo to present the return in two parts: the sector part (the group's multiple regime) and the company part (the discount).
  • For the fair multiple (the memo's topic 11, which uses the model's guideline): the model's guideline of 15.82x is the ten-year 90th percentile band, which the first set associated with peak-delivery phases; the first set's C-50 argued for the ten-year median (12.3x) as the anchor for a company with a bounded reinvestment runway, and nothing in the third-set evidence changes that. On the co-movement evidence the stock is in the sector's down-phase and at its own trough percentile, which is a "turning up" setting, not a "peak delivery" one; a re-rating to the median on FY2027 delivery is the plausible 18-month path (about €140 on the first set's arithmetic), and the 75th percentile (14.0x) would require the proofs of 2027 plus a sector recovery. I record this for the Consumer's view of the guideline rather than as a change to anything in the model.
  • For risk: the company-driven half of the recent fall is already in the price (the stock has de-rated to its five-year low while estimates have fallen only 3 percent for FY2027 over twelve months), which is why the first set's trough analysis gives only about 10 percent downside on unchanged earnings; the sector-driven half could continue if the group de-rates further on capacity or rates, and Nexans would follow with a beta of 1.0.

Outside the eleven ratings

Answers not placed under any of the eleven ratings

The table the ratings map uses places these nowhere; each is shown with the reason the table records.

Why it is not placed It asks whether the stock fits the sector's historical patterns of share price and financial outperformance, a cross-check across many topics rather than one of the eleven.

C-84

What do historical precedents, rules of thumb and the empirical evidence show about how companies in this sector and industry have typically generated share price, financial and operational outperformance, and underperformance, and to what extent does this company fit those patterns?

D4625th of 35

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. The sector's history offers five rules, and Nexans today fits the favourable ones only partially: it fits the "self-help turnaround" pattern historically (already paid for), it fits the "cheap, de-rated, estimates not cut" pattern now, but it does not fit the pattern the market is currently paying for, proven margin conversion with rising estimates.

The rules the evidence supports:

  1. Margin conversion in a price plateau pays, and is rewarded after it is visible. A plateau of high prices means two to three more years of rising executed margins as old contracts roll off (sector digest, 13 October 2026). The market pays for proven conversion (Prysmian's Transmission margin from the low teens to 21.2 percent; its shares +95 percent in the twelve months to mid-July 2026) and pauses where the earnings step is signed but not yet shown.
  2. Capacity announcements de-rate before prices fall. The market marks a cable maker down when it announces capacity, years before prices move; de-ratings historically preceded earnings falls by one to two quarters; the 2015 to 2020 surplus took high-voltage margins below 5 percent.
  3. Mega-project execution failures cause sudden, deep drawdowns, and recoveries follow if the franchise is intact. Prysmian's Western Link (2018): a €165m provision, the shares down 38 percent, then about 412 percent over seven years. Nexans: two profit warnings in 2013, the 2018 crisis (shares to €22, market value about €800m); bears on Nexans's execution were right in the short term about 80 percent of the time, with 30 to 50 percent drawdowns.
  4. Self-help turnarounds in this industry work but take years. Nexans's SHIFT programme took margins from 6.1 percent (2020) to 11.9 percent (2025) and delivered about 215 percent total shareholder return over six years (about 400 percent from 2019 to 2024 on another measure).
  5. Conservative guiders who beat and raise compound their multiples (Prysmian's sandbagging since FY2023; NKT's 100 percent beat ratio against initial EBITDA guidance), whereas acquisition-led raises are discounted.

How Nexans fits now:

  • It is not a conversion leader: its Transmission margin trails peers by about 650 to 750 basis points, and its raise in 2026 was acquisition-led (rule 5 not met).
  • It is not suffering a capacity de-rating of its own (rule 2), because it is not adding speculative subsea capacity; it is being dragged by the sector's.
  • Its turnaround premium was earned and has been spent (rule 4); the shares returned about -4 percent over twelve months against cable peers at +10 to +60 percent.
  • It now trades at 10.4x 24-month-forward earnings (5th percentile of five years) with no estimate collapse and buy ratings at 69 percent: the shape of a de-rated quality stock waiting for a catalyst, which in this sector has historically been a clean beat with an organic raise, a signed order filling an under-loaded plant, or a capital-return change.

For the model and the later sets: the historical base rate argues that a re-rating requires visible proof (a signed replacement order; a Transmission margin print at or above 15 percent; Connect stable) rather than the passage of time, and that the downside path, if proof fails, has historically been sharper than consensus models.

Why it is not placed The table holds this question in reserve for Riskiness sub-items that are not yet built, so it is not yet placed.

C-83

How far could the shares fall to a fair trough multiple?

A781st of 35

Layer 1 · Risks

Judgement. With earnings unchanged, about 10 percent; with a plausible earnings cut, about 20 percent; in a severe execution-plus-cycle scenario like the company's past crises, about 35 percent. The downside to a trough multiple is now much smaller than it was at the 30 September memo, because the stock has already de-rated to near its five-year low.

  • Starting point: €113.60 on 24-month-forward consensus earnings of about €10.79 (brokers' basis), a multiple of about 10.4x to 10.5x, the 5th percentile of five years.
  • Unchanged earnings: the ten-year 10th percentile of 9.5x gives about €102 (-10 percent); the five-year low of 9.46x gives about €102.
  • A plausible earnings cut (the replacement order slips into 2027 and Connect stays weak, cutting forward earnings about 10 percent to €9.7): at 9.5x, about €92 (-19 percent). Jefferies' May 2026 bear scenario was €97.
  • Severe (a 2013- or 2018-style cluster: an execution failure plus a construction downturn plus a US price war; forward earnings -20 percent to about €8.6 at 8.5x): about €73 (-36 percent). The company's history of 30 to 50 percent drawdowns in execution crises supports carrying this tail.
  • Upside for symmetry: if FY2027 meets consensus and the multiple returns to the ten-year median of 12.3x on 24-month-forward earnings of about €11.4 a year from now, about €140 plus a €3.10 dividend (+26 percent).

Why it is not placed It rates the staircase of news the market can look forward to, and none of the eleven rates the narrative or the catalysts as such.

Group judgement

Stock market narrative

D4524th of 35

Layer 1 · Stock market narrative

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The narrative ahead is a "prove it" staircase rather than a surprise staircase: the near-term steps (the replacement order, the February results and FY2027 guide, capacity starts) are expected and dated, and the market will pay only when they convert into a visibly rising Transmission margin, stable Connect and free cash flow. The genuinely novel upside steps (a Great Sea Interconnector restart, ambitions above consensus at a capital markets update, data-centre superconducting awards, a pivot towards buybacks) are possible but not yet well founded. With the shares at the bottom of their valuation range, the staircase needs only to hold, not to surprise, for the stock to re-rate towards its median; the steps to watch first are the order signature before the end of Q1 2027 and the Transmission margin in the February and July 2027 results.

Why it is not placed It rates how well the earlier answers agree with each other, which is a check on the analysis rather than on the company.

D-01

How congruent are the answers to all the questions in the earlier sets: to what extent do they support and align with each other, rather than contradict or jar?

–70

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement. The two sets are highly congruent in substance and jar only in bases and definitions, and that pattern is itself the most useful finding: Nexans is a case whose numbers are unusually exposed to definitional error (four earnings-per-share bases, three free-cash-flow definitions, two revenue bases, IFRS 5 restatements and a FactSet half-year artefact), and the one material contradiction in the file, the model's 13 percent earnings shortfall against the second set's cross-check, is an instance of exactly that exposure rather than a disagreement about the business. On the operating story the sets support each other at every point that matters: Grid is under-modelled by the company and the street; Transmission's margin rises mechanically on contract vintage and Electra while its volume depends on an unsigned order; Connect's European margin is capped by construction and unindexed costs; Republic Wire is a scope step with a pricing risk; depreciation, interest and tax make FY2026 a flat earnings year; cash converts worse than earnings; the team is new and disclosure thinned in October. The new AlphaSense evidence confirms rather than disturbs this picture, and where it adds something (the Mallorca-Menorca technology, the earn-out liability, the cash-target definition, the conversion record of preferred-bidder positions) it changes the odds on timing and the size of two gaps to targets, not the direction of any judgement.

Where the sets align, and the evidence that now underpins each alignment.

  • Both sets make Grid the engine the market under-rates and both model high single-digit growth on a capacity ceiling (second set +9 percent in FY2027 against the company's mid-single-digit guideline and consensus segment EBITDA of €248m against the second set's €255m). The nine-month release and Deutsche Bank (26 October 2026) confirm capacity arriving progressively from late 2026; the risk the first set named (start-up costs ahead of volume) is also the one T-D1 finds in peer ramps (TKH, NKT Applications), and the second set already holds the Grid margin flat at 15.8 to 16.0 percent for two years because of it.
  • Both sets treat Transmission's margin as the most mechanical driver and its volume as the most binary. The second set's path (14.3, 15.6, 16.6 percent) matches the previous chief executive's 100 to 150 basis points a year and sits 40 basis points below the company's 17 percent; Deutsche Bank's 26 October note says the 2028 margin is "heavily supported by executing the existing backlog" with new awards aimed at 2029 and beyond. The first set's probability of a material slip in the replacement order (25 to 30 percent) and the second set's base case (signed by end-March 2027) are consistent with each other and with the 9.6-month conversion average now in evidence.
  • Both sets put consensus EPS and free cash flow, not EBITDA, at the centre of the expectation gap; the second set quantified it (EBITDA -1 to -4 percent, adjusted net income -4 to -10 percent, free cash flow far below), and OWN-2 confirms the mechanism (basic share counts at Bernstein and Hana, consensus depreciation that cannot be reconciled with H1 2026, an implied tax rate below the 32.7 percent reported).
  • Both sets read capital allocation the same way (acquisitions first, anti-dilutive buybacks only, leverage falling only if deals pause); OWN-6 confirms the stance and adds that management regards 2.5x as digestible for a transformational deal, which makes the "base case as a floor" framing of P-02 the right one for EPS and the wrong one for leverage.
  • Both sets' sector and cycle readings (scarcity converting to margin through 2028, parity around 2030, entrants pricing post-2029 slots, Mediterranean as the region where Nexans is most exposed) are consistent with T-D2 and T-I2, which add Morgan Stanley's parity date (2030) and the RWE procurement view that operators are increasingly open to second-tier suppliers.

Where the sets jar, and how I resolve each.

  1. Earnings per share. First set €6.95, €8.45, €10.10; second set €6.38, €8.04, €9.56; the model €5.50, €7.04, €8.47. The second set is right against the first (depreciation and tax) and the model is wrong against the second (the revenue artefact, reading note A). Resolved: the second set's figures stand, with my depreciation caveat (note C.7) leaning them slightly lower.
  2. Free cash flow against guidance and target. The second set understates how well its own FY2026 forecast fits the guidance (note C.1) and overstates how close its FY2028 sits to the 45 percent target (note C.2). Resolved in opposite directions: the near-term cash picture is a little better than the second set says, the mid-term target gap wider.
  3. Transmission's weight and the Halden sensitivity. The second set's "20 to 25 percent" of EBITDA against its own 28 percent; T-D2's €7.5m per half-year against the second set's €20m to €25m. Resolved: 28 percent and €15m to €20m net per half-year.
  4. The earn-out. €43m against €86m: resolved by modelling the recognised €86m liability.
  5. A capital markets update in 2027. The first set expected one; the new evidence says none is scheduled. Resolved: no 2030-target event in the 18-month window in the base case.
  6. The dating of the Transmission trough. The first set dated it H2 2026 to H1 2027 (C-06); the second set modelled Transmission organic growth of -4 percent in FY2026 and +6 percent in FY2027 with H1 2027 helped by an easy comparison (-0.1 percent). Both are consistent with a Q4 2026 organic decline steeper than Q3's -4.0 percent against a +40 percent comparison, which management has warned of. No contradiction, but the Consumer should expect the Q4 sales print (24 February 2027) to show Transmission organic growth of perhaps -10 to -15 percent and should not read it as a change in the case.
  7. Minor: the first set's free-cash-flow-to-net-income history (82 to 179 percent) is after dividends (second set reading note 8), so the first set's C-52 overstates how far conversion has fallen; the first set's "consensus capex looks light" is supported by the second set's €390m, €320m, €300m and by Jefferies's view that Republic Wire needs capital to serve data-centre customers.

What the congruence means for predictability. A case in which two independent analysts, working from different angles, arrive at the same drivers, the same ordering of risks and EBITDA figures within 1 percent (€805m against €810m; €909m against €910m to €920m; €1,013m against €1,020m to €1,040m) is one whose operating forecast is well founded; the sets disagree only below EBITDA and in cash, which is where the brokers also disagree (FY2027 EPS €7.72 to €11.78 on EBITDA of €898m to €1,012m). The practical consequence for the memo is that the Consumer must present every figure with its basis stated, must build EPS from net income on 45.0m diluted shares, and must treat any comparison with consensus EPS or free cash flow as a comparison of definitions first and of forecasts second. The one sentence I would not write is "the sets contradict each other on earnings"; they do not, once the bases are aligned.

Why it is not placed It rates how well the sell-side and investors already articulate the case, and none of the eleven rates the narrative or how much of the case is known.

I-03

How comprehensively are these dynamics articulated in the current narrative of (i) sell-side commentary and (ii) investor commentary (for example on Twitter): how well known is each of them to investors who own or cover the company or its sector, primarily for the next 18 months and secondarily for 18 months to three years out?

D4022nd of 35

Layer 4 · Stock market narrative

Judgement. The sell-side narrative articulates the operating dynamics of the next 18 months well and the financial dynamics poorly; it is comprehensive on what Nexans will sell and at what segment margin, and thin on what reaches earnings per share and cash, on the definition of the cash target, on the technology mismatch in the Mallorca-Menorca award, and on the capital-allocation choice at a trough multiple. Of the eleven dynamics I tested, six are widely discussed (Transmission margin conversion and Electra; the unsigned order and the Great Sea Interconnector; Grid's growth; Republic Wire's integration; high-voltage parity around 2030 and the entrants; copper pass-through), three are discussed by one or two houses in the terms that matter (the below-EBITDA step-up, by Bernstein and in Deutsche Bank's depreciation figures; the end of the down-payment tailwind, by Bernstein, Jefferies and management's own admission; US low-voltage price normalisation, by Deutsche Bank, Morgan Stanley and Jefferies but mostly as a Prysmian question), and two are effectively absent (the brokers'-basis-against-continuing EPS problem, which no house reconciles, and the Hormuz-driven non-metal cost step and its 18-month hedge lag, which no house quantifies for Nexans). Investor commentary beyond the sell-side is not captured in the evidence at all: the AlphaSense report found no social-media or fund commentary, and the only investor-sentiment evidence is second-hand (Deutsche Bank's investor-call feedback, DNB Carnegie's "some investors worry", Jefferies's "on clients' minds"), so I treat the investor narrative as a gap and commission it (request OWN-3). The net implication for the memo is asymmetric: the dynamics the narrative misses are mostly negative for the next 18 months (EPS, cash, costs), which means the February prints can surprise the less careful brokers downwards, but the dynamics the narrative also undervalues are the positive ones that resolve company-specific risk (the order's near-certain conversion, Grid's capacity step, deleveraging to about 0.5x by 2028 without acquisitions), and the share price already discounts the negatives more fully than the brokers' models do.

Coverage check. FactSet shows thirteen broker sheets (Barclays, CIC, Jefferies, Equita, Oddo BHF, AlphaValue, Deutsche Bank, UBS, Goldman Sachs and four restricted houses, plus Bernstein outside the sheet). The AlphaSense research base holds and the reports cite the actual research of Deutsche Bank, Bernstein, Jefferies, DNB Carnegie (on NKT), Morgan Stanley (sector), ING, Hana Financial, Ambit and Rothschild & Co Redburn (sector); it does not hold Goldman Sachs (the most bullish house on FY2028 EBITDA, €1,199m, target €188), Barclays (FY2027 EPS €11.78, an outlier), UBS, Oddo BHF (€190), CIC, Equita, AlphaValue or the restricted houses (two of which share identical figures). The narrative map below therefore covers about half of the covering houses by number and the most-cited ones by influence (Deutsche Bank, Bernstein, Jefferies); the bullish tail of consensus is not in the evidence, which biases the map towards the cautious houses.

Connect margin: Nordic weakness; Hormuz polymers and energy Nordic weakness widely (Jefferies 11 May, 31 August; Bernstein 22 October; Deutsche Bank 26 October notes Finland and Norway improving); non-metal costs generically (Jefferies 2024 initiation on unhedged polymers; Bernstein 22 September on short cycle; NKT's own H1 2026 report); no house quantifies the Nexans effect or the 18-month hedge roll-off none Nordics widely; costs little known the 2027 cost step (about €20m) and the H2 2027 hedge expiry are not in consensus margins (12.6 percent against our 11.8 percent)
5. Republic Wire integration; US price normalisation integration widely and positively (Deutsche Bank "return to America" 29 April; Jefferies; Hana); the price as a "strategic price" (Bernstein 28 April: 40 percent premium); the scope miss (Bernstein 22 October); US margin normalisation (Deutsche Bank 4 August and 8 February: normalised to about 15 percent; Morgan Stanley October 2024: 15 to 11 percent by 2028; Jefferies 31 August) mostly in Prysmian notes none integration widely; price risk known at sector level, rarely applied to Nexans we hold the margin flat; consensus Connect FY2028 (€447m) assumes it rises
6. The below-EBITDA step-up; brokers' basis against continuing EPS Bernstein (22 October: adjusted EPS €7.91 on basic shares; cautious on depreciation and interest); Deutsche Bank (depreciation €318m and €327m; diluted; tax benefits possible); Hana (depreciation €188m, wrong); no house reconciles the brokers' basis to continuing net income; T-I2 names this as a "critical gap" none the step-up known to two houses; the basis problem known to none the single largest source of the EPS expectation gap (5 to 10 percent) is a definitional problem nobody has written up
7. Cash conversion; the end of the down-payment tailwind Bernstein (6 January 2026: prepayment pauses; 13 August), Jefferies (7 October 2025: capacity holes; 29 July 2026: cash guide up), Deutsche Bank (26 October: cash outlook "attractive"); management conceded the 2025 flattering (19 February 2026); Prysmian guides to down-payments offsetting execution; the definition change to a full basis: no house none known in outline to three houses; the target's definition and its achievability unexamined consensus free cash flow (€439m FY2027) is the least defensible line in the street's model
8. Capital allocation: acquisitions against buybacks at a 5th-percentile multiple Bernstein (6 January: use Autoelectric proceeds for a buyback; 28 April: the premium; April to May: 2.5x digestible), Jefferies (10 March: buybacks as a "safety valve"; 11 October: the 30 percent discount), Deutsche Bank (3 March: over €2bn of firepower, expects M&A); a debate, not a demand; no activist none captured known as a debate; not a pressure the arithmetic (a €500m buyback adds 7 percent to FY2028 EPS, an acquisition 4 percent) is ours; the street does not run it
9. High-voltage parity about 2030; entrants Morgan Stanley (27 January 2025 deep dive; 2 July 2026), DNB Carnegie (1 September: "some investors worry"; 6 August: entrants gradual), Jefferies (5 August: Hellenic 525 kV), experts (7 July; RWE procurement 27 October: open to second-tier suppliers); the 3 to 4 point share loss: one expert the sector de-rating is the investor view widely known at sector level; Nexans's relative exposure (least dependent on high voltage; thinnest backlog) less so agreed; we hold Transmission flat after FY2028
10. Copper's effect on revenue and reported profit Deutsche Bank's primer (24 June 2025); the company's standard-sales disclosure; the Core exposure (€75.2m) in the accounts; the mixed revenue bases in consensus: no house flags none pass-through widely known; the reported-profit reversal on a copper fall little discussed a copper fall would make reported EPS fall while adjusted EPS rises; the narrative is unprepared for it
11. Great Sea Interconnector restart (Meridiam 66 percent) Jefferies (31 August; 1 September), Deutsche Bank (13 January 2026: "from downside risk to upside risk"), Cypriot press (surveys to resume; cost unknown); the chief executive: production restart targeted 2028, no effect on 2028 targets Cypriot and Greek press widely known as an option agreed; pure upside to guidance, not in any forecast

The secondary horizon (18 months to three years). The narrative for 2028 to 2029 is dominated by three sector items (parity, entrants, the capacity ramps of NKT and Prysmian) and one company item (the 2028 ambition and whether acquisitions fill it). It barely touches the items that would decide Nexans's 2028 to 2029 earnings on our analysis: the mass-impregnated line's order book after mid-2028 (Deutsche Bank's reference to GREGY is the only pipeline name), the Grid capacity's margin at full utilisation, the Republic Wire margin after the tariff schedule resolves, and the balance-sheet deployment that P-02 shows decides the EPS path after FY2027. That is a narrative gap on both sides: the risk (an empty line from H2 2028) and the option (a near-zero net debt company by 2030 on the organic path).

What investors appear to focus on, from the second-hand evidence. Deutsche Bank's investor-call feedback (26 October) and Jefferies's "clients' minds" pieces suggest the questions are the order, the Transmission margin, Republic Wire's start, and the discount to Prysmian and NKT; DNB Carnegie's NKT notes suggest the sector's investors are focused on the capacity-overhang timetable; the dashboard's sector-wide technical rating of D or F and Nexans's 18th-percentile relative strength suggest positioning is light and sentiment poor. The absence of any activist after Quiñenco's exit, and a 34.84 percent vote against a Quiñenco director in May 2025, are the only register facts; neither shows an investor narrative pushing for capital returns.

Score reasoning. The question's score measures how favourable the gap is between our assessment and what the narrative reflects. The narrative has not absorbed the negative financial dynamics (EPS basis, cash, costs), which is unfavourable because they will be learned in February; it has also not absorbed the positive resolutions (the order's conversion odds, Grid's under-modelled capacity, the balance-sheet option), which is favourable; and the share price, unlike the brokers' models, already discounts the negatives. On balance a neutral-to-slightly-favourable gap, with the caveat that the investor side of the narrative is unobserved.
