Each layer's own material

What each layer was asked, what its scores mean, and the Investment Analyst's notes and appendices

Layer 1: Assessing the foundational components of an investment case

Written 25 October 2026, 12:10 to 12:33 UK, Opus, main login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to investigate and research each question, analyse the relevant information and form an insightful judgement regarding it, and to do the same for each group of questions as a whole, so as to best help the Consumer achieve their objective, which is to produce a well-reasoned, coherent, deeply understood and accurate operating model and financial model for the company for the next 18 months to four years.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

The Investment Analyst's title
Nexans (NEX-FR): first set of questions, the foundational components of the investment case

Written 25 October 2026 by the Investment Analyst answering the first set, for the Investment Analyst who builds the operating and financial model.

The Investment Analyst's opening notes

Reading notes for the Consumer: the state of play and eight traps in the source data

State of play at 25 October 2026. The share price is €113.60 (23 October), down from €133.20 at the 30 September memo and €122 on 21 October, the day before the nine-month trading update. The 52-week range is about €108 to €166. The 24-month-forward price-to-earnings ratio is 10.4x, the 5th percentile of the last five years and about the 22nd percentile of ten years (ten-year bands: 10th percentile 9.5x, median 12.3x, 75th 14.0x, 90th 15.8x). Market capitalisation is €5,113m and enterprise value €6,606m on FactSet's definition (debt includes leases). FactSet consensus (median) is adjusted EBITDA of €817m, €936m and €1,052m for FY2026 to FY2028 and brokers' adjusted earnings per share of €7.03, €9.13 and €10.88. Company guidance for FY2026 is adjusted EBITDA of €770m to €840m and free cash flow of €235m to €325m; the 2028 ambition is adjusted EBITDA of €1,150m plus or minus €75m. The nine-month update of 22 October 2026 beat on Grid and Connect organic growth, missed on Transmission organic growth, did not raise guidance, did not announce the replacement Mass Impregnated cable order, and disclosed no profit or cash figures; the shares fell about 7 percent over the next two days.

Eight traps the Consumer must not fall into when building the model:

  1. The Norwegian krone sensitivity runs the opposite way to what the 30 September memo says. The company's 2024 registration document states that a 10 percent fall in the krone raises continuing net income by €32m (about +9 percent of earnings per share), and the 2025 disclosure gives +€28m to net income with a €140m hit to equity. The mechanism is that Halden's costs are in krone while subsea project revenue is largely priced in euros, so a weaker krone widens the Transmission margin, while translation and hedge revaluation hit equity. The memo's statement that a krone fall "costs about €28m of net income" has the sign wrong. A 10 percent fall in the US dollar costs about €21m of net income.
  2. The FY2025 brokers'-basis earnings per share of €8.08 is not a continuing-operations base. It was struck before Autoelectric, Lynxeo and AmerCable were reclassified as discontinued; reported diluted continuing earnings per share for FY2025 were €4.75. The consensus "fall of 13 percent" into FY2026 is partly a perimeter effect, and the Consumer should rebuild FY2025 on a continuing basis before computing growth.
  3. The consensus revenue line mixes two bases. Some brokers forecast standard sales (revenue at standard metal prices, about €6.6bn to €6.8bn for FY2026) and others revenue at current metal prices (€8.3bn to €11.3bn). The median of €6,848m is a standard-sales figure set against a current-metal history (€7,810m in FY2025), so the "-12 percent" FY2026 revenue growth is an artefact. Model standard sales for margins and growth, and current-metal revenue only for working capital and gross margin.
  4. Brokers' earnings per share sit about 9 percent above consensus adjusted net income divided by diluted shares (FY2027: €376m over about 45m shares is €8.36, against €9.13). Some brokers appear to exclude amortisation of acquired intangibles. Build earnings per share from net income.
  5. H1 2026 reported continuing net income of €122.8m included a €75.2m non-cash Core exposure gain (metal inventory revalued as copper rose, against €10.9m in H1 2025). Underlying continuing net income therefore fell far more steeply than the reported 14 percent; the files do not explain the residual (reorganisation, acquisition costs and interest are the likely items). This is a research gap.
  6. The Electra cable-laying vessel cost €380m (earnings review, on budget), not the €150m in the Business Foundations digest.
  7. The Transmission backlog has been flat: €7.7bn at end-2025, €7.9bn at Q1 2026, €7.7bn at H1 2026 and €7.8bn at September 2026.
  8. FY2026 free cash flow guidance is €235m to €325m (raised in July from €210m to €310m); the memo's monitoring table still quotes the older range.

The Investment Analyst's group headings, as written Group: External change forces · Group: Internal change forces · Group: Industry and Value Chain · Group: Company - Competitive advantage · Group: Company - Economic strength · Group: Company - Management · Group: Risks · Group: Guidance · Group: Sell-side estimates · Group: Stock market narrative

Appendix Research requests the Investment Analyst raised for Layer 2

Research requests for the next set

Company: Nexans (NEX-FR), a French-listed cable maker that completed its exit from non-electrification businesses in July 2026; it reports three electrification segments, PWR-Transmission (high-voltage subsea and land cable systems with installation, including the Halden mass-impregnated line in Norway, the Charleston plant in the United States and three cable-laying vessels), PWR-Grid (medium- and low-voltage cables and accessories for distribution utilities) and PWR-Connect (low-voltage cables for buildings, industry and data centres, including Republic Wire in the United States since June 2026), plus a shrinking metallurgy segment (Other Activities).

Request: T-P1

Peers: Prysmian (the largest high-voltage maker, with a multi-site footprint, the Pikkala tower, new cable-laying vessels and integration cost programmes after General Cable and Encore Wire); NKT (its single-hub Karlskrona expansion enters commercial operation in 2027, the clearest current case of fixed-cost absorption on new high-voltage capacity, and its 2019-2020 losses are the down-cycle precedent); Cenergy Holdings and its subsidiary Hellenic Cables (a vessel-light cost model with sector-high margins and a doubled Corinth plant); Sumitomo Electric Industries (the Nigg greenfield subsea plant started in September 2026); LS Cable & System (new UK and US plants, a greenfield ramp comparison); Southwire (private; the US low-voltage cost leader that Republic Wire competes against); TF Kable (private, Poland; the Central European low- and medium-voltage cost benchmark for Connect and Grid); Pfisterer Holding (a listed accessories specialist, a cost-structure comparison for Nexans's medium-voltage accessories business). Inputs: (1) What share of each Nexans segment's cost base (Transmission, Grid, Connect) is fixed and what share variable once metal pass-through is excluded, and how does this compare with Prysmian and NKT; test the sensitivities Nexans published in its 2024 universal registration document (a 1 percentage point change in group revenue moving EBITDA by about €11m; a 50 basis point change in EBIT margin moving EBIT by about €34m). (2) How large the under-absorption cost is when a high-voltage line is idle or under-loaded: evidence from Nexans's Halden mass-impregnated line after the Great Sea Interconnector was halted in 2025-2026 (including Bernstein's published analysis of fixed-cost under-absorption at Halden), Nexans's closure of its Hanover land high-voltage plant, and NKT's and Prysmian's experience in 2016-2020. (3) The dated margin path of new high-voltage capacity from first cable to full volume: Nexans's Halden doubling (2024), Prysmian's Pikkala tower, NKT's Karlskrona (pre-operational costs published by DNB Carnegie at about 100 to 200 basis points of group margin in 2025-2026), Hellenic Cables' Corinth expansion; how long absorption took and what disrupted it. (4) The cost effect of owning installation vessels rather than chartering them: Nexans's Electra (in service June 2026, a €380m budget per the company), Prysmian's Monna Lisa and fleet programme, NKT's Eleonora (2027). (5) Nexans's past cost and restructuring programmes: the 2018 restructuring and removal of two management layers, the SHIFT programme (about €300m of structural EBITDA from 2018 to 2024 per the company), SHIFT AI, and the La Triveneta Cavi synergies (5.6x pre-synergy and 4.6x post-synergy EBITDA per the company, June 2024): what was promised, what was delivered, how long it took and which cost lines proved cuttable. (6) Whether Connect's European plant footprint, including the Nordic plants, could be consolidated in a prolonged construction downturn, and what peers have done in comparable downturns. (7) How far labour cost flexes with volume in European cable plants, and how quickly energy price increases reach conversion costs given that Nexans secures energy at least 18 months ahead (2025 universal registration document). (8) The depreciation and fixed-cost effect of Nexans's capacity programme: European Grid capacity up about 40 percent by 2028, medium-voltage accessories capacity up 39 percent from 2026 to 2028 for about €62m, the Charleroi high-voltage tower (in service mid-2027), the Morocco medium-voltage plant and the Lens recycling plant (company Q3 2026 presentation, 22 October 2026). (9) Republic Wire's cost structure and the delivery record on the €23m run-rate synergy target announced in April 2026 (split between purchasing and commercial synergies), against the US integration precedents of Prysmian (General Cable, Encore Wire) and Nexans's own earlier North American acquisitions. Focus: How much of the operating-profit growth the sell-side expects for Nexans in 2027 and 2028 comes from absorbing fixed costs (a loaded Halden line, new Grid capacity, the Electra vessel) rather than from price, and how much operating profit is lost for each half-year that the mass-impregnated line runs under-loaded; secondly, whether any material cost lever exists in Connect if European construction stays weak.

Request: T-P2

Peers: Prysmian (the high-voltage price leader and, with Southwire, the US low-voltage duopolist); NKT (a high-voltage pure play with the clearest published contract-vintage pricing evidence); Cenergy Holdings and Hellenic Cables (newly 525 kV-qualified in July 2026 and winning Greek awards from IPTO, Nexans's Great Sea Interconnector customer); Sumitomo Electric Industries (the new Nigg plant and the SSEN Shetland work); LS Cable & System (the first entrant to win European 525 kV subsea awards, with TenneT); Taihan Cable & Solution (a new subsea plant and vessel); Southwire (private; the US low-voltage price setter against Republic Wire); TF Kable (private, Poland; a Central European low- and medium-voltage price setter); Rexel and Sonepar (distributors, for channel evidence on low-voltage price and volume). Operating conditions: an industry sold out of high-voltage capacity (2022-2028); a high-voltage capacity overhang and price trough (2015-2020); supply and demand reaching parity or oversupply as new capacity lands, with newly qualified entrants bidding (about 2029-2031); transmission system operators prioritising cost over speed (TenneT's re-phasing, Elia's postponement and Energinet's pauses in 2026); sharp copper price rises and volatility (2021-2022 and 2025-2026); input shocks without escalation clauses (polymers, compounds, energy and freight after the 2026 Strait of Hormuz disruption, and the 2010 polymer spike); a prolonged European and Nordic residential construction downturn; US low voltage after the Section 232 tariffs and incumbent consolidation (Prysmian with Encore Wire and Atkore); a data-centre demand surge and a possible pause. Inputs: (1) How much of the rise in high-voltage contract value per kilometre (DNB Carnegie: about €0.62m for 2019-2020 awards to €1.44m for 2024-2026 awards on 525 kV direct-current projects) is price and how much scope, and whether Nexans's awards show the same rise as NKT's and Prysmian's. (2) Why Nexans's Transmission margin (13.7 percent in H1 2026, company) trails Prysmian's (21.2 percent in Q2 2026) and NKT's (20.2 percent in Q2 2026) on the same price regime: legacy framework contracts still in execution (Bernstein), utilisation, mix and installation costs; when Nexans's lower-priced legacy contracts finish executing. (3) The contract terms in Nexans's high-voltage and Grid contracts: metal indexation (the company states 83 to 85 percent of its business is indexed, with no lag on major contracts), indexation of non-metal costs, down-payments at award, capacity reservation fees, penalty and liquidated-damage terms, and what happens to price and payments when a customer delays a project, with the Great Sea Interconnector as the case. (4) How high-voltage pricing behaved in 2015-2020: how far contract prices fell, at what level of utilisation they broke, which makers cut first and how long recovery took. (5) The prices at which European high-voltage tenders have cleared since mid-2026, including LS Cable's TenneT awards and Hellenic Cables' IPTO awards, and any evidence of entrants bidding below incumbents in Europe or the United States. (6) Grid pricing: the price mechanics of long frameworks such as Enedis's seven-year €600m framework (February 2026), pass-through of polymer and energy costs, and the margin contribution of accessories. (7) Connect pricing: how quickly Nexans passes polymer, compound, energy and freight inflation through in low voltage, distributors' bargaining power, and the causes of the Connect margin falling from 13.6 percent to 11.8 percent between H1 2025 and H1 2026 (company). (8) US low-voltage pricing after the Section 232 tariffs (50 percent on imported copper cable), with Southwire's and Prysmian's scale; Republic Wire's pricing against the incumbents; the precedent of Atkore's margin compression. (9) What customers, competitors and named brokers (Bernstein, Jefferies, Deutsche Bank, DNB Carnegie, Morgan Stanley) say now about pricing on high-voltage awards for delivery after 2030, and about mass-impregnated cable against extruded cable. Focus: Whether the prices in Nexans's Transmission backlog will convert into margin as signed, how much of the margin gap to Prysmian and NKT closes mechanically by 2028, and at what price Nexans's next high-voltage awards, above all for its mass-impregnated line, will clear once entrants bid; secondly, how much pricing power Grid and Connect retain against unindexed input inflation and a weak construction market.

Request: T-P3

Why requested: Nexans's cash flow, plant utilisation and pricing are driven by linked loops (down-payments and backlog, scarcity and capacity additions, selectivity and market share, transmission operators' cost discipline and award timing) that will decide whether the 2026 slowdown in Transmission is a pause or the start of a balancing phase. Peers: Prysmian (positive working capital and capacity built only against customer down-payments); NKT (prepayment-funded expansion with deeply negative working capital, the strongest example of the down-payment loop and of its possible reversal); Cenergy Holdings and Hellenic Cables (an entrant whose awards test the scarcity loop); Siemens Energy (a grid backlog funded by advance payments, a comparator for prepayment reversal); LS Cable & System and Sumitomo Electric Industries (entrants whose capacity can close the scarcity loop). Inputs: (1) The down-payment loop: how far Nexans's free cash flow depends on Transmission down-payments (cash conversion of 83.1 percent in H1 2025 against 42.7 percent in H1 2026, company), and what happens to working capital when the Transmission backlog stops growing (€7.7bn at end-2025 and €7.8bn at September 2026, company). (2) The scarcity loop: high prices lead to capacity additions (NKT Karlskrona in 2027, Prysmian's towers and vessels, Hellenic Cables' Corinth, Sumitomo's Nigg, LS Cable's UK and US plants), which move the market towards parity (Morgan Stanley's published model dates parity to about 2030, with oversupply if European offshore wind installs about 13 GW a year). (3) The selectivity loop: value-over-volume order-taking protects margin but cedes share (a Prysmian strategy director's estimate that Nexans lost 3 to 4 points of European subsea share), shortening the backlog and raising utilisation risk. (4) The customer cost loop: higher prices lead transmission operators to phase projects and coordinate procurement (TenneT, Elia, Energinet; Germany's 2026 move back towards overhead lines for unawarded direct-current corridors), which reaches award timing and down-payment terms before it reaches price. (5) The Grid capacity loop: added capacity shortens lead times, wins frameworks and fills the new capacity (Nexans expects US accessories lead times to fall by up to about 60 percent, company, October 2026). (6) The US protection loop: Section 232 tariffs and Buy America rules favour local producers such as Republic Wire but block Nexans's Canadian rod. (7) The track-record loop: whether cable stocks have re-rated on confirmation of conservative guidance rather than on surprise. Focus: Which of these loops are reinforcing Nexans's earnings and cash today and which are turning into balancing loops for 2027-2029, and in particular whether new awards and frameworks will add down-payments faster than project execution consumes them.

Request: T-P4

Why requested: Nexans's earnings per share are unusually sensitive to items below EBITDA (perimeter restatements, metal revaluation, depreciation and interest from acquisitions and capacity, tax, and brokers' differing adjusted bases), which is where sell-side forecasts diverge most. Peers: Prysmian (a comparator for the tax rate, purchase-price amortisation after Encore Wire and adjusted earnings-per-share definitions); NKT (a comparator for tax normalisation, the depreciation step on new capacity and the treatment of hybrid capital in adjusted earnings); Cenergy Holdings (financing costs and depreciation on new plants); Siemens Energy (adjusted against reported earnings definitions). Inputs: (1) The continuing-operations FY2025 base: reported diluted continuing earnings per share of €4.75 against the brokers' adjusted €8.08 on FactSet, and how each broker rebuilds a continuing base after Autoelectric, Lynxeo and AmerCable were classified as discontinued. (2) Core exposure effects (metal inventory revaluation: +€75.2m in H1 2026 against +€10.9m in H1 2025, company) and their treatment in reported and adjusted earnings; what else explains the gap between reported continuing net income (€122.8m in H1 2026) and adjusted earnings. (3) The depreciation and amortisation path from Electra, Charleroi, Grid and accessories capacity, Morocco, Lens and the purchase-price amortisation of Republic Wire; whether brokers exclude acquired-intangible amortisation from adjusted earnings per share. (4) Interest: the €500m 4.25 percent bond due 2031 (September 2026), the €400m sustainability-linked bond maturing in April 2028 with a coupon step-up feature, leases, and net debt of €1,038m at June 2026 (company). (5) Tax: the reported effective rate (30.9 percent in FY2025 on FactSet) and the normal rate by country mix, including Norway's weight in profits. (6) Currency: the company's disclosed sensitivities (2024 universal registration document: a 10 percent fall in the krone raising continuing net income by about €32m; a 10 percent fall in the US dollar lowering it by about €21m) and how brokers model them. (7) Share count: performance-share dilution and anti-dilution buybacks (up to 176,005 shares, July 2026). (8) Antitrust: the €75m contingency provision (2025 annual report), the UK class action and other proceedings, and fines in comparable cable cases. (9) Capitalised Republic Wire integration costs (€23m) and reorganisation costs excluded from adjusted EBITDA. (10) Capital expenditure against the €1.2bn 2025-2028 plan (company, November 2024) and the Republic Wire earn-out of up to €43m. Focus: How far each below-EBITDA item will move Nexans's earnings per share and free cash flow between FY2025 and FY2028, what each named broker assumes and why broker earnings-per-share forecasts diverge so much more than their EBITDA forecasts, and what a clean continuing-operations earnings base is.

Layer 2: Translating the foundational components into an operating and financial model for the next 18 months, and 18 months to 3 years out

Written 26 October 2026, 00:12 to 00:31 UK, Opus, main login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to produce a well-reasoned, coherent, deeply understood and accurate operating and financial model for the company for the next 18 months to four years, based on the first set's answers, a base of the company's historical financial and operating performance, and how businesses in the sector and industry outperform or underperform, so as to best help the Consumer achieve their objective, which is to produce a trustworthy judgement regarding the predictability of delivery of the financial forecasts over the next 18 months.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the likely effect on the company's financial forecasts is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

The Investment Analyst's title
Nexans (NEX-FR): second set of questions, the operating and financial model

Written 25 October 2026 by the Investment Analyst answering the second set, for the Investment Analyst who answers the third and fourth sets (the predictability of the forecasts, and how far they are reflected in guidance, consensus and the market narrative).

The Investment Analyst's opening notes

Reading notes for the Consumer: the bases, the base case on one page, and where I differ from the first set

The bases (read before any figure). Unless a figure says otherwise: money in EUR millions; "standard sales" are the company's headline revenue at fixed metal prices (copper €5,000 per tonne, aluminium €1,200 per tonne), which is the basis for organic growth and margins; "revenue" or "current-metal revenue" is FactSet's revenue line (FY2025 €7,810m), which includes metal at market prices; "EBITDA" is the company's adjusted EBITDA, which is also the brokers' basis (FY2025 €728m) and excludes the Core exposure metal revaluation, reorganisation costs and share-based payments; "D&A" excludes amortisation of acquired intangibles; "adjusted net income" and "adjusted EPS" are built from adjusted EBITDA less D&A, net financial items, tax at the stated rate and minorities, over about 45.0m diluted shares; "FCF" is my full-basis free cash flow (adjusted EBITDA less capital spending, lease payments, cash interest, cash tax, reorganisation and integration cash, and the change in working capital, before dividends, acquisitions and buybacks).

My base case on one page (company adjusted basis; FY2023 to FY2025 actual, continuing operations, as restated by the company).

The FY2025 adjusted EPS of about €6.4 is my rebuild of a continuing-operations base (adjusted EBITDA €728m less D&A about €244m excluding acquired intangibles, net financial items about €60m, tax at 31 percent, minorities €5m, about 44.8m diluted shares); it is not a reported or FactSet figure. The base case includes no acquisition that has not been announced; P-02 and P-10 give the effect of the acquisitions management says it intends.

The nine things I would want to know first if I were the Consumer.

  1. FY2026 is a flat year for earnings per share on a like-for-like basis, not a 13 percent fall. The consensus "fall" from €8.08 to €7.03 compares a brokers' FY2025 base that still contained the businesses since sold with a continuing FY2026. On my continuing rebuild, adjusted EPS is about €6.4 in FY2025 and €6.38 in FY2026: EBITDA rises €77m, but D&A (+€41m excluding acquired intangibles) and net financial items (+€40m to €47m) absorb all of it, and a higher tax rate takes the rest. The earnings-per-share growth the market will pay for is FY2027 (+26 percent) and FY2028 (+19 percent).
  2. Depreciation is higher than the first set modelled, and higher than FactSet's consensus D&A line. H1 2026 D&A was €153.4m (Electra in service from the second quarter, Republic Wire from June). FactSet's consensus D&A line (€268m, €278m, €293m) is inconsistent with FactSet's own consensus EBITDA minus EBIT (€817m less €526m = €291m; €317m; €312m). The first set used the €268m line; I use €285m, €300m and €315m excluding acquired-intangible amortisation (about €15m to €18m a year on top). This alone lowers the first set's FY2027 EPS by about €0.30.
  3. My EBITDA is close to consensus; my EPS and FCF are not. FY2026 to FY2028 EBITDA is €805m, €909m and €1,013m against FactSet medians of €817m, €936m and €1,052m (-1, -3 and -4 percent). My pre-tax profit is within 1 percent of consensus pre-tax profit in FY2026 and FY2027 (€420m against €421m; €524m against €530m), because consensus carries more D&A. The EPS gap (-9, -12 and -12 percent against €7.03, €9.13 and €10.88) is mostly basis: about 9 points of it is the brokers' basis sitting above adjusted net income per diluted share (some brokers exclude acquired-intangible amortisation, use 43.7m basic shares or a 27 to 29 percent tax rate). Against consensus adjusted net income (€301m, €376m, €476m) I am -5, -4 and -10 percent.
  4. Free cash flow will convert only about 28 to 36 percent of EBITDA through FY2028, against 47 percent in FY2025 and the company's target of above 45 percent in 2028. The reasons are structural for the period: capital spending still at 4 to 6 percent of standard sales, lease payments of about €45m, interest of about €75m to €100m, tax at about 30 percent, about €30m to €60m a year of reorganisation and integration cash, and no down-payment tailwind while the Transmission backlog is flat (€7.7bn to €7.8bn for nine months). The company's 45 percent target is on a "normalised" basis that excludes reorganisation cash and strategic capital spending; on that basis my FY2028 is about 44 percent.
  5. The revenue line is dominated by copper, not by the business. With metal held at about the second-half 2026 level, revenue at current metal prices rises 26 percent in FY2026 (to €9,866m) on standard-sales growth of 9.4 percent and no profit effect. The model's revenue inputs are on the current-metal basis because the model's history is FactSet's current-metal revenue; its EBITDA margin input is therefore 8.2 to 9.1 percent, which corresponds to 12.1 to 13.6 percent on standard sales.
  6. FactSet's semi-annual history for H2 2025 is an artefact. FactSet shows H2 2025 revenue of €3,114m, EBITDA of €236m and a net loss of €158m because it subtracts an H1 2025 that was not restated for the discontinued businesses from a restated full year. The correct continuing H2 2025 figures are revenue at current metal prices of €3,833m (€7,810m less the restated H1 of €3,977m), standard sales of €3,004m and adjusted EBITDA of €356m. Read Part 3 of the forecast inputs against these, not against FactSet's semi-annual column.
  7. FactSet's history EBITDA for H1 2026 (€453m) includes the €75.2m Core exposure gain. The company's adjusted EBITDA was €387.7m. Use the company figure.
  8. FactSet's historical "free cash flow" line is operating cash flow less capital spending less dividends paid. For every year from FY2021 to FY2025 the difference between operating cash flow less capital spending and the stated free cash flow equals dividends paid exactly (€31m, €53m, €92m, €102m, €116m). The first set's free-cash-flow-to-net-income ratios (82, 61, 78, 179 and 126 percent) are therefore after dividends and understate conversion.
  9. The consensus FY2028 EBITDA gap to the company's ambition is acquisitions plus Transmission volume. The company's €1,150m plus or minus €75m includes "additional M&A". My organic €1,013m is €62m below the bottom of the range; closing to the midpoint needs about €140m of acquired EBITDA, or about €1.3bn of acquisitions at 9 to 10 times EBITDA, which the balance sheet can carry (P-02).

Where I agree with the first set, and where I differ. I agree with its structure of the case (Grid under-modelled, Connect over-modelled by consensus, Transmission volume dependent on unsigned orders, capital spending and tax light in consensus) and with its FY2026 EBITDA (I take the €805m guidance midpoint against its €810m). I differ on: D&A (higher, as above); FY2027 and FY2028 EBITDA (€909m and €1,013m against its €910m to €920m and €1,020m to €1,040m, because I hold Transmission revenue growth to 6 to 7 percent a year against its implied higher path and keep Connect's margin below 12 percent in FY2027 for unindexed input costs); EPS (€6.38, €8.04, €9.56 against its €6.95, €8.45, €10.10); and FCF (€258m and €362m in FY2027 and FY2028 against its €380m to €420m and €450m to €500m, because I deduct lease payments and reorganisation cash and assume no down-payment release).

Part 4. The reasoning behind the inputs

The basis of every input (read first). Revenue is FactSet's revenue at current metal prices (FY2025 €7,810m), because that is the model's history; the company's headline revenue is standard sales at fixed metal prices (FY2025 €6,098m). The EBITDA margin is the company's adjusted EBITDA (the brokers' basis; FY2025 €728m) divided by current-metal revenue, so it reads 8.2 to 9.1 percent where the company would say 12.1 to 13.6 percent on standard sales. Metal prices are held at about the second-half 2026 level, so current-metal revenue is 1.50 times standard sales from H2 2026 (1.46 times in H1 2026; 1.28 times in FY2025). The operating drivers behind the inputs

  • Transmission: organic growth -4 percent in FY2026 (Q3 -4.0 percent; Q4 against a +40 percent comparison), +6 percent in FY2027 and +7 percent in FY2028 (replacement mass-impregnated order assumed signed by the end of Q1 2027 and loading Halden to mid-2028; LanWin2 and BalWin3 525 kV in production; Mallorca-Menorca from mid-2027; Charleroi 525 kV land capacity from mid-2027; Electra booked to end-2028), +2 percent in FY2029 and FY2030 (no visible mass-impregnated work beyond mid-2028; entrants bidding for post-2029 slots). Margin from 13.7 percent (H1 2026) to 16.6 percent (FY2028) as legacy contracts finish and Electra replaces chartered vessels, then flat. No Great Sea Interconnector revenue (excluded from guidance; a restart is upside).
  • Grid: organic growth about 8 percent in FY2026 (Q3 +14.8 percent), 9 percent in FY2027, 8 percent in FY2028, then 6 and 5 percent; European capacity +40 percent by 2028, accessories +39 percent (€62m), Enedis volumes doubled. Margin held at 15.8 to 16.0 percent in FY2026 and FY2027 by start-up costs and the 2027 energy step, then 16.4 to 16.5 percent on accessories mix.
  • Connect: organic growth about 7.5 percent in FY2026, then 3.5 to 4 percent (North American data centres up; European construction flat to 2027-28); Republic Wire scope June 2026 to May 2027. Margin 11.5 percent in FY2026 and 11.8 percent in FY2027 (Nordic weakness, about €12m to €15m of unindexed polymer, compound and energy cost in FY2027), rising to 12.6 to 13.0 percent with synergies and input-cost recovery.
  • Other Activities: metallurgy shrinking by design; Lens recycling ramps from 2027; EBITDA about €20m.
  • Group: Electrification organic growth about 5 percent a year to FY2028, at the top of the company's 3 to 5 percent framework.
  • Depreciation (excluding acquired intangibles): €285m, €300m, €315m, €325m, €330m. H1 2026 total D&A was €153.4m (Electra from June, Republic Wire from June); FactSet's consensus EBITDA minus EBIT implies €291m, €317m and €312m. Acquired-intangible amortisation of about €15m to €18m a year (Republic Wire purchase-price allocation, still provisional) is excluded.
  • Net financial items: FY2026 includes acquisition debt, the bridge loan and a foreign-exchange hedging loss in H1 2026; then falling as net debt falls and the €400m 5.5 percent bond is repaid from cash in April 2028. All debt is fixed-rate.
  • Tax: 30.5 percent in FY2026 (H1 2026 32.7 percent, FY2025 30.9 percent), 30 percent in FY2027 and FY2028, 29.5 percent from FY2029 as US and Norwegian profit weigh more. Minorities about €5m to €6m a year (not a model input; deducted in my cross-check).
  • Dividend: progressive policy, payout at least 30 percent of recurring net income; €2.90 for FY2025; my payout is about 40 to 49 percent of adjusted EPS.
  • Share count: about +0.20m shares a year from free and performance-share vesting (1.29m outstanding, 2.95 percent of capital), offset by anti-dilution buybacks (€40m in FY2026 including the July 2026 programme of up to 176,005 shares; €25m a year after), so the basic count stays about 43.7m and the diluted count about 45.0m.
  • Capital spending: €390m in FY2026 (H1 €191.3m; Electra completion, Charleroi, Republic Wire's expansion), then €320m and €300m a year: above consensus (€309m and €283m for FY2027 and FY2028) because the 2025-28 plan of €1.2bn predates the €62m accessories programme, Morocco, Lens and Republic Wire.
  • Working capital: the percentage is set on the model's current-metal revenue and FactSet's balance (receivables plus inventories less payables: €846m at end-2025) so that the change it implies equals the cash movement I expect: an inflow of about €116m in FY2026 (H1 2026 inflow €121.1m; H2 neutral) and outflows of about €30m to €35m a year after. The percentage falls from 10.8 percent to 7.4 percent because (a) revenue is inflated about 26 percent by copper while the company's operating working capital includes metal-linked supplier payables (€449m under negotiated arrangements at end-2025) and customer down-payments, which FactSet's measure excludes, and (b) Republic Wire's working capital (about €90m) was paid inside the acquisition price and is not an operating cash outflow. The company's own operating working capital is about 1 percent of standard sales. If the model is rebuilt on a true end-2026 FactSet balance (about €900m to €950m), the percentage should be about 9.5 percent and the acquired working capital moved to acquisitions.
  • Lease payments: lease liabilities €178m at June 2026 (€161m at end-2025); payments of about €42m rising with the asset base.
  • Acquisitions less disposals: FY2026 -€485m (Republic Wire about -€675m in H1, Autoelectric proceeds about +€190m in H2; enterprise value €207m); FY2028 -€40m (Republic Wire earn-out, up to €43m). No unannounced acquisition is included, although management calls acquisitions a central capital-allocation priority: each €500m at 9 to 10 times EBITDA adds about €50m to €55m of EBITDA, about €20m of interest and about €0.38 of FY2028 EPS.
  • Currency: spot rates held; the krone is 26.6 percent of standard sales (a 10 percent fall raises net income by about €28m to €32m); a 10 percent fall in the US dollar cuts net income by about €21m.

The bridge from the company's own reported figures to the basis consensus uses

  • Revenue. The company headlines standard sales (FY2025 €6,098m; H1 2026 €3,248.6m). FactSet's history and roughly half of the brokers use revenue at current metal prices (FY2025 €7,810m; H1 2026 €4,736.0m); the other half forecast standard sales. The FactSet median (€6,848m for FY2026) is a standard-sales figure, so its "-12 percent" growth against €7,810m is an artefact. Brokers on the current-metal basis range from €8,323m to €11,338m for FY2026 (Deutsche Bank €9,241m); my €9,866m sits inside that range with copper at the H2 2026 level.
  • EBITDA. Company adjusted EBITDA (the brokers' basis) excludes the Core exposure metal revaluation, reorganisation costs, share-based payments (about €9m in H1 2026) and disposal results. FactSet's history line is "operating" EBITDA: €717m for FY2025 (close to €728m) but €453m for H1 2026, because it includes the €75.2m Core exposure gain. My margins are on the company adjusted basis.
  • FactSet's semi-annual history for H2 2025 is wrong for this purpose. FactSet shows H2 2025 revenue €3,114m, EBITDA €236m and net income -€158m because it subtracts an H1 2025 not restated for the businesses since sold from a restated full year. The correct continuing H2 2025 figures are current-metal revenue €3,833m (€7,810m less the restated H1 2025 of €3,977.2m), standard sales €3,004m (€6,098m less €3,093.5m) and adjusted EBITDA €356m (€728m less €371.7m). My Part 3 growth rates are against these; applied to FactSet's €3,114m they would understate H2 2026 revenue by about €960m. The absolute figures intended are: current-metal revenue €5,130m (H2 2026), €5,333m (H1 2027) and €5,444m (H2 2027); adjusted EBITDA €417m, €439m and €470m.
  • Earnings per share. Four bases circulate. (1) Reported diluted continuing EPS: €4.75 for FY2025 (€7.84 including discontinued operations). (2) FactSet adjusted diluted (before its "unusual" items): €2.76 for FY2025. (3) Brokers' adjusted (consensus): €8.08 for FY2025, struck before Autoelectric, Lynxeo and AmerCable were reclassified, so not a continuing base; €7.03, €9.13 and €10.88 for FY2026 to FY2028. (4) My adjusted EPS: adjusted EBITDA less D&A excluding acquired intangibles, net financial items, tax and minorities, over about 45.0m diluted shares; about €6.4 for FY2025 continuing and €6.38 for FY2026. The brokers' figures sit about 9 percent above consensus adjusted net income per diluted share (FY2027: €376m over 45.0m is €8.36 against €9.13) because some brokers exclude acquired-intangible amortisation, some use 43.7m basic shares, and the implied tax rate is about 27 to 29 percent. From my adjusted to reported continuing EPS in FY2026: less reorganisation after tax (about €1.0 a share; €34.3m pre-tax in H1 alone), less acquired-intangible amortisation after tax (about €0.25), plus the Core exposure gain after tax (about €1.2 to €1.5 at current copper); in a year when copper falls, reported EPS will sit well below adjusted.
  • Free cash flow. Three definitions: the company's (FY2025 €344m; FY2026 guidance €235m to €325m; the 2028 conversion target is on a normalised basis excluding reorganisation cash and strategic capital spending); FactSet's historical line, which for FY2021 to FY2025 equals operating cash flow less capital spending less dividends paid (the difference equals dividends exactly each year); and the consensus estimate (€309m, €439m, €534m), each broker's own definition and, on the evidence of consensus operating cash flow less capital spending, mostly before lease payments and part of interest. My figure is after capital spending, lease payments, cash interest, cash tax and reorganisation and integration cash, before dividends, acquisitions and buybacks.
  • Net debt. The company excludes leases (€1,038.2m at June 2026; 1.4 times); FactSet includes them (1.51 times; debt €2,880m in its snapshot).

Appendix Research requests the Investment Analyst raised for Layer 3 and Layer 4

Research requests for the next set

Company: Nexans (NEX-FR), a French-listed cable maker that completed its exit from non-electrification businesses in July 2026; it reports PWR-Transmission (high-voltage subsea and land cable systems with installation: the Halden plant in Norway with its mass-impregnated and extruded lines, Charleston in the United States, Charleroi in Belgium and three cable-laying vessels including Electra), PWR-Grid (medium-voltage cables and accessories for distribution utilities), PWR-Connect (low-voltage cables for buildings, industry and data centres, including Republic Wire in the United States since June 2026) and Other Activities (metallurgy and copper rod).

Request: T-D1

Peers: Prysmian (the largest high-voltage maker, whose Transmission margin reached 21.2 percent in Q2 2026, and the US low-voltage incumbent with Encore Wire and Atkore); NKT (a high-voltage pure play whose Transmission margin rose from 15.3 to 20.2 percent in a year on newer contracts, with Karlskrona capacity from Q2 2027); Cenergy Holdings and Hellenic Cables (vessel-light, 525 kV qualified in July 2026, winning IPTO awards in Nexans's Mediterranean region); Sumitomo Electric Industries (Nigg subsea plant opened October 2026); LS Cable & System (first entrant to win European 525 kV subsea awards, from TenneT; new UK and US plants); Southwire (private; the US building-wire leader Republic Wire competes against); Pfisterer Holding (listed accessories specialist, a margin benchmark for Grid accessories); Siemens Energy (a grid backlog converting into margin, a comparator for backlog conversion) Operating conditions: an industry sold out of high-voltage capacity (2022 to 2028); supply and demand reaching parity around 2029 to 2031 as incumbents' and newly qualified entrants' capacity lands; transmission system operators prioritising cost over speed (TenneT re-phasing, Elia postponement, Energinet pauses, Germany's 2026 move back towards overhead lines); a prolonged European and Nordic residential construction downturn; non-metal input cost shocks after the 2026 Strait of Hormuz disruption; US low voltage after the Section 232 tariffs and incumbent consolidation; a data-centre demand surge and a possible financing-led pause; record copper prices Inputs: (1) The Transmission EBITDA margin rising from 13.7 percent in H1 2026 (company) towards the company's 17 percent target for 2028 as legacy contracts finish and the Electra vessel replaces chartered vessels: which cable makers have converted a legacy-priced high-voltage backlog to higher margins, how long it took, and whether any did so with a backlog of about four to five years of sales and one technology line (mass-impregnated) dependent on a single unsigned replacement order; precedents include NKT 2018 to 2021, Prysmian 2018 to 2021 and Nexans 2013 to 2018. (2) PWR-Grid growing above the company's mid-single-digit guideline on about 40 percent more European capacity by 2028 and 39 percent more medium-voltage accessories capacity from 2026 to 2028 (company, 22 October 2026): how quickly peers' new medium-voltage and accessories capacity filled, and at what margin during the ramp. (3) Republic Wire: €23.6m of run-rate synergies within three years (company) for an entrant with about 4 percent of US building wire against integrated incumbents; the record of European cable makers' US acquisitions, including Nexans's own earlier North American acquisitions and Prysmian's General Cable and Encore Wire. (4) Recovery of the PWR-Connect margin from 11.8 percent in H1 2026 (13.6 percent a year earlier) without a European construction upturn. (5) Free-cash-flow conversion above 45 percent of adjusted EBITDA by 2028 (company target) after 83.1 percent in H1 2025 and 42.7 percent in H1 2026, when the earlier figure was lifted by Transmission down-payments. (6) A leadership team new in every senior role (chief executive from October 2025, finance director from January 2026, chief operating officer from November 2026) delivering targets set by its predecessors. The demonstrated strengths to test against these: the SHIFT selectivity programme (about €300m of structural EBITDA from 2018 to 2024, company), Electra delivered on time and on budget, 525 kV extruded subsea cable moved into production for TenneT's LanWin2 (October 2026), and La Triveneta Cavi synergies delivered on schedule. Focus: Whether Nexans's expected 2027 and 2028 earnings rest on strengths it has demonstrated (converting contract vintage into margin, delivering capacity on schedule, integrating European acquisitions) or on strengths it has not yet shown (keeping the mass-impregnated line loaded after mid-2028, competing in US low voltage, restoring Connect margins without a construction recovery), and what the precedents say about each.

Request: T-D2

Peers: Prysmian (multi-segment comparator whose Transmission, Grid and US low-voltage drivers overlap Nexans's); NKT (high-voltage concentration, the clearest case of earnings resting on one or two drivers, and of down-payment-funded working capital); Cenergy Holdings and Hellenic Cables (single-region subsea exposure, capacity doubling); LS Cable & System and Taihan Cable & Solution (entrants whose bidding sets post-2029 prices); Southwire (private; sets US building-wire prices); Siemens Energy (advance-payment-funded grid backlog; a comparator for working-capital reversal) Operating conditions: sold-out high-voltage capacity through 2028; parity or oversupply around 2030; transmission system operator project re-phasing and procurement coordination; European construction downturn; non-metal input-cost shock in 2026 to 2027; US tariff protection and possible price normalisation; record and volatile copper prices; rising interest costs after acquisitions Inputs: (1) How concentrated the drivers of Nexans's 2025 to 2028 EBITDA growth are among Transmission margin conversion, the Republic Wire acquisition and new Grid capacity, and how sensitive each is to one event. (2) The Halden mass-impregnated line: preferred-bidder status announced July 2026 for a replacement order said to load the line to mid-2028, unsigned at the 22 October 2026 update; the earnings cost of each half-year of under-loading (Bernstein put the Great Sea Interconnector halt at about €100m of lost annual sales at Halden, 19 June 2026); and how often preferred-bidder positions in subsea cable slip or fall away. (3) The items between EBITDA and earnings per share: depreciation (€153.4m in H1 2026 against €101.1m in H1 2025, company), net debt of €1,038m at June 2026 and the €500m 4.25 percent bond issued September 2026, a reported tax rate of 30.9 percent in FY2025 and 32.7 percent in H1 2026; why broker FY2027 EPS estimates range from €7.72 to €11.78 while FY2027 EBITDA estimates range only from €898m to €1,012m (FactSet, 23 October 2026). (4) Free cash flow when the Transmission backlog is flat (€7.7bn at end-2025, €7.8bn at September 2026, company) and down-payments no longer exceed those consumed. (5) Input costs not indexed to contracts: energy bought at least 18 months ahead (2025 universal registration document), polymers and compounds, and the 2010 precedent when about 70 percent of a polymer price rise was recovered. (6) Copper at a record: its effect on revenue at current metal prices, working capital and the Core exposure revaluation (+€75.2m in H1 2026). (7) The historical dispersion of cable makers' results against initial guidance and consensus, including Nexans's 2013 profit warnings and 2018 crisis, Prysmian's Western Link (2018) and NKT's 2018 to 2020 losses: how often forecasts resting on one or two drivers were met. Focus: How wide and how skewed the range of Nexans's 2027 EBITDA, earnings per share and free cash flow outcomes is, and how much of that range hinges on the mass-impregnated replacement order, the pace of Transmission margin conversion and US low-voltage pricing.

Request: T-I1

Peers: Prysmian (Q3 2026 results on 29 October 2026; a conservative guider that raised 2026 guidance twice); NKT (Q3 2026 results on 19 November 2026; record of beating initial EBITDA guidance); Siemens Energy (2030 targets on 11 November 2026; read-across on grid pricing and backlog conversion); Cenergy Holdings (transmission tender results in H2 2026); Rexel (distributor read-across on European and US low-voltage cable price and volume); Legrand and Schneider Electric (data-centre and building electrical demand read-across) Operating conditions: high-voltage pricing on a plateau with executed margins still rising; transmission operators re-phasing awards; European construction contracting; North American data-centre demand strong with financing stress among neoclouds; non-metal input inflation after the Strait of Hormuz disruption; copper at a record; a risk-off market regime with rising bond yields Inputs: (1) FY2026 adjusted EBITDA guidance of €770m to €840m (raised 29 July 2026, confirmed 22 October 2026) against a FactSet median of €817m (25 October 2026), and free-cash-flow guidance of €235m to €325m against FactSet's €309m and Jefferies' compiled €258m. (2) The first FY2027 guidance, due with the FY2026 results on 24 February 2027, against a FactSet FY2027 EBITDA median of €936m: the company's record of initial guidance against outcomes (FY2022 €580m to €600m, outcome €599m; FY2023 €640m to €700m, €665m; FY2024 €670m to €730m, €804m as first reported; FY2026 initial €730m to €810m, called "surprisingly light" by Jefferies). (3) The 2028 ambition of €1,150m plus or minus €75m, which the company says includes additional acquisitions, against a FactSet FY2028 median of €1,052m; whether a capital markets update is planned for 2027 and what peers did at theirs. (4) Revision momentum: FactSet mean EPS for FY2026 down 2.8 percent and FY2027 down 2.6 percent over one month to 23 October 2026, with 69 percent buy ratings. (5) What peers' Q3 2026 results and guidance imply for transmission margins, award pricing, down-payment terms, European construction and US low-voltage prices. (6) How brokers model depreciation, interest, tax and the share count, since these drive most of the dispersion in EPS estimates. (7) The 22 October 2026 nine-month update's absence of profit and cash data, and what management said about the Q4 Transmission comparison and the mass-impregnated order. Focus: Where Nexans's first FY2027 guidance is likely to sit against consensus on 24 February 2027, and whether consensus EBITDA, earnings per share and free cash flow are more likely to be raised, held or cut over the FY2026 results, the H1 2027 results and the following update.

Request: T-I2

Peers: Prysmian, NKT, Cenergy Holdings and Hellenic Cables (the listed cable group whose narrative moves with Nexans); Siemens Energy (the grid-equipment narrative); LS Cable & System and Sumitomo Electric Industries (the entrant narrative); Pfisterer Holding (the accessories narrative) Operating conditions: the market rewarding proven margin conversion and pausing where the earnings step is signed but not shown; capacity-overhang worries marking cable makers down years before prices fall; a risk-off regime with rising yields and French sovereign stress; the data-centre theme questioned at its financing Inputs: (1) Transmission margin conversion as legacy contracts finish and Electra replaces chartered vessels, against peers' 20 to 21 percent. (2) The unsigned replacement mass-impregnated order for Halden and the line's utilisation after mid-2028. (3) PWR-Grid growth above the company's guideline on new capacity (Q3 2026 organic growth of 14.8 percent, company). (4) PWR-Connect margin pressure from Nordic construction and unindexed polymer and energy costs after the Strait of Hormuz disruption. (5) Republic Wire's integration and the risk of US low-voltage price normalisation. (6) The step-up in depreciation, interest and tax that makes FY2026 earnings per share fall while EBITDA rises, and the difference between the brokers' EPS basis and continuing-operations earnings. (7) Free-cash-flow conversion and the end of the Transmission down-payment tailwind. (8) Capital allocation: acquisitions against share buybacks, with the shares at the 5th percentile of their five-year 24-month-forward price-to-earnings range (10.4 times, FactSet, 23 October 2026). (9) High-voltage supply-demand parity around 2030 and newly qualified entrants. (10) Copper's effect on revenue and reported profit. (11) A possible restart of the Great Sea Interconnector after Meridiam's agreement to take a 66 percent stake (5 August 2026). Focus: Which of these dynamics sell-side analysts and investors discuss widely, which only one or two discuss, and which nobody discusses; above all whether the below-EBITDA drag, free-cash-flow conversion, Halden's utilisation after mid-2028 and the capital-allocation choice are understood or overlooked.

Request: OWN-1

Question: What is the status, value, technology, delivery schedule and contract structure (down-payments, reservation fees, liquidated damages) of the replacement mass-impregnated subsea cable order for which Nexans (NEX-FR) announced preferred-bidder status in July 2026, what have the customer, regulators, governments and the press said about it since, and how long have comparable preferred-bidder positions at Nexans, Prysmian and NKT taken to convert into signed contracts, and how often have they fallen away?

Request: OWN-2

Question: How do the brokers covering Nexans (NEX-FR) build adjusted earnings per share from adjusted EBITDA for 2026 to 2028: what depreciation and amortisation (including the Electra vessel, Charleroi and the Republic Wire purchase-price allocation), net financial items, tax rate, minorities and share count does each assume, which brokers exclude amortisation of acquired intangibles or use basic rather than diluted shares, and how does each rebuild a continuing-operations 2025 base after Autoelectric, Lynxeo and AmerCable were classified as discontinued?

Request: OWN-3

Question: How has Nexans's (NEX-FR) operating working capital evolved from 2021 to H1 2026, including customer down-payments and other contract liabilities, inventories at record copper prices and copper supplier-payment arrangements (€449m at end-2025, company), how does the company define free cash flow and normalised free cash flow, and what have the company, brokers and peers (NKT, Prysmian, Siemens Energy) said about working capital and cash conversion when high-voltage backlogs stop growing?

Request: OWN-4

Question: What has happened to US building-wire and data-centre cable prices, volumes and capacity since the 2026 Section 232 copper tariffs, how are Southwire, Prysmian (with Encore Wire and Atkore) and other US producers behaving on price and capacity, and what evidence exists on Republic Wire's commercial performance, capacity expansion and synergy delivery under Nexans (NEX-FR) ownership since June 2026?

Request: OWN-5

Question: How exposed is Nexans (NEX-FR) in 2027 to European electricity, gas, polymer, compound and freight cost increases following the 2026 Strait of Hormuz disruption, given its policy of buying energy at least 18 months ahead and its single source (Borealis) for cross-linked polyethylene compounds, and what have Prysmian, NKT, other European cable makers and electrical distributors said about passing these costs into 2027 prices and frameworks?

Request: OWN-6

Question: What have Nexans's (NEX-FR) management, board and major shareholders said since January 2026 about capital allocation after the Republic Wire acquisition (the acquisition pipeline, target multiples, leverage tolerance, share buybacks and the timing of a capital markets update), how has the shareholder register changed since Quiñenco reduced its stake to about 4 percent, and how have comparable European industrial companies with similar deleveraging profiles chosen between acquisitions and buybacks?

Layer 3 and Layer 4: Assessing the predictability of the financial forecasts for the next 18 months / Assessing the extent to which our assessment of the foundational components and financial forecasts is reflected or not in current company guidance, sell-side estimates and stock market narrative, primarily for the next 18 months and secondarily for 18 months to 3 years out

Written 27 October 2026, 20:15 to 20:39 UK, Fable, ricardinho login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to produce a robustly tested assessment of the predictability of the financial forecasts for the next 18 months, based on the first and second sets' answers.

The objective is to produce that judgement, based on the first and second sets' answers and your answers to the third set, for the Investment Analyst who writes the investment memo, so that Richard, who reads the memo, can form his judgement on the investment case.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each judgement on a group or set with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For the third set's questions, the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for the predictability of the company's financial forecasts over the next 18 months, and so for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

For the fourth set's questions, the score says how favourable for an investment case in the company the gap is between our assessment and what current guidance, sell-side estimates and the stock market narrative already reflect: 100 the most favourable, 1 the least, 50 neither.

The Investment Analyst's title
Nexans (NEX-FR): third and fourth sets of questions, the predictability of the forecasts and how far they are reflected in guidance, consensus and the narrative

Written 27 October 2026 by the Investment Analyst answering the third and fourth sets, for the Investment Analyst who writes the investment memo for Richard.

The Investment Analyst's opening notes

Reading notes for the Consumer

A. The model's cross-check gaps, explained, and the inputs I change

The one-page summary flags that the model's earnings per share sit 13 to 14 percent below the second set's cross-check in every year (€5.50 against €6.38 for FY2026, €7.04 against €8.04, €8.47 against €9.56, €9.26 against €10.41, €9.92 against €11.12) and that free cash flow differs by between +€38m and -€62m. Every one of these gaps has the same single cause, and none of them reflects an operating judgement that differs between the model and the second set.

The cause is the FactSet half-year artefact the second set warned about (its reading note 6), which the model has applied through the revenue base. FactSet's semi-annual history shows H2 2025 revenue of €3,114m because it subtracts an unrestated H1 2025 (€3,977m) from a restated full year (€7,810m); the correct continuing H2 2025 figure is €3,833m. The model's FY2025 base is the sum of its two half-years, €7,091m, not the annual €7,810m. Applying the second set's FY2026 revenue growth of 26.3 percent to €7,091m gives €8,956m, which is exactly the model's FY2026 revenue (the summary shows it as +14.7 percent against €7,810m). The second set intended €9,866m. The €910m shortfall then compounds through every later year at the second set's growth rates (the model's revenue is 9.2 percent below the intended figure in each of FY2026 to FY2030: €9,780m against €10,776m, €10,298m against €11,345m, and so on), and because the EBITDA margin input is a percentage of revenue, EBITDA is 9.2 percent short in every year: €731m against the intended €805m for FY2026, €825m against €909m, €920m against €1,013m, €964m against €1,063m, €1,000m against €1,103m. Everything below EBITDA in the model is as the second set specified (depreciation €285m, net financial items -€100m, tax 30.5 percent for FY2026), so the earnings gap is the EBITDA gap less tax: €74m of EBITDA less 30.5 percent tax is €51m of net income, or about €1.18 a share on 43.7m shares, which is the FY2026 gap of €1.18 (€6.68 expected on the model's basis less €5.50). The same arithmetic explains each later year.

The half-year phasing note on the summary ("the periods were moved by -1.5 points to match the year") is a second symptom of the same family of artefact: the model's H1 2026 EBITDA is FactSet's €453m, which includes the €75.2m Core exposure gain, where the company's adjusted figure is €387.7m; to land the year on the (understated) annual total the model has pushed the H2 2026 margin down to about 6.6 percent against the input of 8.13 percent. The annual figures are unaffected by this second artefact; only the H2 2026 and, by difference, the H1 2027 period figures are distorted.

What this means for the cross-check once corrected. With revenue at the intended level, the model should show earnings per share of about €6.68, €8.39, €9.97, €10.85 and €11.57 for FY2026 to FY2030 (on about 43.7m basic shares and before minorities), which is 4 to 5 percent above the second set's cross-check (€6.38, €8.04, €9.56, €10.41, €11.12, on 45.0m diluted shares and after €5m to €6m of minorities); that residual is the definitional difference the second set said to expect. Free cash flow should come to about €261m, €267m, €375m, €421m and €457m, between €8m and €22m above the second set's €239m, €258m, €362m, €412m and €449m, because the model carries no reorganisation cash (€45m, €30m, €25m, €20m, €20m) and deducts the full net financial items where the second set deducts cash interest about €10m to €20m lower; again the residual the second set predicted. The model's FY2026 free cash flow is currently €38m above the second set's rather than €22m because the working-capital percentage applied to understated revenue produces an inflow of €183m rather than the intended €116m, which more than offsets the lower EBITDA; from FY2027 the model is below the second set because the EBITDA shortfall dominates. Both gaps close with the revenue correction.

The inputs I change (written in full in inputs-changed.md). (1) Part 1, FY2026 revenue growth: from 26.3 percent to 39.1 percent, which is €9,866m against the model's own base of €7,091m; the FY2027 to FY2030 growth rates are unchanged because they chain from FY2026. (2) Part 3, revenue growth for the half-year to 31 December 2026: from 33.8 percent to 64.7 percent, which is €5,130m against FactSet's H2 2025 of €3,114m, so that the half-year and annual inputs agree and the model needs no re-phasing; the H1 2027 and H2 2027 rates are unchanged because their bases (H1 2026 €4,736m and the now-correct H2 2026) are right. The cleaner alternative, which I recommend to the Financial Analyst, is to overwrite the two history cells (H2 2025 revenue to €3,833m and H1 2026 EBITDA to €387.7m) and keep the second set's original inputs; if the history is corrected, the original 26.3 percent and 33.8 percent are the right inputs and mine are wrong, so the two corrections must not be combined. (3) Part 2, acquisitions less disposals in FY2028: from -€40m to -€86m, for the reason given in note C below (the earn-out liability the company has recognised is €86m, not the €43m headline); this changes net debt only. I change no operating input: my answers below support the second set's margins, growth rates, depreciation, financial items and tax, with the caveats on depreciation and the 2027 input-cost step recorded in D-01.

Two consequences for the summary's other figures. The "Viewforth against consensus" revenue line (+30.8 percent) is meaningless because the FactSet median revenue is a standard-sales figure (€6,848m) against our current-metal forecast; after the correction it will read about +44 percent and should be ignored. The like-for-like comparison is against the brokers on the current-metal basis, whose FY2026 figures run from €8,323m to €11,338m (Deutsche Bank €9,241m): our €9,866m sits inside that range. The EBITDA line will read about -1.5, -2.9, -3.7 and -5.4 percent against consensus for FY2026 to FY2029 once corrected, not -10.6 to -14.2 percent. The return figures (a 14.3x two-year-forward multiple today, +34.7 percent to December 2027 and +50.1 percent to June 2029 at the 15.82x guideline multiple) are computed on the understated earnings and must be re-run: on corrected model earnings the current two-year-forward multiple is about 12x, so the return at any given fair multiple is higher than shown, and the Dupont split (dividends +5.7 percent, multiple -80.8 percent, earnings +573 percent) is not interpretable until then.

B. What the new research adds, in one paragraph each

  • The replacement mass-impregnated order (OWN-1). The Mallorca-Menorca 3 award of 22 October 2026 uses extruded XLPE alternating-current cable (Spanish regulatory filing, 22 October 2026; Bernstein, 22 October 2026), so it is not the July 2026 preferred-bidder mass-impregnated order; that order is a separate, still unannounced Mediterranean project for which Deutsche Bank (26 October 2026) reports engineering preparation already under way, an award expected by late 2026 and loading of Halden's mass-impregnated line through 2027 and to mid-2028. The line is about 20 percent of Transmission revenue (Deutsche Bank). Across the three European majors, preferred-bidder positions have converted into signed contracts in 2 to 30 months, 9.6 months on average, and none has fallen away in the last five years. The deep-water mass-impregnated market is a Nexans-Prysmian duopoly (a 525 kV mass-impregnated system was qualified at 3,000 metres in August 2026), but the installed base of extruded subsea cable is projected to equal mass-impregnated cable by 2030.
  • The brokers' earnings bridge (OWN-2). Brokers differ on share count (Bernstein and Hana on 43.7m basic; Deutsche Bank on 44.9m diluted), on depreciation (Deutsche Bank €318m for FY2026 and €327m for FY2027 in total; Hana €188m and €247m, which cannot be reconciled with the reported H1 2026 figure of €153.4m) and on the 2025 base (Bernstein's €8.34 "adjusted" FY2025 EPS against continuing basic EPS of €4.90; restated continuing net income was €219m, discontinued €138m). The Republic Wire purchase price was €752m: €662m of net cash plus €86m of earn-out liabilities; provisional goodwill is €474m; customer relationships are amortised over 14 years. The H1 2026 tax rate was 32.66 percent against a theoretical 25.83 percent; Deutsche Bank expects the asset-purchase structure of Republic Wire to yield tax benefits over time.
  • Working capital and cash (OWN-3). Contract liabilities (customer down-payments) rose to €1,137m at end-2025 from €1,004m and €738m in the two prior years; copper supplier-payment arrangements to €449m from €341m and €253m; inventories rose to €1,719m at June 2026 from €1,366m and receivables to €1,546m from €1,057m. Management conceded (19 February 2026) that the 47 percent conversion in 2025 benefited from above-average down-payments. Nexans moved its cash target from a normalised to a full basis in February 2025 (strategic capital spending included), and the Q3 2026 presentation states the 2028 target of above 45 percent "assuming a flat change in working capital". Prysmian's 2028 cash target assumes down-payments merely offset execution; Siemens Energy carries a €8.5bn cumulative working-capital inflow that Bernstein calls a debt-like liability.
  • US building wire and Republic Wire (OWN-4). Republic Wire contributed €55m of sales and €5m of operating margin in June 2026, its first month (pro forma H1 2026: €330m and €31m, a 9.4 percent operating margin); the Q3 scope effect was 24.7 percent against 28 percent expected; management says synergies are ahead of schedule; Jefferies reports extra shifts on strong demand; the 30 percent capacity expansion is online by December 2026. Prysmian closed Atkore on 19 October 2026 ($3.8bn, $150m of synergies); Southwire is spending $2bn; the two hold 60 to 65 percent of US building wire and price in step. The scheduled refined-copper tariffs (15 percent from January 2027, 30 percent from 2028) are doubted, with over 600 kt of COMEX stocks; Morgan Stanley's earlier work had US building-wire margins normalising from 15 to 11 percent by 2028; Deutsche Bank says post-pandemic margins have already normalised to about 15 percent.
  • Input costs after Hormuz (OWN-5). European gas above €90 per megawatt-hour; Strait of Hormuz traffic not expected to normalise before mid-2027; war-risk insurance up 500 percent; Nexans buys energy at least 18 months ahead, so the hedge rolls off through 2027 (2025 consumption: 306,479 MWh of gas and 174,449 MWh of electricity); Borealis is the single source of cross-linked polyethylene compound, where Prysmian has qualified Dow and has its own P-Laser; polyethylene is globally oversupplied (Jefferies, Bernstein), so compound prices should deflate once shipping normalises; the 2022 precedent was a €60m annual price-cost headwind at EBITDA for European cable makers (Morgan Stanley); NKT's Distribution unit and Prysmian's Power Grid margin (11.2 percent against 11.7 percent expected) already showed the effect in Q2 2026; Connect uses daily price indexation (Bernstein, June 2026) and distributors (Rexel) are passing costs through.
  • Capital allocation and the register (OWN-6). Management calls M&A "a core capital-allocation pillar" with "a rich pipeline" in Grid and Connect, targets leverage below 1.0x by 2028 and, per Bernstein (April 2026), regards up to 2.5x as digestible for a transformational deal; buybacks are anti-dilutive (24,000 shares bought in the week to 9 October 2026); no capital markets day is scheduled for 2027 and the 2024 roadmap is reaffirmed. The register: Baillie Gifford 6.14 percent, BlackRock 5.44 percent, Bpifrance 5.20 percent, Quiñenco 4.13 percent, employees 3.9 percent; 34.84 percent voted against a Quiñenco director's renewal in May 2025; no activist.
  • The template reports (T-D1, T-D2, T-I1, T-I2) add peer precedents (NKT's Transmission margin 15.2 to 20.2 percent in a year; Prysmian's Projects margin stuck at 13 percent in 2020 to 2021 before rising; TKH's 2.5-year grid-connection wait; NKT's Applications margin -290 basis points on Nordic construction), the historical dispersion of cable makers' outcomes (Nexans's 2013 warnings and 42 percent rights issue, 2018's 31 percent fall in operating income on a 21 percent organic fall in high voltage, Prysmian's €70m Western Link provisions, NKT's Solutions EBITDA from €63m to €14m in 2019), Deutsche Bank's expectation of a FY2027 guide of about €880m to €940m, and a narrative map that I use in I-03.

C. Where I differ from the first and second sets

  1. The FY2026 free cash flow guidance comparison. The second set judged its €239m "in the lower part" of the €235m to €325m range. That compares a full-basis figure (after lease payments of €42m) with the company's definition (operating cash flow less capital spending, before lease principal, which sits in financing). On the company's basis the second set's forecast is about €280m, the midpoint. The guidance is more likely to be met than the second set implied.
  2. The 2028 cash-conversion target. The second set read the above-45 percent target as normalised (excluding reorganisation cash and strategic capital spending) and put its own FY2028 at about 44 percent on that basis. The new evidence is that the company moved to a full-basis definition in February 2025 and states the target "assuming a flat change in working capital". On the company's full basis the second set's FY2028 is about €409m (€362m plus leases), 40 percent of €1,013m, against a target that on the company's €1,150m implies €517m. The gap to the target is therefore real, not marginal, and it is the mid-term target most likely to be missed (I-01).
  3. The Republic Wire earn-out. The second set modelled €40m of cash in FY2028 and flagged the €86m figure as a discrepancy. The H1 2026 accounts carry €86m of earn-out liabilities inside a €752m purchase price; the €43m headline is either a different measure or one tranche. I model the liability the company has recognised and change the FY2028 acquisitions line to -€86m. It affects net debt (about 0.05x) and nothing else.
  4. The cost of an idle mass-impregnated line. T-D2 computes €7.5m of EBITDA per half-year of under-loading (15 percent of €50m of lost sales). That treats the line's cost as variable; it is a dedicated line with its own staff and depreciation, so the lost contribution is nearer the second set's €20m to €25m per half-year gross and €10m to €15m net of repair work, and I use €15m to €20m net per half-year including the weaker bargaining position Bernstein describes. The point matters for the cone in D-04.
  5. Transmission's weight. The second set's P-05 says Transmission is "about 20 to 25 percent" of EBITDA; its own tables put it at 28 percent in FY2025 and 29 percent in FY2028. The higher figure is right and the sensitivity to Halden is correspondingly larger.
  6. A 2027 capital markets update. The first set (C-22, C-73) expected the sell-side to get one; the new evidence is that none is scheduled and management says it will focus on executing the 2024 roadmap. The "novel upside step" of 2030 targets set above consensus is therefore less likely inside 18 months (I put it at 25 percent) and the February 2028 FY2028 guide becomes the event at which the 2028 ambition is reframed (I-01).
  7. Depreciation. The second set's €285m for FY2026 (excluding acquired intangibles) is probably €10m to €15m light: H1 2026 total depreciation was €153.4m with one month of Republic Wire and of Electra, and Deutsche Bank models €318m in total for FY2026; stripping about €19m of acquired-intangible amortisation gives about €299m. The effect is about €0.20 of EPS, inside the second set's own tolerance, so I do not change the input, but the lean on EPS is slightly further downward than the second set states.
  8. The first set's EPS path (€6.95, €8.45, €10.10). I side with the second set (€6.38, €8.04, €9.56) for the reasons above; the first set's figures used FactSet's consensus depreciation line, which is inconsistent with FactSet's own EBITDA less EBIT.
  9. The probability that the replacement order is lost. The first set put 25 to 30 percent on a material delay; given that no preferred-bidder position at the three majors has fallen away in five years and that conversion averages 9.6 months (July 2026 plus 9.6 months is late April 2027), I put the probability of outright loss below 10 percent and of signature after the end of the first quarter of 2027 at about 35 percent. The risk is timing and under-loading in H1 2027, not loss.

Third set: Assessing the predictability of the financial forecasts for the next 18 months

Forecast inputs changed by the third and fourth sets: Nexans SA (NEX-FR), as at 27-Oct-26

Money in millions of EUR, except per-share figures; costs and outflows negative. Only the lines changed are shown; every other line of the second set's forecast inputs stands.

Part 1. Profit and loss inputs, one column for each of the five fiscal years

Input FY2026 (year to 31-Dec-26) FY2027 (year to 31-Dec-27) FY2028 (year to 31-Dec-28) FY2029 (year to 31-Dec-29) FY2030 (year to 31-Dec-30)
Revenue growth (%) 39.1 9.2 5.3 3.7 3.5

The FY2026 figure is re-expressed against the model's own FY2025 base of €7,091m (the sum of FactSet's H1 2025 €3,977m and its artefact H2 2025 €3,114m, which understates continuing revenue by €719m), so that the model reproduces the intended FY2026 revenue at current metal prices of €9,866m (26.3 percent on the true €7,810m) and, chaining from it at the unchanged later rates, €10,776m, €11,345m, €11,769m and €12,182m; if the Financial Analyst instead corrects the H2 2025 history cell to €3,833m, revert this figure to 26.3 and do not apply both corrections.

Revenue growth is against the same period a year earlier.

Part 2. Cash flow and balance sheet inputs, one column for each of the five fiscal years

Input FY2026 (year to 31-Dec-26) FY2027 (year to 31-Dec-27) FY2028 (year to 31-Dec-28) FY2029 (year to 31-Dec-29) FY2030 (year to 31-Dec-30)
Acquisitions less disposals -485 0 -86 0 0

The FY2028 figure is raised from -40 to -86 because the H1 2026 accounts carry €86m of Republic Wire earn-out liabilities inside a €752m purchase price (€662m of net cash plus €86m of earn-out), so the cash the company has itself recognised as payable exceeds the €43m headline cap; the change affects net debt only (about 0.05 times EBITDA) and nothing in earnings or free cash flow.

Part 3. The next 18 months, one column for each of the company's reporting periods

Input Half-year to 31-Dec-26 Half-year to 30-Jun-27 Half-year to 31-Dec-27
Revenue growth (%) 64.7 12.6 6.1

The H2 2026 figure is re-expressed against FactSet's artefact H2 2025 base of €3,114m so that the model reproduces the intended H2 2026 revenue of €5,130m (33.8 percent on the true continuing H2 2025 of €3,833m) and the half-year and annual inputs agree without re-phasing; the H1 2027 and H2 2027 rates are unchanged because their bases (H1 2026 €4,736m and the corrected H2 2026) are right, and if the Financial Analyst corrects the H2 2025 history cell to €3,833m instead, revert this figure to 33.8.

Revenue growth is against the same period a year earlier.

Note for the Financial Analyst on the EBITDA phasing (no input change). The model's H1 2026 history EBITDA is FactSet's €453m, which includes the €75.2m Core exposure gain; the company's adjusted figure is €387.7m. With the revenue correction the annual FY2026 EBITDA will be the intended €805m (8.16 percent of €9,866m), but the model will again push the H2 2026 margin below the 8.13 percent input to land the year, distorting the H2 2026 and H1 2027 period figures. The remedy is to overwrite the H1 2026 history EBITDA with €387.7m, after which the Part 3 margin inputs (8.13, 8.23 and 8.63 percent) reproduce the intended period EBITDA of €417m, €439m and €470m with no re-phasing.

Appendix The share-price co-movement table the Investment Analyst was given

Co-movement of NEX-FR with its dashboard peers (computed 2026-10-26 from the master dashboard's daily prices)

What this is: daily close-to-close returns over rolling windows of 126 trading days (about six months). Correlation: how closely the daily moves line up (1 = always together, 0 = unrelated). Beta: how far the stock moves for a 1% move in the basket. R-squared: the share of the stock's daily price variance the peer basket explains; the remainder is the stock's own (idiosyncratic) movement. Basket: the peers below, equal-weighted. Prices only; the multiple's own history is in the financials block.

Peers (from the dashboard data file): ABB (ABBN-CH), Alfen (ALFEN-NL), Arteche (ART-ES), Cenergy (CENER-GR), Cembre (CMB-IT), Siemens Energy (ENR-DE), Fastned (FAST-NL), Kempower (KEMPOWR-FI), Landis+Gyr (LAND-CH), Legrand (LR-FR), NKT (NKT-DK), Pfisterer (PFSE-DE), Prysmian (PRY-IT), R&S Group Holding (RSGN-CH), Siemens (SIE-DE), Schneider Electric (SU-FR)
